Azure cloud · interview · version 1 · 2026-09-08

Azure cloud

interview · rolling release, no version number · snapshot 2026-09-08

Assumption stated up front: “Azure cloud” here is the Azure platform as a working engineer meets it — identity, compute, networking, data, messaging, IaC, observability, cost and governance — not the Microsoft 365 side of the tenant and not a certification syllabus (AZ-104/AZ-305 order is not interview order, and this bank ignores it). Azure has no version number: it ships continuously, so everything below is labelled with the date a thing became GA, default or retired, and the bank is current to 2026-09-08. What Azure interviews actually test at lead level is three things — whether you pick the right service for a workload and can say what you gave up, whether you know the failure and blast-radius story (identity, zones, quotas, the control plane) rather than the happy path, and whether you can put a number on cost and reliability instead of adjectives. Work the map in order: the first eight sub-areas are roughly three quarters of what you will be asked, and identity alone appears in almost every loop. Every answer is written to be spoken in 60–90 seconds and then deepened by the follow-ups; each “wrong answer” is the plausible mistake candidates really make, not a strawman.

20 sub-areas 122 questions screener → principal 3 design exercises current to 2026-09

The map is ordered by how often the sub-area is actually probed in a senior/lead Azure loop, not by textbook or exam order. Asked is my calibration for a platform/backend role at a company that runs a real Azure estate; Critical says whether a weak answer is disqualifying or merely a missed bonus.

What changed most recently, and is worth naming unprompted. Mandatory Entra MFA Phase 2 — every Azure Resource Manager write from CLI, PowerShell, REST, SDK or an IaC tool — began rolling out on 1 October 2025; tenants could postpone only until 1 July 2026, so as of today it is universal. That is the single change most likely to have broken someone's pipeline this year, and the correct answer to “how did you handle it” is workload identity federation, not a service-principal secret and not an MFA exemption. Close seconds: Basic Load Balancer and Basic public IPs retired 30 September 2025 (as did default outbound access for new VNets), Application Gateway v1 retired 28 April 2026, Bicep Azure Verified Modules for the platform landing zone went GA in January 2026 with Deployment Stacks as the default lifecycle mechanism (classic ALZ-Bicep archived 16 February 2026), and Karpenter-based AKS Node Auto Provisioning reached GA in early 2026.

01Identity, Entra ID & RBAC

Identity is the Azure control plane's front door and the sub-area that appears in essentially every loop, because every real incident and every real design decision routes through it. Screener depth is “what is a managed identity”; senior depth is designing role assignment scope and eliminating secrets from pipelines; principal depth is tenant topology, break-glass, Privileged Identity Management, and migrating a large estate off service-principal secrets without an outage. Interviewers probe here hardest because the wrong answer is expensive and irreversible in a way a wrong SKU choice is not.

Docs: Managed identities · Azure RBAC · Workload identity federation

What is a managed identity, and how does the token actually get to my code? screener

Answer. A managed identity is a service principal in Microsoft Entra ID whose credential is created, stored and rotated by the platform, so your code never sees a secret. On an Azure compute host, the platform exposes a local token endpoint — the Instance Metadata Service at 169.254.169.254 for VMs and VMSS, or an injected per-app endpoint (IDENTITY_ENDPOINT / IDENTITY_HEADER) on App Service, Functions and Container Apps. Your SDK calls that endpoint asking for a token for a resource audience such as https://vault.azure.net, the platform proves the host's identity to Entra ID and returns a normal OAuth 2.0 bearer JWT, typically valid for about an hour and cached in-process. Nothing leaves the host that a human could copy; the “credential” is the host itself. There are two flavours: system-assigned, whose lifecycle is tied to the resource and dies with it, and user-assigned, a standalone resource you can attach to many resources and pre-grant roles to before the workload exists.

Example

// DefaultAzureCredential walks a chain: env vars -> workload identity -> managed identity -> Azure CLI -> ...
// In production, pin it, so a developer credential can never silently be used in prod.
var cred = new ManagedIdentityCredential(
    ManagedIdentityId.FromUserAssignedClientId("8f1c...b204"));
var secrets = new SecretClient(new Uri("https://kv-prod.vault.azure.net/"), cred);
KeyVaultSecret s = await secrets.GetSecretAsync("Storage--ConnectionString");

The raw HTTP underneath, on App Service, is just GET $IDENTITY_ENDPOINT?resource=https://vault.azure.net&api-version=2019-08-01 with an X-IDENTITY-HEADER. Knowing that is how you debug a 403 without the SDK in the way.

Follow-ups

  • System-assigned or user-assigned by default? — User-assigned for anything with a deployment lifecycle: you can grant roles once, then recreate the app/VMSS/cluster freely. System-assigned when you want the identity to be garbage-collected with the resource and never outlive it.
  • Why does a fresh role assignment sometimes still 403? — RBAC is eventually consistent (usually seconds, occasionally minutes) and the cached token already in memory does not carry the new role. Force a new token or wait; do not add a broader role in a panic.
  • What can a managed identity not do? — Historically it could not cross tenants; that is now possible for user-assigned identities via federated identity credentials, but it is still not a general-purpose user identity: no interactive sign-in, no Conditional Access grant controls like MFA, and Graph permissions must be app-role (application) permissions.
  • How do you use one locally? — You don't. Locally you sign in as yourself (az login) and the credential chain picks that up, which is exactly why the roles you grant the identity must also be granted to the developer group — otherwise “works on my machine” hides a missing production role.

Wrong answer: “A managed identity is basically a service principal with the secret stored in Key Vault for you.” No — there is no secret anywhere for you to fetch, rotate or leak, and that is the entire point. Candidates who say this usually then propose putting the identity's “credentials” in a pipeline variable, which tells the interviewer they have never actually removed a secret from a system.

Explain the Azure RBAC model: scopes, role definitions, assignments, and inheritance. screener

Answer. An assignment is a triple: a security principal (user, group, service principal, managed identity), a role definition (a set of Actions/NotActions on the management plane and DataActions/NotDataActions on the data plane), and a scope — management group, subscription, resource group, or individual resource. Assignments inherit downward, and the effective permission is the union of everything that applies: there is no “deny wins” between two role assignments, because assignments are purely additive. The only true denies are deny assignments, which you cannot author directly — they are created by Azure Blueprints (deprecated), by managed applications, and by Deployment Stacks' denySettings. Azure Policy with a deny effect is the other way to stop an action, but it works at the ARM request layer, not in RBAC.

Example

{
  "roleName": "Blob reader, no key access",
  "assignableScopes": ["/subscriptions/<sub>"],
  "permissions": [{
    "actions":        [ "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/blobServices/containers/read" ],
    "notActions":     [ ],
    "dataActions":    [ "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/blobServices/containers/blobs/read" ],
    "notDataActions": [ ]
  }]
}

Note what is absent: .../listKeys/action. Contributor has it, which is why Contributor on a storage account is effectively full data access even though it grants no DataActions.

Follow-ups

  • Why is Owner different from Contributor? — Owner adds Microsoft.Authorization/*/write: the ability to grant roles. That is the privilege-escalation bit, and it is why “just make them Owner” is never a fix. The middle ground is Role Based Access Control Administrator, which grants role management but not resource access.
  • How do you scope least privilege in practice? — Assign to Entra groups at resource-group scope, never to individuals at subscription scope; a subscription is the blast radius you were trying to avoid. Assignment limits are per subscription (4,000 by default), which is itself an argument for groups.
  • What are ABAC conditions? — Conditions attached to an assignment that narrow it by attribute — e.g. a blob role that only applies where @Resource[...]/tags:Project<$key_case_sensitive$>Contoso matches. GA for Azure Storage data actions; it collapses hundreds of container-scoped assignments into one.
  • Data plane vs management plane? — Reading a blob's bytes is a DataAction; reading the storage account's properties is an Action. Some services (Key Vault, Storage, Service Bus, Cosmos DB) have full data-plane RBAC; others still gate the data plane with keys or connection strings, and knowing which is which is the real question.

Wrong answer: “Add a Reader assignment at the resource group and a Deny at the resource to carve out an exception.” Azure RBAC has no deny in role assignments — the union always wins. Candidates carry this over from AWS IAM, where explicit deny does win, and it is the single most common RBAC misconception in interviews.

Your GitHub Actions pipeline authenticates to Azure with a service-principal client secret. Replace it. Why, and with what? mid

Answer. Replace it with workload identity federation (OIDC): you register a federated identity credential on the app registration or user-assigned managed identity that trusts GitHub's OIDC issuer for a specific subject — repository, and branch, tag or environment. At run time the Actions runner asks GitHub for a short-lived OIDC token, exchanges it at Entra ID's token endpoint (RFC 7523 client assertion flow) for an Azure access token, and never holds a long-lived credential. The reasons are not only hygiene: a client secret has a maximum lifetime, so it will expire at 02:00 on a release night; it can be exfiltrated from a fork or a log; and since MFA Phase 2 the human-account fallback people used to reach for is gone. Federation also gives you a policy hook the secret never had — you can scope trust to environment:production, so a pull request from a fork cannot mint a production token.

Example

# one federated credential per trusted subject; subject must match GitHub's OIDC claim exactly
az identity federated-credential create \
  --name gh-prod --identity-name id-deploy --resource-group rg-identity \
  --issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com \
  --subject "repo:contoso/orders:environment:production" \
  --audiences api://AzureADTokenExchange
permissions: { id-token: write, contents: read }   # id-token: write is the line people forget
steps:
  - uses: azure/login@v2
    with:
      client-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_CLIENT_ID }}
      tenant-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_TENANT_ID }}
      subscription-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_SUBSCRIPTION_ID }}
      # no client-secret

Follow-ups

  • What breaks if the subject is wrong? — AADSTS70021: No matching federated identity record found. The subject string is exact and case-sensitive; ref:refs/heads/main and environment:production are different subjects and each needs its own credential (up to 20 per identity).
  • Does this satisfy MFA Phase 2? — Yes, and that is the point: the enforcement applies to user sign-ins doing ARM writes. A workload identity is not a user, so a federated pipeline is unaffected — whereas a pipeline running under someone's personal account with az login -u/-p stopped working.
  • Same story for Azure DevOps? — Yes: Azure DevOps service connections support workload identity federation, and there is a conversion flow for existing secret-based connections. Same trust model, different issuer.
  • How do you stop a rogue workflow using it? — Scope the federated subject to a protected GitHub environment with required reviewers, and scope the Azure role assignment to the target resource group only. Two independent narrowings, neither of which a workflow file can edit alone.

Wrong answer: “We store the client secret in GitHub Actions secrets, which are encrypted, and rotate it every 90 days with a script.” It is defensible and it is what most estates still do — but as an answer to this question it says you think the problem is storage rather than the existence of a bearer credential. Interviewers are listening for the words “federated” and “short-lived”.

What is Entra ID Conditional Access, and where does it apply to Azure workloads? mid

Answer. Conditional Access is the policy engine that sits after primary authentication and before token issuance: given signals (user/group, application, device compliance, location/IP, sign-in risk, client app type), it grants, blocks, or grants with requirements (MFA, compliant device, approved app, sign-in frequency, token protection). For Azure specifically the important target is the Microsoft Azure Management cloud app, which covers the portal, CLI, PowerShell and ARM REST. Since mandatory MFA Phase 1 (portal, 100% of tenants by March 2025) and Phase 2 (ARM writes from any client, rolling from 1 October 2025 with postponement only to 1 July 2026), the MFA baseline is enforced by Microsoft regardless of your policies — Conditional Access is now how you add more: named locations for admin work, compliant-device requirements, and session controls. Workload identities can also be targeted, but that needs Workload Identities Premium and the conditions are limited to location and risk, because there is no device or MFA to require.

Example

Policy: "Admin plane from managed devices only"
  Users:       group "Azure Platform Admins" (exclude: 2 break-glass accounts)
  Cloud apps:  Microsoft Azure Management
  Conditions:  any location
  Grant:       Require MFA AND Require device to be marked compliant
  Session:     Sign-in frequency 4 hours, persistent browser: never

The exclusion of break-glass accounts is not optional; it is the difference between a policy and a lockout.

Follow-ups

  • How do you deploy a CA policy safely? — Report-only mode first, read the sign-in logs' “would have” results for a week, then a pilot group, then broad. The What-If tool answers “would this policy have blocked Alice from the portal on Tuesday?”
  • What are break-glass accounts and how are they protected? — Two cloud-only Global Administrators, excluded from all CA policies, with long random passwords in physical escrow or split custody, phishing-resistant credentials, alerting on any sign-in, and a quarterly test that they still work. Excluded from CA because a misconfigured CA policy is the most common cause of a tenant lockout.
  • Does CA protect a managed identity calling Key Vault? — No. That call carries no user context. Protect it with RBAC scope, Key Vault firewall/private endpoint, and network policy — not CA.
  • Where does PIM fit? — CA decides how you authenticate; Privileged Identity Management decides whether you hold the role at all right now. Eligible-not-active Owner with approval and a time box, plus CA requiring MFA on activation, is the standard senior answer for admin access.

Wrong answer: “We enforce MFA with the per-user MFA settings in the legacy portal.” Legacy per-user MFA is the pattern you will still find in old tenants, and it conflicts badly with Conditional Access and with the mandatory-MFA rollout. Naming it as the legacy thing you migrate off is a strong signal; naming it as current is a weak one.

App registration, enterprise application, service principal, managed identity — what is the actual object model? mid

Answer. The application object (what the portal calls an App registration) is the global definition of an application, living in its home tenant: redirect URIs, exposed scopes and app roles, requested permissions, credentials. A service principal is the local instance of that application in a given tenant — the thing that actually holds role assignments, consent grants and sign-in logs; the portal calls it an Enterprise application. A multi-tenant app has one application object and one service principal per consenting tenant. A managed identity is a service principal with no application object you can edit and with platform-managed credentials; it shows up under Enterprise applications with type “Managed Identity”. The confusion is worth resolving out loud because it explains everyday behaviour: you grant Azure RBAC to the service principal (by object ID), you configure the app on the application object (by application/client ID), and a role assignment on the wrong ID is the classic silent failure.

Example

az ad app create --display-name orders-api          # -> appId (client id), id (app object id)
az ad sp create --id <appId>                        # -> id (SP object id) - THIS is the principal id
az role assignment create --assignee-object-id <SP object id> \
  --assignee-principal-type ServicePrincipal \
  --role "Storage Blob Data Contributor" --scope /subscriptions/.../rg-orders

--assignee-principal-type exists precisely because Entra replication lag makes assignment-by-name flaky right after creation.

Follow-ups

  • Delegated vs application permissions? — Delegated acts on behalf of a signed-in user and is capped by that user's own rights; application permissions act as the app itself with no user, need admin consent, and are what a daemon or a managed identity uses.
  • How do you grant a managed identity Microsoft Graph access? — Assign a Graph app role to the identity's service principal (there is no consent UI for managed identities). Prefer the narrowest role — e.g. User.Read.All, never Directory.ReadWrite.All — and remember Graph app roles are tenant-wide, so scope-by-scope narrowing has to come from Graph's own resource-specific consent where available.
  • What happened to Azure AD Graph and ADAL? — Both retired. Azure AD Graph (graph.windows.net) is shut down and ADAL/Microsoft.Azure.Services.AppAuthentication are end-of-life; the current stack is Microsoft Graph plus MSAL, or in Azure SDKs, Azure.Identity. Finding these in a codebase is a migration ticket, not a style preference.
  • Why does my app show up in two tenants? — Multi-tenant registration: one application object at home, a service principal materialised in each tenant at first consent. Revoking access means deleting the foreign service principal, not the app.

Wrong answer: “App registration and enterprise application are two names for the same thing.” They are two objects, and treating them as one produces the very common bug where someone deletes the enterprise application to “clean up” and silently destroys every role assignment and consent grant while the registration survives.

How do you design tenant, management group and subscription topology for a mid-size company? senior

Answer. Default to one Entra tenant: a tenant boundary is an identity boundary, and every extra tenant multiplies B2B invitations, Conditional Access policy sets and licensing. Split tenants only for a genuine sovereignty, regulatory or M&A-isolation reason, and say that explicitly. Inside the tenant, use management groups as the policy and RBAC inheritance tree — typically the Azure landing zone shape: root → Platform (identity, management, connectivity) and Landing zones (corp, online), plus Sandbox and Decommissioned. Use subscriptions as the unit of scale, blast radius, quota and billing: one per workload per environment is the modern default, because subscription limits (cores per region, private endpoints, ARM write throttling) are real ceilings and because a compromised subscription is a bounded loss. Resource groups are the lifecycle unit — everything that is deployed and deleted together. The principal-level point is that this tree is where policy, cost allocation and access all land, so getting it wrong is expensive to unpick later; moving a subscription between management groups is cheap, moving resources between subscriptions is not.

Example

Tenant contoso.com
└─ MG: contoso (root-child)
   ├─ MG: platform          policy: no public IPs, mandatory diagnostics
   │  ├─ sub: connectivity  (hub VNet, firewall, DNS, ExpressRoute)
   │  ├─ sub: identity      (domain controllers, PIM tooling)
   │  └─ sub: management    (Log Analytics, automation, backup vault)
   ├─ MG: landingzones
   │  ├─ MG: corp           policy: deny public ingress, require private endpoints
   │  │  ├─ sub: orders-prod      sub: orders-nonprod
   │  └─ MG: online         policy: allow public ingress behind WAF
   ├─ MG: sandbox           policy: budget alerts, auto-delete after 30 days, cheap SKUs only
   └─ MG: decommissioned    policy: deny all writes

Follow-ups

  • Why not one big subscription with resource groups per team? — Quotas and throttling are per subscription, so one noisy team's deployment loop can 429 everyone else's; cost allocation degrades to tag hygiene; and RBAC blast radius is the whole estate. Tags are a reporting tool, not an isolation boundary.
  • How do teams get subscriptions without a ticket queue? — Subscription vending: an automated pipeline that creates the subscription, places it under the right management group, applies budget and policy, connects networking, and hands back a resource group with the team's group already assigned. This is the answer that separates lead from senior.
  • What is the cost of too many subscriptions? — Landing-zone plumbing per subscription (peering, DNS links, diagnostic settings, private endpoint quotas), and a real risk of policy drift. Automate it or don't do it.
  • Do management groups affect performance or cost? — No, they are free and purely a scope tree — but policy evaluation and RBAC inheritance flow through them, and the depth limit is six levels below root.

Wrong answer: “We use one subscription per team and separate environments with resource group naming conventions and tags.” It reads as tidy and it is what many estates do, but it fails the interviewer's real question — where is the blast radius, the quota boundary and the budget? Naming conventions enforce nothing.

A deployment fails with 403 AuthorizationFailed. Walk me through debugging it. mid

Answer. Work the request, not the theory. First establish which principal — the error body names the object ID; confirm it is the identity you think (a pipeline running as the wrong service connection is the most common cause). Second, which action and scope — the message gives both, e.g. Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks/subnets/join/action on a subnet in a different resource group, which is the classic missing permission when deploying private endpoints or App Service VNet integration. Third, run az role assignment list --assignee <id> --all --include-inherited and check the effective set, remembering that assignments are additive and inherited. Fourth, rule out the two non-RBAC causes that produce the same status: an Azure Policy deny (the response contains a policy definition ID and RequestDisallowedByPolicy, not AuthorizationFailed — read the code carefully) and a resource lock or Deployment Stack deny assignment. Fifth, if the assignment is brand new, allow for replication and force a fresh token.

Example

# who am I, really?
az account show --query "user"
# what does this principal actually have, everywhere?
az role assignment list --assignee <objectId> --all --include-inherited -o table
# is something denying it rather than not-allowing it?
az policy state list --resource <resourceId> --filter "complianceState eq 'NonCompliant'" -o table
az lock list --resource-group rg-orders -o table

Follow-ups

  • Which permission is missing when a private endpoint deployment fails? — Almost always Microsoft.Network/virtualNetworks/subnets/join/action on the target subnet, which lives in the connectivity subscription the workload team has no rights in. Custom role or a scoped Network Contributor on that subnet, not Contributor on the hub.
  • How do you prove after the fact who deleted a resource? — Activity log (90 days by default) filtered on the operation, or a Log Analytics workspace if you exported it — which is why exporting the activity log at the management-group scope is a landing-zone requirement, not a nice-to-have.
  • What if it works in the portal but not from the CLI? — Different token audience and often different API version; also MFA Phase 2, where an unauthenticated-for-MFA CLI session can fail an ARM write that the portal session performs happily. Re-run az login and check the token's amr claim.
  • 403 on the data plane of a storage account with Contributor? — Expected. Contributor grants no DataActions; you need Storage Blob Data Reader/Contributor. The trap is that Contributor can list keys, so the same person succeeds with a connection string and fails with Entra auth.

Wrong answer: “Give the deployment identity Contributor at subscription scope and move on; we'll tighten it later.” Everybody has done it; saying it in an interview without immediately naming the narrowing plan says you would leave it. The strong version is “unblock with a time-boxed PIM activation, then encode the exact missing action in a custom role in the same PR.”

How does a token from Entra ID actually get validated by my API, and what do you check? senior

Answer. The caller presents a JWT signed by Entra ID. Your API fetches the signing keys from the tenant's OpenID Connect metadata (https://login.microsoftonline.com/{tenant}/v2.0/.well-known/openid-configuration → jwks_uri), caches them, and validates: signature against the key named by kid, iss matches the expected tenant issuer, aud matches your API's application ID URI (not the client's), exp/nbf within skew, and tid if you are single-tenant. Then you do authorization: scp for delegated scopes, roles for application permissions or app roles, and — critically — you use oid+tid as the stable user key, never email or upn, which are mutable and not always present. The v1.0 versus v2.0 endpoint distinction still bites: a token minted for a v1 audience has issuer sts.windows.net and different claim shapes, and a mismatch between the registration's accessTokenAcceptedVersion and your validation config produces a 401 that looks like a key problem.

Example

builder.Services.AddAuthentication(JwtBearerDefaults.AuthenticationScheme)
    .AddMicrosoftIdentityWebApi(builder.Configuration.GetSection("AzureAd"));
// appsettings: Instance, TenantId, ClientId (= the API's own app id)

builder.Services.AddAuthorization(o => o.AddPolicy("Orders.Write", p =>
    p.RequireAssertion(ctx =>
        ctx.User.HasClaim("scp", "Orders.Write")          // delegated: a user is present
     || ctx.User.HasClaim("roles", "Orders.Write.All"))); // app-only: daemon or managed identity

Follow-ups

  • Why must you not validate an access token you did not request? — Tokens for Microsoft Graph are not yours to inspect; they are encrypted/opaque for some resources and their format is not contractual. Validate only tokens whose aud is your own API.
  • How do you handle key rollover? — Cache JWKS with a TTL and refetch on an unknown kid, with a rate limit so a bad token cannot turn into a JWKS stampede. Hard-coding a key is a scheduled outage.
  • Revocation? — Access tokens are not revocable within their lifetime (typically 60–90 minutes, randomised). For fast revocation you need Continuous Access Evaluation, which pushes revocation events to CAE-aware resources, or short-lived tokens plus a server-side check for the highest-risk operations.
  • On-behalf-of flow — when? — When your API must call a downstream API as the user: exchange the incoming token for a new one via the OBO grant. It is the correct answer to “how does my middle tier call Graph with the user's own rights”, and the wrong problem to solve with app permissions.

Wrong answer: “We decode the JWT and check the email claim against our users table.” Two failures in one: decoding without verifying the signature, and keying on a mutable claim. Interviewers ask the follow-up “what if the user changes their surname?” specifically to catch it — the stable identifier is oid within tid.

Design privileged access for an Azure estate of 60 subscriptions. Standing access is not allowed. principal

Answer. Three layers. Entitlement: nobody holds Owner or User Access Administrator standing; those are PIM-eligible at management-group scope with approval, justification, a maximum activation of 4–8 hours, and MFA on activation. Day-to-day work uses narrower standing roles at resource-group scope granted to Entra groups sourced from HR/joiner-mover-leaver automation, so the access list is a consequence of employment, not a ticket. Authentication: phishing-resistant credentials (FIDO2 or certificate-based) for anyone eligible for a privileged role, enforced by Conditional Access with an authentication strength, plus device compliance for the management plane; break-glass accounts excluded and monitored. Verification: access reviews quarterly on privileged groups, activity log exported to a workspace with alerts on role-assignment writes and on any use of a break-glass account, and Deployment Stacks or deny assignments to protect platform resources from even a legitimately elevated Owner. The bit that makes this workable rather than theatre is that the everyday path must not require elevation — if PIM activation is needed for routine deploys, engineers will get a standing exception within a month.

Example

Standing (no approval):   grp-orders-devs      -> Contributor on rg-orders-nonprod
                          grp-orders-devs      -> Reader on sub orders-prod
PIM eligible (approval):  grp-orders-oncall    -> Contributor on rg-orders-prod, max 8h, MFA + ticket
PIM eligible (2 approvers): grp-platform-admins -> Owner on MG platform, max 4h
Never assigned:           any individual user at subscription or MG scope
Break-glass:              2 cloud-only GAs, FIDO2 in escrow, alert on any sign-in, tested quarterly

Follow-ups

  • How do you handle emergencies at 03:00 when the approver is asleep? — A named on-call approval group with follow-the-sun rotation, plus a documented self-approval path that fires a high-severity alert and generates an automatic post-incident review. Elevation must never be impossible; it must always be noisy.
  • What about the pipelines, which are the real privileged actors? — Federated workload identities scoped per environment, deploying through a stack that owns platform resources, and reviewed as code. In most estates the CI identity is more privileged than any human, and candidates who don't mention it have not run a platform.
  • How do you prove this to an auditor? — PIM activation history plus access reviews plus activity log export, joined in KQL. Show them the query, not a screenshot.
  • What is the failure mode of this design? — Approval fatigue leading to blanket approvals, and a slow drift where “temporary” standing exceptions accumulate. Counter it with an automated report of standing privileged assignments that goes to the platform lead weekly, with a burn-down target.

Wrong answer: “Everyone uses PIM to activate Owner when they need to do anything.” Making elevation routine destroys its signal value and guarantees a workaround. The point of just-in-time access is that activation is rare and interesting; if it happens forty times a day, you have built a slower version of standing access.

Your organisation acquires a company with its own Entra tenant. How do you integrate the Azure estates? principal

Answer. Separate the three questions people conflate: identity, billing, and resources. Billing is the easy one — subscriptions can be transferred between billing accounts, and an Enterprise Agreement or MCA can absorb the acquired subscriptions without touching anything technical. Identity is the hard one: a subscription's trusted directory can be changed, but doing so destroys every RBAC assignment, managed identity, and Key Vault access policy in it, because those principals exist only in the old tenant. So the realistic sequence is: connect the tenants with B2B collaboration and cross-tenant access settings first so people can work; inventory what the move will break (an export of role assignments, managed identities, Key Vault references, and any app registration referenced by the workloads); rebuild those as code targeting the destination tenant; then move subscription by subscription during a maintenance window, re-applying assignments from the IaC immediately after. Resources: prefer moving the subscription over moving resources, because cross-subscription resource moves have long unsupported-type lists and take the resource offline in some services. If the acquisition is temporary or the regulatory posture differs, the right answer may be to leave the tenant standing and integrate at the network and data layers only.

Example

# BEFORE the tenant change: capture what you are about to lose
az role assignment list --all --include-inherited -o json > assignments.json
az identity list -o json > user-assigned-identities.json
az keyvault list --query "[].{n:name, rbac:properties.enableRbacAuthorization}" -o table

# the move itself (billing owner + destination tenant global admin required)
az account tenant transfer ...   # portal flow in practice; every SP/MI in the sub becomes orphaned

System-assigned identities must be disabled and re-enabled after the move to be recreated in the new tenant, and every downstream role assignment for them re-granted — this is the step that turns a “simple” migration into a weekend.

Follow-ups

  • What if you must keep two tenants long term? — Cross-tenant access settings for B2B trust (including trusting the other tenant's MFA and device claims so users aren't double-prompted), Entra Private Access or VNet peering/ExpressRoute for the network, and cross-tenant managed identity federation for workload-to-workload calls.
  • How do you share a Log Analytics workspace or a hub network across tenants? — Azure Lighthouse: delegated resource management lets principals in tenant A hold RBAC on subscriptions in tenant B without guest accounts. It is the standard answer for MSPs and for post-merger platform teams.
  • What is the sequencing risk? — Moving a subscription before rebuilding the identities means an outage for anything using a managed identity — which today is everything. Rehearse on the smallest non-prod subscription first and measure the actual break.
  • What do you do about duplicate policy and naming? — Land the acquired subscriptions in a dedicated management group with the acquired-state policy set, then move them up the tree as they are remediated. Never apply the full corp policy set on day one; you will spend the quarter on deny failures.

Wrong answer: “We just change the subscription's directory, the resources are unaffected.” The resources are indeed unaffected; everything that authorises access to them is not. This question exists to find out whether you have separated the resource plane from the identity plane in your head.

02Choosing compute

“We need to run this service on Azure — what do you use, and why?” is the most reliably asked design question in the whole bank, because the answer reveals whether you reason from constraints or from familiarity. Screener depth is naming the options; senior depth is a decision with named trade-offs and a cost/ops argument; principal depth is the migration path when the choice turns out wrong, and the organisational cost of each option. Interviewers are rarely looking for a specific service — they are listening for the constraint you elicit first.

Docs: Compute decision tree · Container options compared · Well-Architected Framework

App Service, Container Apps, AKS, Functions or VMs — how do you choose? screener

Answer. Choose by the constraint that is hardest to move, then by operational cost. App Service for a normal HTTP application when you want managed TLS, slots, autoscale and zero cluster ops — still the fastest path for most web APIs, and the only one with a first-class Windows story for legacy .NET Framework. Azure Functions when work is event-driven and bursty and you want scale-to-zero and per-execution billing; on the Flex Consumption plan you also get VNet integration at no extra cost and always-ready instances to kill cold start. Container Apps when you want containers, KEDA-based event scaling, revisions and Dapr without operating Kubernetes — the sweet spot for microservices that do not need cluster-level extensibility. AKS when you genuinely need Kubernetes: custom operators, service mesh, GPU scheduling, portability across clouds, or an existing platform team and Helm estate. VMs/VMSS when you need OS control, licensed software, GPUs with specific drivers, or a lift-and-shift with no time to refactor. The honest framing at lead level is that the ranking is by how much Kubernetes your organisation can afford to operate, not by technical capability.

Example

NeedPickWhat you give up
HTTP API, small team, no containersApp Service (Premium v3/v4)Per-request billing; sidecars; fine-grained scheduling
Queue/timer/event work, spikyFunctions, Flex ConsumptionLong-running work (default 30 min cap); some runtime pinning
Containers, many small servicesContainer AppsCluster-level extensibility, CRDs, node access, DaemonSets
Mesh, operators, GPUs, portabilityAKSAn on-call platform team and upgrade cadence
OS control, legacy or licensedVMSS FlexPatching, image pipeline, scaling you build yourself

Follow-ups

  • What single question do you ask first? — “What is the traffic shape?” Steady traffic favours reserved/dedicated capacity; bursty or scale-to-zero favours consumption. Cost differences of 5–10× hide in that one answer.
  • When is AKS the wrong answer even though it can do everything? — When the team is under about 20 engineers with no platform specialisation. The recurring cost is not the cluster, it is version upgrades, node image patching, CVE churn and the on-call load — and interviewers want to hear you price that.
  • Container Apps or AKS for 15 microservices? — Container Apps first, unless you already need a CRD-based capability. The escape hatch is real: the container image is unchanged, so moving to AKS later is a deployment-pipeline change, not a rewrite. Say the escape hatch out loud — it is what makes the choice defensible.
  • Where does Azure Container Instances fit now? — Rarely as a primary host; it is a burst target (virtual nodes) and a good fit for isolated short jobs. Container Apps jobs have absorbed most of what people used ACI for.

Wrong answer: “AKS, because it is the most flexible and everything runs in containers anyway.” Flexibility is not free and the interviewer is measuring whether you price the operational tail. The same answer becomes strong when it starts “we already run a platform team and 40 services on AKS, so the marginal cost of one more is near zero”.

What actually happens when App Service autoscales, and how do you configure it well? mid

Answer. App Service scales the plan, not the app: adding an instance means a new worker running every app in that plan, each with its own copy of local state and its own warm-up. There are two mechanisms — the classic autoscale rules in Azure Monitor (metric thresholds, schedules, cool-down) and automatic scaling (a per-app setting where the platform scales on incoming request pressure, with a per-app maximum and a “always ready” instance count). Configure it by scaling out on a metric that leads demand rather than trails it — HTTP queue length or requests per instance, not CPU, for an IO-bound API — with an asymmetric rule set: scale out fast and by more than one instance, scale in slowly and by one, with a cool-down long enough that you do not oscillate. Then make the scale event survivable: enable health checks so a bad instance is removed, set WEBSITE_SWAP_WARMUP_PING_PATH or app initialization so a new worker is warmed before it takes traffic, and make sure nothing in the app depends on instance-local state.

Example

resource scale 'Microsoft.Insights/autoscalesettings@2022-10-01' = {
  name: 'asp-orders-autoscale'
  properties: {
    targetResourceUri: plan.id
    profiles: [{
      name: 'default'
      capacity: { minimum: '3', maximum: '20', default: '3' }   // min 3: zone redundancy needs >= 3
      rules: [
        { metricTrigger: { metricName: 'HttpQueueLength', timeGrain: 'PT1M', statistic: 'Average'
                         , timeWindow: 'PT5M', timeAggregation: 'Average', operator: 'GreaterThan', threshold: 25 }
        , scaleAction: { direction: 'Increase', type: 'ChangeCount', value: '3', cooldown: 'PT5M' } }
        { metricTrigger: { metricName: 'HttpQueueLength', timeGrain: 'PT1M', statistic: 'Average'
                         , timeWindow: 'PT15M', timeAggregation: 'Average', operator: 'LessThan', threshold: 5 }
        , scaleAction: { direction: 'Decrease', type: 'ChangeCount', value: '1', cooldown: 'PT15M' } }
      ]
    }]
  }
}

Follow-ups

  • Why is CPU a bad scale metric for a web API? — An IO-bound API saturates on threads, connections or downstream latency long before CPU moves; by the time CPU reaches 70% you are already timing out. CPU is right for compute-bound work and wrong for almost everything else.
  • What is the minimum instance count for zone redundancy? — Historically 3; App Service now supports zone redundancy from 2 instances in enabled regions, and it can be toggled on an existing plan. Either way it is not 1, and a plan with one instance has no availability story at all.
  • What breaks when instances come and go? — In-memory session and cache, in-process background timers (they now run on every instance), local file writes, and singleton scheduled jobs. The fixes are distributed cache, a leader election or a queue-triggered job, and blob/Files for shared state.
  • Premium v3 vs Premium v4? — Premium v4 went GA on 1 September 2025 on newer general-purpose and memory-optimised hardware with NVMe temp storage, roughly 25%+ faster than v3 in Microsoft's testing and cheaper per unit of throughput on pay-as-you-go. For new plans in supported regions it is the default choice; the migration is a plan SKU change, so the interesting question is region availability, not risk.

Wrong answer: “Set autoscale on CPU > 70% with min 1, max 10 — that's the standard.” It is the portal default, it lags real load, and min 1 means a single instance restart is an outage. Reciting the default without questioning the metric is exactly the signal the interviewer is testing for.

Explain Azure Functions hosting plans and when each is right. mid

Answer. Four that matter. Consumption: scale to zero, pay per GB-second and execution, cold starts of seconds, limited VNet support, 5-minute default timeout (10 max) — right for genuinely intermittent work. Flex Consumption, the current default recommendation: still scale-to-zero and per-execution billing, but with per-function concurrency-based scaling, scale to 1,000 instances, choice of 2,048 MB or 4,096 MB instances, VNet integration at no extra cost, and always-ready instances you pay a baseline for so latency-sensitive functions never cold start. Premium (Elastic Premium): pre-warmed instances, unlimited execution duration, VNet, predictable pricing — the pre-Flex answer to cold start and increasingly the legacy one for new work. Dedicated (App Service plan): functions co-located with web apps on capacity you already own, no per-execution billing, and the only way to run them on an ASE. The modern-versus-legacy line matters here: if a codebase is on Premium purely for cold start and VNet, Flex Consumption is the migration, and saying so is a currency signal.

Example

PlanScale to 0Cold startMax durationVNetTypical fit
Flex Consumptionyesnone with always-readyunbounded (configurable)includedDefault for new work
Consumptionyesseconds10 min maxlimitedRare, tiny, cost-critical
Premiumno (min 1)none (pre-warmed)unboundedyesExisting estates; ASE-adjacent
DedicatednononeunboundedyesReuse of an existing plan

Follow-ups

  • What actually causes a cold start? — Allocating a worker, mounting the app content, starting the language worker and JIT-ing or interpreting your code, then running any startup DI graph. The controllable parts are package size, the number of dependencies resolved at startup, and running from a package rather than many small files.
  • Why did per-function scaling matter enough to change plans? — On Consumption the whole function app scales as a unit, so one hot HTTP trigger drags along every timer and queue function in the app. Flex scales per function (or per scale group), which is why you no longer have to split apps just to isolate scaling.
  • In-process or isolated worker for .NET? — Isolated worker is the supported model going forward; the in-process model has reached end of support, so a legacy in-process function app is a migration item — different startup, different middleware, different bindings package.
  • How do you stop a function from overwhelming a database? — Bound concurrency at the source: maxConcurrentCalls/maxConcurrentSessions for Service Bus, batchSize/newBatchThreshold for queues, plus a max instance count on the plan. Autoscaling compute in front of a fixed-size database is how you turn a spike into an outage.

Wrong answer: “Use Premium to avoid cold starts — that's what it's for.” It was the right answer until Flex Consumption reached GA; repeating it now, without mentioning always-ready instances on Flex, dates you by about two years and misses that you are paying for idle instances you no longer need.

A batch job runs for four hours and needs 32 GB of RAM. Where do you run it? mid

Answer. Not in a plain HTTP-triggered Function, and the reason is the shape of the work rather than any single limit. Realistic options: a Container Apps job (event- or schedule-triggered, scales to zero between runs, per-job resource allocation, retries and parallelism built in) is the modern default; an AKS Job/CronJob if you already run the cluster and want the scheduler; Azure Batch if this is genuinely a pool-and-tasks HPC workload with dependencies and thousands of tasks; a VMSS with spot instances if the job is interruption-tolerant and cost dominates; and Durable Functions if the four hours are mostly waiting rather than computing, because then it is an orchestration and should be decomposed into activities with checkpoints. The senior instinct is to ask whether the four hours is compute or waiting, and whether the job is restartable — because a four-hour non-idempotent job with no checkpoints is an availability problem regardless of where you host it.

Example

# Container Apps job: scheduled, isolated resources, retries, no always-on cost
az containerapp job create -n nightly-recon -g rg-batch --environment cae-prod \
  --trigger-type Schedule --cron-expression "0 2 * * *" \
  --replica-timeout 18000 --replica-retry-limit 2 --parallelism 1 \
  --cpu 4 --memory 32Gi --image acr.azurecr.io/recon:2026.09.1 \
  --mi-user-assigned /subscriptions/.../userAssignedIdentities/id-recon

Follow-ups

  • Why not Functions? — You can (Flex and Premium have no hard duration cap), but you inherit a request-oriented runtime, per-execution billing that is unattractive for sustained CPU, and awkward memory ceilings. If the answer is Functions, it should be because the work is naturally event-driven, not because Functions is the serverless brand you know.
  • How do you make it restartable? — Checkpoint progress to durable storage (a blob or a table with the last processed key), make each unit idempotent, and design so a rerun from the last checkpoint is cheaper than a full rerun. Then a node eviction costs minutes, not hours.
  • Spot instances — when? — When eviction is survivable and the job can be re-queued: savings are typically large but the platform can reclaim capacity with 30 seconds' notice. Never for the last stage of a pipeline with a hard SLA.
  • What about the data path? — Four hours of compute usually means moving a lot of bytes; check whether the storage account, database or network egress is the actual bottleneck before you size the VM. Cross-region reads are the classic hidden cost.

Wrong answer: “Run it as a timer-triggered Function on the Consumption plan and raise functionTimeout.” Consumption caps at ten minutes and the setting will not save you; the job dies at the cap, usually silently, usually in production. This is a favourite question precisely because the wrong answer is a config change that looks like it works in dev.

How do you get a compute resource onto a private network — and what is the difference between the options? senior

Answer. Two directions, and candidates conflate them constantly. Outbound — my app calling private things — is VNet integration: App Service and Functions inject outbound traffic into a delegated subnet; Container Apps environments and AKS clusters live in a subnet by construction; VMs are simply in the VNet. Inbound — private things calling my app, or my app being unreachable from the internet — is a private endpoint plus disabling public access, which gives the service a private IP in your VNet and requires a matching private DNS zone so the public hostname resolves to it. A fully private App Service is therefore two independent configurations, not one. The heavier options exist for isolation rather than connectivity: an App Service Environment v3 or an AKS cluster with a private API server puts the whole platform inside your network, at a fixed monthly cost and with more operational surface. Say the DNS part out loud — the most common private-endpoint failure is not networking, it is a missing zone link or a hardcoded public IP resolution.

Example

// outbound: app -> private things
resource app 'Microsoft.Web/sites@2023-12-01' = {
  name: 'app-orders'
  properties: {
    virtualNetworkSubnetId: integrationSubnet.id     // delegated to Microsoft.Web/serverFarms
    siteConfig: { vnetRouteAllEnabled: true }        // send ALL egress through the VNet, not just RFC1918
    publicNetworkAccess: 'Disabled'                  // inbound: only via the private endpoint
  }
}
// inbound: private things -> app  (plus a privatelink.azurewebsites.net zone linked to the VNet)
resource pe 'Microsoft.Network/privateEndpoints@2023-11-01' = {
  name: 'pe-app-orders'
  properties: {
    subnet: { id: privateEndpointSubnet.id }
    privateLinkServiceConnections: [{ name: 'sites', properties: {
      privateLinkServiceId: app.id, groupIds: [ 'sites' ] } }]
  }
}

Follow-ups

  • Why does vnetRouteAllEnabled matter? — Without it only RFC 1918 destinations go through the VNet, so calls to a private endpoint whose DNS resolves privately still work, but internet-bound traffic bypasses your firewall and NSGs entirely. If your control is “all egress inspected”, this flag is the control.
  • What does a private endpoint cost you operationally? — A private IP and a NIC per endpoint (subject to subscription limits), a private DNS zone per service type, zone links to every VNet that must resolve it, and a DNS forwarding story for on-premises. At scale, centralise the zones in the connectivity subscription and link outward.
  • Service endpoints versus private endpoints? — Service endpoints are the legacy option: they keep traffic on the Microsoft backbone and let the service firewall allow a subnet, but the service keeps its public IP and there is no on-premises path. Private endpoints are the current recommendation; service endpoints survive where cost or endpoint counts dominate.
  • When is an ASE actually justified? — Regulatory single-tenancy, very large scale on dedicated hardware, or a hard requirement for inbound-private plus custom hardware isolation. Otherwise Premium v3/v4 with private endpoints achieves the same network posture for far less fixed cost.

Wrong answer: “We added VNet integration, so the app is private now.” VNet integration is outbound only — the app is still reachable from the internet at its *.azurewebsites.net hostname. This is the single most common Azure networking misconception at senior level, and interviewers ask it in exactly this form.

How do you size and cost compute for a service before it exists? senior

Answer. Start from load, not from SKUs. Estimate peak requests per second and the latency budget, derive concurrency (Little's law: concurrency ≈ RPS × latency), and turn that into instances using a measured or assumed per-instance concurrency limit. Then apply three multipliers: headroom for the difference between average and peak (2–3× for a consumer app), redundancy for zone or instance loss (n+1 at minimum, which is why zone redundancy implies at least two or three instances), and a growth factor for the planning horizon. Price the result at pay-as-you-go, then subtract the commitment you can honestly make — reservations or savings plans for the steady base, on-demand for the peak. Finally, name the costs that are not compute, because they usually dominate a real bill: egress, storage transactions, Log Analytics ingestion, private endpoints, and the database. The lead-level move is to present a range with the assumption that drives it, and to say which measurement would collapse the range fastest.

Example

Assume 1,200 RPS peak, p95 latency budget 200 ms, measured 80 concurrent requests/instance.
  concurrency = 1200 x 0.2 = 240 in flight
  instances   = 240 / 80   = 3   -> x2 peak headroom = 6 -> +1 for zone loss = 7
  plan        = 7 x P2v4  (4 vCPU / 16 GB) ~ steady base 4 instances reserved, 3 on demand
  NOT compute: egress 8 TB/mo, App Insights ~120 GB/mo ingest, 3 private endpoints, SQL Hyperscale
Range presented: EUR X-Y per month; the number that collapses it is per-instance concurrency,
which one load test on a single P2v4 gives us in an afternoon.

Follow-ups

  • What is usually the surprise line on the bill? — Log ingestion and egress. A chatty debug logger in production can cost more than the compute; so can inter-region traffic from a poorly placed dependency. Both are invisible in a SKU comparison.
  • Reservations or savings plans? — Reservations are cheaper but pin you to a family and region; savings plans (hourly compute commitment) are more flexible across SKU and region for slightly less discount. Cover only the base you are confident about, typically 60–70% of steady state.
  • How do you avoid over-provisioning “just in case”? — Autoscale with a real max, a budget alert, and a monthly review of P95 utilisation. Capacity you never use is invisible until someone audits it, so make it visible on a dashboard the team sees.
  • What if you genuinely cannot estimate load? — Choose a consumption-priced option for the first quarter (Flex Consumption, Container Apps scale-to-zero) so being wrong is cheap, and instrument for the metric you need. Buying flexibility while uncertain is a legitimate, articulable trade.

Wrong answer: “We'll start with the biggest SKU and scale down once we see real traffic.” It sounds pragmatic and it is how a lot of over-spend begins, because nobody ever schedules the scale-down. The strong version commits to the measurement and the date on which the decision is revisited.

Your monolith on VMs must move to Azure in six months. Design the migration. senior

Answer. Sequence by risk, not by elegance. Phase one is rehost the parts that carry no architectural debt — Azure Migrate for discovery and dependency mapping, then Site Recovery or Migrate's own replication to move VMs with minimal change — because it buys you a datacentre exit and a working baseline. Phase two is replatform the pieces where a managed service removes real toil: SQL Server to Azure SQL Managed Instance (near-full compatibility, so no code change) or Azure SQL Database if the app is already compatible, file shares to Azure Files, scheduled tasks to Container Apps jobs, and IIS-hosted web tiers to App Service — including, since September 2026, the GA'd managed-instance option on App Service for legacy .NET Framework apps that need a machine-like environment with an SLA. Phase three is refactor only what has a business reason: the modules that change often or scale differently. Throughout, keep one non-negotiable: the cutover must be reversible for at least one release cycle, which usually means dual-writes or a read-replica and a tested rollback, not a big-bang weekend.

Example

M0  Azure Migrate appliance: inventory + dependency map + right-size + cost model
M1  Landing zone: MG/sub structure, hub VNet, ExpressRoute or S2S VPN, DNS, policy baseline
M2  Data first, quietly: SQL -> Managed Instance via Azure DMS with continuous sync (no cutover yet)
M3  Rehost app tier to VMSS Flex behind App Gateway v2 + WAF; traffic still on-prem
M4  Cutover per module behind Front Door weighted routing; rollback = shift weight back
M5  Replatform: web tier -> App Service, batch -> Container Apps jobs, files -> Azure Files
M6  Decommission on-prem; only now delete the rollback path

Follow-ups

  • What kills these migrations? — Latency between a moved app tier and a not-yet-moved database. Move data first (or move both together), and measure the round trips per transaction — a chatty ORM that is fine at 0.2 ms is unusable at 20 ms.
  • What licensing lever do you name? — Azure Hybrid Benefit for Windows Server and SQL Server, plus Extended Security Updates for out-of-support versions, which are free in Azure for some products. Not mentioning licensing in a lift-and-shift answer is a gap; it is often the largest single line.
  • How do you avoid rebuilding the monolith's problems in the cloud? — Take the operational wins first (managed patching, backup, identity) and refuse refactors that are not tied to a measurable pain. “Strangler where it hurts” is a defensible position; “microservices because cloud” is not.
  • What do you do about the machines nobody owns? — They surface in dependency mapping and they are the schedule risk. Freeze them, document the owner search, and be willing to leave a small on-prem or IaaS remnant rather than let it block the exit.

Wrong answer: “Containerise everything first, then move — we get portability and a clean slate.” It doubles the work and moves the risk to the end, where the deadline is. Rehost-then-improve is unglamorous and it is what actually lands; interviewers reading for seniority want to hear you choose the boring order deliberately.

When would you deliberately choose not to use a managed Azure service? principal

Answer. Four defensible reasons, and the discipline is naming which one applies rather than reaching for “lock-in” as a slogan. Capability gap: the managed service cannot do something the workload requires — a database extension, a kernel module, a specific driver — and the workaround is worse than running it yourself. Cost at scale: managed pricing has a break-even, and past some sustained volume (large Kafka throughput, very high-cardinality metrics, petabyte object stores with heavy transactions) self-hosting on VMs is genuinely cheaper — but only if you count the people. Regulatory or residency: a control you cannot satisfy without owning the key material, the host, or the physical location. Exit cost: when a workload is the company's core differentiator and a future move is a board-level risk, paying an ongoing premium for portability can be rational — though most of the time the right hedge is a clean seam in the code, not self-hosting. The anti-pattern is choosing self-hosted for a reason you cannot quantify; the strongest form of this answer includes the number at which you would switch back.

Example

SituationManagedSelf-hostedDeciding number
Event streaming, 50 MB/s sustainedEvent HubsKafka on VMSSTU/PU cost vs 3 brokers + 0.5 FTE
PostgreSQL with an unsupported extensionAzure DB for PostgreSQLPG on VMsIs the extension on the supported list yet?
Redis at very large memoryAzure Managed RedisRedis cluster on VMsGB-hours vs patching + failover drills
Search over 2 TB with custom scoringAzure AI SearchOpenSearch on AKSReplica/partition cost vs cluster ops

Follow-ups

  • How do you count the people cost honestly? — Patching, upgrades, on-call, capacity planning, and the incident you will have at 03:00. A conservative rule is that a self-hosted stateful system costs at least a third of an engineer indefinitely; if the saving is smaller than that, it is not a saving.
  • Does “lock-in” ever justify it on its own? — Rarely, and only when there is a concrete scenario with a date. The cheaper hedge is an abstraction at the seam (a repository or a messaging port) so the swap is a module, plus data in an exportable format.
  • What is the reversibility test? — Ask what it costs to go back. Managed→self-hosted is usually easy; self-hosted→managed can be blocked by a feature you started depending on. Prefer the decision that is cheaper to reverse when the evidence is weak.
  • How do you keep this from being re-litigated every quarter? — Write the decision down with the trigger conditions (“revisit if sustained throughput exceeds X or the extension ships”) and review on that trigger, not on opinion. An architecture decision record with a trigger is the principal-level artefact.

Wrong answer: “We avoid managed services to stay cloud-agnostic.” Almost nobody actually exercises that option, and the cost is paid every day in operational load while the benefit is hypothetical. If you say it, the follow-up is “when did you last move a workload between clouds?” — and the honest answer is usually never.

03Networking & private connectivity

Networking is where Azure differs most from the other clouds and where senior candidates are separated fastest, because the failure modes are DNS and routing rather than code. Screener depth is VNets, subnets and NSGs; senior depth is hub-and-spoke with private endpoints and a firewall, and the DNS design that makes it work; principal depth is egress control, transitive routing, address-space planning across an estate, and the migration off retired SKUs. Expect at least one live troubleshooting question — “my app can't reach the database, walk me through it” — because it cannot be memorised.

Docs: Virtual networks · Private Endpoint · Private endpoint DNS configuration

Explain NSGs: how are rules evaluated, and what do they not protect? screener

Answer. A network security group is a stateful five-tuple filter with separate inbound and outbound rule sets, evaluated by priority (100–4096, lowest number first) with first match winning and no further evaluation. It attaches to a subnet, a NIC, or both — and when both apply, inbound traffic is evaluated subnet-first then NIC, outbound NIC-first then subnet, so a flow must be allowed by every NSG in its path. Because it is stateful, allowing an inbound flow implicitly allows the return traffic; you do not write a reciprocal rule. Default rules that you cannot delete allow VNet-to-VNet, allow outbound to the internet, and deny everything else inbound, which is why the common mistake is thinking a subnet with no NSG is closed — it is closed from the internet and wide open within the VNet. What NSGs do not give you: any layer-7 inspection, FQDN filtering, TLS termination, IDS/IPS, or protection against a compromised workload calling out to an allowed IP. Those are Azure Firewall, WAF, or a network virtual appliance.

Example

securityRules: [
  { name: 'allow-appgw-inbound', properties: { priority: 100, direction: 'Inbound', access: 'Allow'
    , protocol: 'Tcp', sourceApplicationSecurityGroups: [ { id: asgAppGw.id } ]
    , destinationApplicationSecurityGroups: [ { id: asgApi.id } ], destinationPortRange: '443'
    , sourcePortRange: '*' } }
  { name: 'deny-vnet-inbound', properties: { priority: 4000, direction: 'Inbound', access: 'Deny'
    , protocol: '*', sourceAddressPrefix: 'VirtualNetwork', destinationAddressPrefix: '*'
    , sourcePortRange: '*', destinationPortRange: '*' } }   // close the default VNet-wide allow
]

Application security groups replace IP lists with role names, so the rule survives re-addressing — the single biggest maintainability win in NSG design.

Follow-ups

  • What are service tags and why use them? — Microsoft-maintained, auto-updated IP groups (Sql, Storage.westeurope, AzureMonitor, AzureCloud). They keep rules correct as Azure's address ranges change; hand-maintained IP lists silently rot.
  • How do you debug a blocked flow? — NSG flow logs (now via Virtual Network flow logs) into a storage account or workspace, plus Connection Troubleshoot / IP flow verify in Network Watcher, which tells you the exact rule name that matched.
  • Does an NSG apply to a private endpoint? — Yes, since NSG support for private endpoints became generally available; historically it did not, which is why old designs used a dedicated subnet with privateEndpointNetworkPolicies: Disabled. Check which behaviour an existing estate relies on before you tighten it.
  • Where do NSGs sit relative to Azure Firewall? — NSG is the cheap, distributed, layer-4 control near the workload; Firewall is the centralised layer-7/FQDN/TLS-inspecting choke point reached by a route table. Use both: NSG for east-west micro-segmentation, Firewall for egress and inspection.

Wrong answer: “NSGs are stateless, so you need matching inbound and outbound rules.” They are stateful. Candidates import this from classic firewall ACLs, then write twice the rules and wonder why the return path breaks when they later add a deny.

My App Service cannot reach a SQL database over a private endpoint. Debug it. mid

Answer. It is DNS until proven otherwise. Resolve the hostname from inside the app — the Kudu console or a diagnostic endpoint — and check whether sql-orders.database.windows.net returns a private 10.x address or a public one. If public, the chain to check is: does a private DNS zone privatelink.database.windows.net exist, does it contain an A record for the endpoint, is it linked to the VNet the app integrates with, and is the app resolving through Azure DNS at all (a custom DNS server on the VNet must forward to 168.63.129.16 or it will never see the zone). If DNS is right and it still fails, check routing and filtering: is vnetRouteAllEnabled on, is there an NSG or firewall rule blocking 1433, does the SQL server have publicNetworkAccess disabled but the app going out via the public path anyway. Only then look at the database: firewall rules, the connection string, and whether the identity has a database user. The reason to run it in this order is that each step is a one-command check and the first two catch about 80% of real cases.

Example

# from the app's own shell (Kudu / SSH), not from your laptop
nslookup sql-orders.database.windows.net
# expect: sql-orders.privatelink.database.windows.net -> 10.20.4.7   (a CNAME then a private A)
# got a public IP? the zone isn't linked to this VNet, or custom DNS isn't forwarding

tcpping sql-orders.database.windows.net:1433      # App Service has tcpping built in
az network private-endpoint-connection list --id <sqlServerId> -o table
az network private-dns link vnet list -g rg-connectivity -z privatelink.database.windows.net -o table

Follow-ups

  • Why is 168.63.129.16 special? — It is the Azure platform's virtual DNS and health-probe address, reachable from every VNet. A custom DNS server that does not conditionally forward private-link zones to it is the classic cause of “works in one spoke, not another”.
  • Why is a CNAME involved at all? — The public name always CNAMEs to *.privatelink.*; your private zone then answers that second name with the private IP. That indirection is what lets the same connection string work publicly and privately, and it is why overriding the public name in a hosts file or custom zone breaks in confusing ways.
  • On-premises resolution over ExpressRoute? — On-prem DNS must conditionally forward the privatelink zones to a DNS forwarder in Azure (or use Azure DNS Private Resolver, the managed replacement for the two-VM forwarder pattern). Private endpoints are reachable over ExpressRoute private peering, but only if resolution works.
  • How would you prevent this class of bug? — Centralise the private DNS zones in the connectivity subscription, link every spoke automatically at vending time, and enforce it with an Azure Policy deployIfNotExists that creates the zone group on every private endpoint. Then nobody can create an unresolvable endpoint.

Wrong answer: “Add the App Service outbound IPs to the SQL server firewall.” It may even work — by routing you back over the public endpoint, defeating the private endpoint you just built, and it breaks the moment the platform changes outbound IPs. Interviewers watch for whether you notice you have undone the design.

Design hub-and-spoke for an estate with 40 spokes. What goes in the hub? senior

Answer. The hub holds the things that must be shared and inspected: the gateway (ExpressRoute and/or VPN) for on-premises, Azure Firewall as the egress and inter-spoke choke point, DNS (private DNS zones plus a Private Resolver for on-prem forwarding), Bastion for admin access, and any shared network virtual appliance. Spokes hold workloads and nothing shared. The two structural facts that drive the design: VNet peering is not transitive, so spoke-to-spoke traffic must be routed through the hub with user-defined routes pointing at the firewall's private IP; and the gateway must have allow gateway transit on the hub with use remote gateways on each spoke, or on-prem routes never reach the spokes. At 40 spokes the manual version becomes untenable, so the modern answer is Azure Virtual WAN with secured virtual hubs: it manages the routing tables, connections and branch scale for you, at the cost of some control and a different pricing model. Address planning matters more than any of it — a non-overlapping, contiguous plan per region with room for growth, because re-addressing a live spoke is the most expensive networking mistake there is.

Example

10.0.0.0/8 reserved for Azure
  10.0.0.0/16   hub-weu       10.1.0.0/16   hub-eus
    10.0.0.0/26   AzureFirewallSubnet         (name is fixed, /26 minimum)
    10.0.0.64/27  GatewaySubnet               (name is fixed, /27 or larger)
    10.0.0.96/26  AzureBastionSubnet          (name is fixed, /26 minimum)
    10.0.1.0/24   dns resolver + shared svc
  10.16.0.0/12  spokes-weu   (each spoke /20: workload /22, private endpoints /24, integration /26)
  10.32.0.0/12  spokes-eus
Route on every spoke workload subnet: 0.0.0.0/0 -> VirtualAppliance 10.0.0.4 (firewall)

Follow-ups

  • Why is peering not transitive, and what are the options? — Peering is a data-plane shortcut, not a router. Options are UDRs via a firewall/NVA in the hub, Virtual WAN's managed routing, or direct spoke-to-spoke peering for a few high-volume pairs (fast and cheap, but it bypasses inspection — a deliberate trade, not an oversight).
  • What do the reserved subnet names cost you? — GatewaySubnet, AzureFirewallSubnet, AzureBastionSubnet, RouteServerSubnet must be named exactly and sized minimally per the docs; getting the size wrong means recreating the gateway. Plan them at design time, not when you add the firewall.
  • Firewall availability? — Azure Firewall deploys zone-redundant by default in multi-AZ regions, and through 2026 Microsoft is migrating existing non-zonal firewalls to zone-redundant. A single-zone firewall in the hub is a single point of failure for 40 spokes; check this on any inherited estate.
  • When is Virtual WAN worth it? — Many branches, many regions, or when routing complexity is consuming a person. It is not cheaper at small scale, and it gives up some routing control, so “we run classic hub-and-spoke in two regions and it is fine” is a perfectly strong answer with the reason attached.

Wrong answer: “Peer every spoke to every other spoke so traffic is direct and fast.” That is n² peerings, no inspection point, and an unmaintainable rule surface. It also hits peering limits. The strong version peers a specific pair deliberately, for a measured latency or cost reason, and documents the inspection gap.

How does outbound internet connectivity work for Azure compute, and what changed recently? senior

Answer. There are four ways a workload gets to the internet, in order of preference: an explicit NAT Gateway on the subnet, an instance-level public IP, the outbound rules of a Standard Load Balancer, or — historically — default outbound access, the implicit platform SNAT that Azure gave any VM with no other path. That last one was retired for new deployments on 30 September 2025: new virtual networks no longer get implicit outbound internet access, so a VM created without an explicit method has no egress at all. This is the single most common “why does my new subnet have no internet” question in 2026. NAT Gateway is the right default because it has a huge, well-managed SNAT port pool (roughly 64,000 ports per attached public IP, allocated on demand rather than pre-allocated per instance), it removes the SNAT exhaustion class of failures, and it gives you a stable, allowlistable egress IP. Load Balancer outbound rules are the legacy way and require manual port allocation per backend instance, which is exactly the arithmetic that goes wrong under load.

Example

# SNAT exhaustion symptom: intermittent connection timeouts to an external API under load,
# while the API itself is healthy. Ports, not bandwidth.
az network nat gateway create -g rg-net -n natgw-spoke1 \
  --public-ip-addresses pip-natgw --idle-timeout 4
az network vnet subnet update -g rg-net --vnet-name vnet-spoke1 -n snet-workload \
  --nat-gateway natgw-spoke1
# and in the app: reuse connections. HttpClient as a singleton / IHttpClientFactory,
# because a new outbound socket per request is what burns the port pool.

Follow-ups

  • What exactly is SNAT exhaustion? — Each outbound flow to the same destination IP:port needs a distinct source port. With a small pool and short-lived connections in TIME_WAIT, you run out; new connections fail while existing ones are fine. The fixes are NAT Gateway, connection reuse, and reducing the idle timeout.
  • How do private endpoints interact with this? — Traffic to a private endpoint never leaves the VNet and consumes no SNAT ports, which is a real secondary benefit of using private endpoints for storage and databases at high request rates.
  • What if you need a fixed egress IP for a partner allowlist? — NAT Gateway with a public IP prefix, or route all egress through Azure Firewall and use its public IP(s). Never rely on App Service's documented outbound IP list as a stable contract — it changes when the plan scales or moves.
  • What about the Basic SKU retirements? — Basic Load Balancer and Basic public IPs both retired on 30 September 2025. Existing resources may still function but are unsupported and outside the SLA; Microsoft ships upgrade scripts and Advisor recommendations. Finding these in an estate is a migration ticket with a real deadline attached.

Wrong answer: “Outbound just works; Azure gives every VM internet access by default.” True until 30 September 2025, and now wrong for anything new — a perfect example of a confidently-stated fact that dates a candidate. The current answer starts with “you must choose an explicit egress method.”

Private Link, private endpoint, service endpoint, Private Link Service — distinguish them. mid

Answer. Private Link is the umbrella technology. A private endpoint is a NIC with a private IP in your subnet that maps to a specific instance of a PaaS service (this storage account, this SQL server, this Key Vault) — traffic never touches the internet, and the service can then refuse all public access. A service endpoint is the older mechanism: it does not give the service a private IP; it tags traffic from a subnet so the service's own firewall can allow that subnet, keeping the traffic on the Microsoft backbone but leaving the public endpoint in place and unreachable from on-premises. A Private Link Service is the inverse direction: you put a Standard Load Balancer in front of your own service and expose it to other VNets or other tenants as a private endpoint they create — the mechanism SaaS vendors use to be reachable privately from customer networks. Modern guidance is private endpoints for PaaS access, service endpoints only where cost or IP-count pressure justifies them, and Private Link Service when you are the provider.

Example

Private endpointService endpoint
Gets a private IPyes, in your subnetno
Reachable from on-premyes (VPN/ER + DNS)no
Public endpoint can be disabledyesno
Granularityone resource (or sub-resource)whole service in a region
Costper endpoint per hour + datafree
DNS work requiredyes, private zonesnone

Follow-ups

  • What is a sub-resource / groupId? — Services with multiple endpoints need one private endpoint each: a storage account needs separate endpoints for blob, file, queue, table, dfs. Forgetting the second one is a common half-private configuration.
  • Does a private endpoint stop data exfiltration? — Not by itself: a compromised workload can still reach other storage accounts publicly unless you also block egress. Combine private endpoints with firewall egress rules or storage's own network rules for a real exfiltration control.
  • Cost at scale? — Private endpoints are billed hourly plus per GB, and they consume subnet IPs and subscription quota. An estate with hundreds of them is a real line item; some teams share endpoints per environment via the hub rather than per spoke.
  • Why would anyone still use service endpoints? — Zero cost, zero DNS work, and no IP consumption for high-volume access to storage from many subnets where on-prem access is not required. It is a legitimate, articulable trade, not just legacy.

Wrong answer: “Service endpoints and private endpoints do the same thing; private endpoints are the newer name.” They differ on the two things that matter most in an audit — whether the public endpoint can be turned off, and whether on-premises can reach it. Interviewers use this question to check that you have actually designed a private estate rather than read about one.

ExpressRoute versus VPN gateway: how do you connect a datacentre, and how do you make it resilient? senior

Answer. A site-to-site VPN is an IPsec tunnel over the public internet: quick to stand up, cheap, throughput to roughly 10 Gbps on the largest SKUs, and with internet-grade jitter and no bandwidth SLA end to end. ExpressRoute is a private circuit through a connectivity provider into a Microsoft edge location: predictable latency, an SLA, bandwidths from 50 Mbps to 100 Gbps, and access to Microsoft peering for some services — but weeks to provision and a monthly cost that starts meaningful.
