The senior answer chooses by requirement and then designs the failure: ExpressRoute alone is not redundant unless you have two circuits at different peering locations (a single circuit already has two connections, which protects the port but not the site), and the standard cost-effective pattern is ExpressRoute primary with a site-to-site VPN as failover, with BGP weights or AS-path prepending making the preference explicit. ExpressRoute Direct and Global Reach are the answers for very high bandwidth and for connecting on-prem sites through Microsoft's backbone respectively; FastPath bypasses the gateway for data-plane throughput.

Example

Primary:   ExpressRoute 1 Gbps, peering location AMS, private peering, FastPath on
Secondary: ExpressRoute 1 Gbps, peering location FRA        (different location = real redundancy)
Tertiary:  S2S VPN over internet, BGP, AS-path prepended twice so it only wins when both ER are down
Test:      quarterly failover drill - disable primary at the provider, measure convergence and
           whether latency-sensitive apps survive the extra 12 ms
Watch:     gateway SKU throughput is shared by ALL connections; ErGw1Az != unlimited

Follow-ups

  • What do people forget about ExpressRoute? — That the ExpressRoute gateway SKU caps throughput independently of the circuit, that Global Reach is a separate paid feature, and that a circuit at one peering location is a single geographic failure domain.
  • How does this interact with Virtual WAN? — Virtual WAN hubs terminate ExpressRoute and VPN and handle transit routing between them and the spokes, which is the main reason multi-region, many-branch estates adopt it.
  • Do private endpoints work over ExpressRoute? — Yes over private peering, provided on-prem DNS forwards the privatelink zones into Azure. Microsoft peering plus public endpoints is the legacy pattern you migrate away from.
  • What is the encryption story? — ExpressRoute is private but not encrypted by default; if a control requires encryption in transit over the circuit you add MACsec at the Direct layer or run IPsec over ExpressRoute. Auditors ask this and candidates often assume “private” means “encrypted”.

Wrong answer: “ExpressRoute is redundant by design, so one circuit is enough.” The two connections in a circuit protect against a device or port failure at one peering location; they do not protect against that location or that provider failing. Every serious ExpressRoute design names a second peering location or an explicit VPN fallback.

How do you control and inspect egress traffic for a regulated workload? senior

Answer. Force all egress through a chokepoint and make bypass impossible. Concretely: a UDR on every workload subnet sending 0.0.0.0/0 to Azure Firewall (Premium, if you need TLS inspection and IDPS), application rules with FQDN allowlists rather than IP allowlists, network rules for the few non-HTTP protocols, and a default deny with logging. Then close the bypasses, which is the part candidates miss: App Service and Functions need vnetRouteAllEnabled or they only route RFC 1918; AKS needs outboundType: userDefinedRouting and the documented required FQDNs allowed or the cluster will not start; storage and database access should move to private endpoints so it never hits the firewall at all; and Azure Policy should deny public IPs on NICs so nobody can attach a direct route out. Finally, feed firewall logs into a workspace and alert on denied destinations — an egress control with no telemetry is a control you cannot tune, and the first week will be almost entirely legitimate traffic you did not know about.

Example

{ "ruleCollectionType": "FirewallPolicyFilterRuleCollection", "name": "workload-egress",
  "priority": 300, "action": { "type": "Allow" },
  "rules": [
    { "ruleType": "ApplicationRule", "name": "package-feeds",
      "sourceAddresses": [ "10.16.0.0/12" ], "protocols": [ { "protocolType": "Https", "port": 443 } ],
      "targetFqdns": [ "*.nuget.org", "registry.npmjs.org", "*.blob.core.windows.net" ] },
    { "ruleType": "ApplicationRule", "name": "aks-required",
      "sourceAddresses": [ "10.16.4.0/22" ], "protocols": [ { "protocolType": "Https", "port": 443 } ],
      "fqdnTags": [ "AzureKubernetesService" ] }
  ] }

Follow-ups

  • Why FQDN rules rather than IPs? — SaaS endpoints sit behind CDNs whose IPs change hourly. An IP allowlist to *.blob.core.windows.net is either impossibly broad or permanently broken; FQDN rules (and fqdnTags for Microsoft's own maintained sets) are the only maintainable form.
  • What is the cost of TLS inspection? — A private CA whose certificate every workload must trust, a real latency and throughput cost, and legal/privacy review. Do it for the traffic that justifies it, not as a blanket setting; and expect certificate pinning in some SDKs to break.
  • How do you roll this out without an outage? — Deploy the firewall in allow-all-with-logging mode, harvest the actual destinations for two to four weeks, build the allowlist from evidence, then flip to deny in one environment at a time with a fast rollback (remove the UDR).
  • What is the residual risk? — Exfiltration to an allowed destination, such as a public storage account under *.blob.core.windows.net. Narrow that with per-account FQDNs, Azure Firewall's storage-specific rules, or Defender for Storage. Saying this unprompted is a strong senior signal.

Wrong answer: “We put NSGs on every subnet with an outbound deny to the internet.” NSGs cannot express FQDNs, so you will either allow whole service tags (far too broad) or break package restores. The right answer names the layer that can do names, and NSGs stay as the east-west control.

How do you plan IP address space for an Azure estate that will grow for a decade? principal

Answer. Treat address space as a scarce, hard-to-change resource and plan it centrally, because re-addressing a live spoke means recreating every resource with a fixed IP and every peering, and nobody ever gets time to do it. Practically: get a large, contiguous, non-overlapping allocation from whoever owns enterprise addressing (typically a /12 or /8 out of RFC 1918, aware of what on-prem and other clouds already use); carve per-region blocks so summarisation on-prem stays simple; carve per-spoke blocks of a uniform size (a /20 or /21 is usually right — generous enough that nobody asks for a second, small enough that you have hundreds); and within a spoke, reserve fixed sub-blocks by purpose so automation can compute subnets rather than negotiate them. Then automate the allocation — an IPAM system, or at minimum a source-controlled allocation file that subscription vending consumes — because the failure mode is not running out, it is two teams being handed the same range six months apart. Plan for private endpoints and AKS explicitly: AKS with Azure CNI consumes IPs per pod unless you use overlay, and a large cluster can eat a /16 by accident.

Example

Enterprise: 10.0.0.0/8 shared with on-prem (on-prem holds 10.100.0.0/14, do not touch)
  Azure:      10.0.0.0/11
    weu hub   10.0.0.0/16
    weu spokes 10.16.0.0/12   -> /20 per spoke (4,096 addresses, 16 spokes per /16)
      spoke layout, fixed by automation:
        x.x.0.0/22   workload / AKS nodes
        x.x.4.0/24   private endpoints
        x.x.5.0/26   app service integration (delegated)
        x.x.5.64/26  reserved
        x.x.8.0/21   growth - do not allocate
AKS: Azure CNI Overlay so pods use 10.244.0.0/16 (non-routable, reused per cluster)

Follow-ups

  • What if you have already run out? — You can add address space to a VNet (and, since it is supported now, remove or resize prefixes with constraints), and you can use non-RFC1918 private space such as 100.64.0.0/10 for AKS pods or private endpoints that never need on-prem reachability. Both are escape valves, not plans.
  • Azure CNI, CNI Overlay, or kubenet? — Overlay is the current default recommendation: pods get IPs from a private overlay range that is not consumed from the VNet, so a cluster costs a node-sized subnet rather than a pod-sized one. Kubenet is legacy and being retired; flat Azure CNI only when pods must be directly routable.
  • How does this interact with mergers? — Overlapping RFC 1918 space is the norm after an acquisition. The tools are NAT (on an NVA or via Virtual WAN), Private Link (which needs no routable path at all), or re-addressing the smaller estate. Private Link is usually the cheapest answer and worth naming first.
  • Who owns the plan? — The platform team, published as code, consumed by vending. If allocation happens in a spreadsheet by email, it will collide; making it a pull request makes the collision a merge conflict instead of an outage.

Wrong answer: “We give every VNet 10.0.0.0/16 since they're isolated anyway.” They are isolated right until the day someone needs to peer two of them or reach on-prem — and then the fix is recreating a production network. Overlapping space is the most common irreversible mistake in a young Azure estate.

What is Azure DNS Private Resolver and why did it replace the DNS-forwarder-VM pattern? mid

Answer. Before it existed, resolving Azure private DNS zones from on-premises required you to run your own DNS forwarder VMs in a hub VNet (usually a pair, for availability), configure on-prem DNS to conditionally forward the privatelink.* zones to them, and then patch, monitor and pay for those VMs forever. Azure DNS Private Resolver is the managed replacement: it provides an inbound endpoint (an IP in your VNet that on-prem DNS can forward to, so on-prem resolves Azure private zones) and an outbound endpoint with forwarding rulesets (so Azure workloads resolve on-prem or third-party zones), with no VMs, zone redundancy, and per-query pricing. The design implication is that DNS becomes a hub service like the firewall: one resolver per region in the connectivity subscription, private zones centralised there, and every spoke VNet linked to the zones it needs — usually automatically at vending time.

Example

az dns-resolver create -g rg-connectivity -n dnspr-weu --id /subscriptions/.../vnet-hub-weu
az dns-resolver inbound-endpoint create  -g rg-connectivity --dns-resolver-name dnspr-weu \
  -n inbound --ip-configurations '[{"private-ip-allocation-method":"Dynamic","id":"<snet-dns-in>"}]'
az dns-resolver outbound-endpoint create -g rg-connectivity --dns-resolver-name dnspr-weu \
  -n outbound --id <snet-dns-out>
# on-prem DNS: conditional forward privatelink.database.windows.net -> <inbound endpoint IP>

Follow-ups

  • Do spokes need their own resolver? — No. Link the private DNS zones to each spoke VNet and let Azure DNS answer locally; the resolver exists for crossing the Azure/on-prem boundary in either direction.
  • What are the subnet requirements? — Each endpoint needs its own dedicated, delegated subnet in the resolver's VNet (Microsoft.Network/dnsResolvers), which is another reason to reserve those ranges in the hub plan up front.
  • What breaks if a spoke is not linked to the zone? — Public resolution of the privatelink CNAME, so traffic goes to the public endpoint and either fails against a firewall or — worse — succeeds over the internet, silently defeating the private design. Enforce zone links with policy.
  • Any reason to keep the VM forwarders? — Only very specific conditional-forwarding or split-horizon behaviour the resolver's rulesets cannot express, or an existing appliance-based DNS strategy. Otherwise it is pure operational debt.

Wrong answer: “Point the VNet's DNS setting at our on-prem DNS servers and we're done.” Then Azure workloads lose the ability to resolve private DNS zones unless on-prem forwards them back, and you have created a circular dependency where a VPN outage also breaks name resolution inside Azure. Custom DNS on a VNet must always forward to 168.63.129.16 for Azure-internal names.

04Data services: SQL & Cosmos DB

Every Azure loop has a data question, and it is usually “which database, and how do you make it fast and available” rather than SQL syntax. Screener depth is knowing the service tiers exist; senior depth is partitioning, consistency, and the cost/latency consequences of a schema choice; principal depth is multi-region write topology, migration without downtime, and knowing when the managed service stops being the right answer. Cosmos DB questions in particular are really distributed-systems questions wearing an Azure badge.

Docs: Azure SQL service tiers · Cosmos DB partitioning · Consistency levels

Azure SQL Database, Managed Instance, or SQL Server on a VM — how do you choose? screener

Answer. Choose by how much SQL Server surface area you need. Azure SQL Database is a single database as a service: no SQL Agent, no cross-database queries, no CLR or Service Broker, but the best availability, scaling and price story, plus Hyperscale and serverless tiers. Managed Instance gives you near-complete instance-level compatibility — SQL Agent, cross-database queries, linked servers, CLR, native backup/restore, a VNet-injected private IP — which makes it the standard lift-and-shift target for a legacy on-prem SQL Server; the price is a longer provisioning time, a VNet subnet requirement, and a higher floor cost. SQL Server on an Azure VM is IaaS: full control, any version, any feature (FILESTREAM, specific trace flags, third-party agents), and full responsibility for patching, backup and HA. The decision rule most interviewers want to hear is: start at SQL Database, step up to Managed Instance for a named compatibility blocker, and go to a VM only for something Managed Instance genuinely cannot do.

Example

RequirementSQL DBMIVM
SQL Agent jobsno (use Elastic Jobs)yesyes
Cross-database queriesno (Elastic Query, limited)yesyes
Native RESTORE FROM URLnoyesyes
Scale to 100 TBHyperscaleup to 16 TBdisk-limited
Scale to zero when idleserverlessnostop the VM
Patching & backupMicrosoftMicrosoftyou

Follow-ups

  • DTU or vCore? — vCore for anything new: it is transparent about CPU, memory and storage, it supports Azure Hybrid Benefit (a large discount if you own SQL licences), and Hyperscale and serverless only exist there. DTU survives in old estates and is a migration item, not a choice.
  • What is Hyperscale actually? — A re-architected storage layer where the log is the source of truth and data pages live in a distributed page server tier: near-instant backups regardless of size, storage to 128 TB, fast scale-out of read replicas, and restore times that do not grow with database size. The trade-offs are that some features differ and moving out of Hyperscale is not a simple tier change.
  • When is serverless right? — Intermittent, unpredictable workloads — dev/test, internal tools, per-tenant databases with idle periods. It bills per second on used vCores and auto-pauses; the cost is a resume latency of tens of seconds, which is unacceptable for a user-facing API.
  • Elastic pools? — For many databases with uncorrelated peaks (classic per-tenant SaaS): they share a pool of resources so you pay for the aggregate rather than the sum of the peaks. The failure mode is correlated load — if all tenants peak at 09:00, the pool is just a smaller total.

Wrong answer: “Managed Instance, because it's the closest to real SQL Server and we won't have to change anything.” Sometimes true, but as a default it buys cost and provisioning friction for compatibility you may not need. The interviewer wants the named blocker; “we might need SQL Agent someday” is not one.

How do you choose a Cosmos DB partition key, and what happens when you choose badly? mid

Answer. The partition key determines how documents are distributed across physical partitions, and it must satisfy three things simultaneously: high cardinality so data spreads, even access distribution so no single logical partition becomes a hot spot, and alignment with your most frequent query so reads are single-partition rather than fan-out. It should also keep any logical partition under the 20 GB limit — that limit is per logical partition (a single key value), and hitting it is a hard failure you cannot fix without a data migration. Bad choices fail in three distinct ways: a low-cardinality key (tenant, country, status) creates hot partitions where you are throttled at a fraction of your provisioned RU/s because each physical partition only gets its share; a key that does not match your queries makes every read a cross-partition fan-out that costs RU proportional to partitions touched; and an unbounded key (one tenant that grows forever) hits the 20 GB wall. When no single field works, hierarchical partition keys (up to three levels, e.g. /tenantId then /userId then /sessionId) let you keep the tenant prefix for query routing while sub-partitioning to escape the size and throughput ceiling — that is the modern answer to the classic multi-tenant problem.

Example

// bad: /tenantId - one enterprise tenant is 90% of the data and all of the traffic
// good: hierarchical, so queries by tenant still route, but storage and RU spread by user
var props = new ContainerProperties("events", new List<string> { "/tenantId", "/userId" });
await db.CreateContainerIfNotExistsAsync(props, ThroughputProperties.CreateAutoscaleThroughput(4000));

// single-partition read: cheap, ~1 RU for a point read
await c.ReadItemAsync<Event>(id, new PartitionKeyBuilder()
    .Add(tenantId).Add(userId).Build());

// always look at the charge; it is the only honest performance metric in Cosmos
Console.WriteLine(response.RequestCharge);

Follow-ups

  • What are the symptoms of a hot partition? — HTTP 429 with x-ms-retry-after-ms while your total consumed RU/s is far below what you provisioned, and the Azure Monitor “normalized RU consumption” metric pinned at 100% for one partition key range. That metric is the diagnostic; total RU is not.
  • Can you change a partition key? — Not in place. Historically the answer was a new container plus a change-feed-driven migration and a dual-read window; there is now a partition key change capability with constraints, but plan as if it is a migration, because on a large container it is.
  • What if you need queries on two different keys? — Either denormalise into a second container keyed differently and keep it in sync with the change feed, or use global secondary indexes (announced at Build 2026), which maintain an alternate-key materialised view for you. Name both; the second is the current-tooling answer.
  • How do you know a query fans out? — Diagnostics show the partition key ranges touched, and the RU charge scales with them. In practice, if the query has no equality filter on the partition key, assume fan-out and design accordingly.

Wrong answer: “Use /id as the partition key — it's unique, so distribution is perfect.” Distribution is perfect and every query that is not a point read becomes a full fan-out, and you can never efficiently query “all events for this user”. It is the textbook trap: cardinality alone is not the criterion.

Explain Cosmos DB's five consistency levels and when you'd choose each. senior

Answer. From strongest to weakest: Strong (linearizable — every read sees the latest committed write; costs roughly double the RU of eventual for reads and, crucially, cannot be used with multi-region writes and adds cross-region write latency when regions are far apart), Bounded staleness (reads lag by at most K versions or T seconds, which you choose — the practical choice when you want a stated guarantee without Strong's cost), Session (the default and the right answer for most applications: monotonic reads, monotonic writes and read-your-own-writes within a session token, at eventual-consistency cost), Consistent prefix (you never see writes out of order, but you may see stale data), and Eventual (no ordering guarantee; cheapest and lowest latency). The point interviewers are testing is that this is a per-account default which a request can weaken but never strengthen, and that Session's guarantee travels in the session token — so a stateless web tier behind a load balancer loses read-your-writes unless it flows the token, typically via a cookie or a distributed cache.

Example

// Session consistency is per-session. Two API instances = two sessions unless you carry the token.
var write = await container.CreateItemAsync(order, new PartitionKey(order.TenantId));
httpContext.Response.Cookies.Append("cosmos-session", write.Headers.Session);

// next request, possibly on another instance:
var opts = new ItemRequestOptions { SessionToken = httpContext.Request.Cookies["cosmos-session"] };
var read = await container.ReadItemAsync<Order>(id, new PartitionKey(tenantId), opts);
// without SessionToken this read may legitimately not see the write you just made

Follow-ups

  • Why is Strong incompatible with multi-region writes? — Linearizability across regions accepting concurrent writes would require cross-region coordination on every write; Cosmos instead offers Strong only with a single write region (and within a limited latency envelope). Choosing multi-region writes is choosing to resolve conflicts, not to avoid them.
  • How are conflicts resolved with multi-region writes? — Last-writer-wins on a configurable numeric property (default _ts) by default, or a custom stored-procedure resolver, or a conflicts feed you drain yourself. If your domain cannot tolerate LWW — inventory, balances — multi-region writes are a design decision with real application work attached.
  • What does bounded staleness actually buy you? — A number you can put in a design document and test against, e.g. “a replica is never more than 5 seconds behind”. It is the honest middle ground for read replicas serving dashboards where Strong is too expensive and Eventual is unexplainable to stakeholders.
  • Does consistency affect cost? — Yes: Strong and bounded staleness reads cost about twice the RU of session/eventual reads for the same operation. Consistency is a line on the bill, not only a correctness setting.

Wrong answer: “We use Strong so we never have to think about stale reads.” It doubles read cost, blocks multi-region writes, raises write latency, and is almost never required end-to-end because there is a cache or a queue in the path anyway. The strong answer starts at Session and justifies any move away from it.

RU/s: manual or autoscale, database-level or container-level? How do you size and control cost? senior

Answer. A request unit is Cosmos DB's normalised cost of work — a 1 KB point read is 1 RU, a write is roughly 5+, a query costs in proportion to items examined and index usage. You provision RU/s per container or shared across a database. Manual throughput is cheapest per RU and right when load is predictable; autoscale costs about 50% more per RU but scales instantly between 10% and 100% of a maximum you set, so it wins whenever peak-to-average ratio exceeds roughly 2:1, and it removes a whole class of 429 incidents. Database-level shared throughput is for many small containers that are individually cheap (the per-container minimum is otherwise wasteful) but it means noisy-neighbour behaviour between containers and a limit on how many share. Sizing method: measure the actual RU charge of your top operations (the SDK returns it on every response), multiply by expected operations per second, add headroom for the peak-to-average ratio, and then check the minimum — RU/s cannot go below a floor derived from storage and historical peak, so a large container has a cost floor you cannot autoscale away.

Example

Top operations, measured from response.RequestCharge:
  point read order        1.0 RU   x 400/s = 400
  query orders by tenant  8.4 RU   x  60/s = 504
  write order            12.8 RU   x  40/s = 512
                                   base   ~1,420 RU/s
peak 3x average -> autoscale max 6,000 RU/s (floor 600, billed 1.5x per RU used)
manual equivalent would need 6,000 provisioned 24/7 -> autoscale wins here
sanity check: minimum RU/s = max(400, storage_GB x 10, historical_peak/100)

Follow-ups

  • What do you do about 429s? — The SDK retries automatically with the server's retry-after; the questions are whether your latency budget survives the retry and whether the root cause is a hot partition rather than genuine capacity. Raising RU/s to fix a hot partition is paying to spread a problem you have not fixed.
  • Serverless Cosmos — when? — Genuinely spiky, low-average workloads (dev, small internal tools) with a single region and no need for SLA-backed high throughput. It bills per RU consumed with no floor, but has lower storage and throughput ceilings.
  • How do you reduce RU on an existing container? — Tune the indexing policy (Cosmos indexes everything by default; excluding unqueried paths can cut write RU substantially), avoid cross-partition queries, prefer point reads to queries, and batch with transactional batch within a partition key.
  • What does adding a region cost? — Roughly the provisioned throughput again per region, plus egress for replication. Multi-region read replicas are a real cost decision, not a checkbox.

Wrong answer: “Set autoscale to a high maximum and let it handle everything.” Autoscale bills for the maximum you actually reach, and a runaway query or a fan-out bug can reach it instantly — teams have discovered a five-figure day this way. The control is a sane maximum plus a budget alert plus fixing the query.

How do you make Azure SQL highly available across zones and regions, and what are the RTO/RPO numbers? senior

Answer. Three layers, and the honest answer names the numbers. Within a datacentre, General Purpose separates compute from remote storage (failover means restarting compute elsewhere, tens of seconds), while Business Critical runs a local Always On availability group with replicas on local SSD (failover in seconds, plus a free readable secondary). Across zones, zone redundancy places those replicas in different availability zones — a checkbox on Business Critical, Hyperscale and Premium/General Purpose in supported regions, at little or no extra cost, and the single highest-value reliability setting most estates miss. Across regions, active geo-replication gives asynchronous readable secondaries with an RPO of about 5 seconds and an RTO of about 30 seconds on manual failover, and failover groups wrap that with a stable listener endpoint and optional automatic failover with a grace period, so the application connection string does not change. Backups are the fourth layer and a different question: point-in-time restore within the retention window (1–35 days, or up to 10 years with long-term retention) is what saves you from a bad migration, and geo-restore is the last resort with an RPO measured in an hour.

Example

az sql db update -g rg-data -s sql-orders-weu -n orders --zone-redundant true
az sql failover-group create -n fog-orders -g rg-data -s sql-orders-weu \
  --partner-server sql-orders-neu --partner-resource-group rg-data-neu \
  --failover-policy Automatic --grace-period 1 --add-db orders
# app uses fog-orders.database.windows.net - the listener follows the primary
# read-only replicas: ApplicationIntent=ReadOnly on the same connection string

Follow-ups

  • What is the risk of automatic failover? — Asynchronous replication means an automatic failover can lose the last few seconds of transactions, and a grace period that is too short will fail over on a transient regional blip. One hour is the usual default; shorter only with a clear appetite for data loss.
  • Does a failover group cover the whole application? — No. It moves the database. Your app tier, caches, storage accounts and DNS have to move too, which is why an untested regional failover is a plan, not a capability. Rehearse it.
  • How do you use the read replica well? — ApplicationIntent=ReadOnly routes to the secondary, which is free on Business Critical and a genuine capacity win for reports — but the replica is asynchronous, so anything requiring read-your-writes must go to the primary.
  • What about Managed Instance? — Same shape, with failover groups at the instance level; be aware that MI failover moves the whole instance, and that its zone redundancy support arrived later and is not available on every tier.

Wrong answer: “Azure SQL is a managed service with a 99.99% SLA, so it's already highly available.” The SLA is a credit, not an architecture, and the default single-zone General Purpose deployment loses the database for the duration of a zone incident. The interviewer is checking whether you know the difference between a service being managed and a workload being resilient.

Cosmos DB or Azure SQL for a new service? Argue both sides. senior

Answer. Pick Cosmos when the access pattern is known, narrow and key-based; when you need single-digit-millisecond reads at high volume; when you need turnkey multi-region distribution with a real latency benefit; when the schema is genuinely heterogeneous; or when the workload is a natural fit for the change feed as an event source. Pick SQL when you need joins across entities that are not co-located, ad-hoc and reporting queries, multi-row transactions across arbitrary rows, strong relational constraints, or simply when the team's existing skill and tooling make it faster to build correctly. The economic dimension matters as much as the technical one: Cosmos costs are dominated by RU/s, so a chatty or badly-partitioned access pattern is expensive in a way that a suboptimal SQL query on a fixed-size database is not — Cosmos punishes design mistakes with a bill, SQL punishes them with latency. The strongest form of this answer names the query you cannot serve: “if we ever need `orders joined to customers filtered by an arbitrary attribute`, Cosmos will make that a fan-out, so either that requirement is out of scope or SQL is the right choice.”

Example

SignalPoints to
“Every read is by user id”, millions of users, 5 ms p99Cosmos
“Finance needs arbitrary reports over 3 years”SQL (or a warehouse)
Writes must be transactional across unrelated entitiesSQL
Active-active in 4 regions with local writesCosmos
Document shapes vary per tenant, evolving weeklyCosmos
Team of 6 with deep T-SQL experience, 3-month deadlineSQL

Follow-ups

  • Can you use both? — Yes, and it is common: Cosmos for the operational hot path, change feed into SQL or Fabric/Synapse for analytics and reporting. Say it explicitly, because “one database for everything” is usually the wrong shape at scale.
  • What about PostgreSQL on Azure? — Azure Database for PostgreSQL Flexible Server is a strong third option and often the right one for a team that prefers Postgres: extensions, pgvector for embeddings, and Citus-based sharding via the elastic cluster option. Not naming it when the team is a Postgres shop is a miss.
  • How do you avoid a one-way door? — Keep persistence behind a narrow interface and avoid leaking store-specific semantics (Cosmos partition keys, SQL identity columns) into the domain. It will not make migration free, but it makes it a bounded project.
  • What is the Cosmos analytical story now? — Mirroring into Microsoft Fabric (the successor to the older Synapse Link analytical store) gives near-real-time analytics without touching provisioned RU/s. Running analytics against the transactional store is the mistake it exists to prevent.

Wrong answer: “Cosmos DB, because it scales infinitely and SQL doesn't.” Cosmos scales if and only if your partition key does, and “infinite scale” is a marketing claim that a hot tenant demolishes in an afternoon. Interviewers ask the partition-key follow-up immediately, and this answer walks into it.

How do you connect to Azure SQL without a password? mid

Answer. Use Microsoft Entra authentication with a managed identity. Two steps: set an Entra admin on the logical server (ideally an Entra group, so administration is not tied to one person), then create a contained database user mapped to the identity's name and grant it roles — no password exists anywhere, and the connection string carries only the server, database and Authentication=Active Directory Default. On the application side, the driver acquires a token via the identity chain, so the app's configuration contains no secret and there is nothing to rotate. The subtleties worth naming: the database user is created FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER and must be created by an Entra-authenticated principal (a SQL admin cannot do it), the identity's name is the link so renaming the managed identity breaks the mapping, and tokens last about an hour so long-lived connection pools must handle re-authentication — modern drivers do, older ones do not.

Example

-- run as the Entra admin, connected with an Entra token
CREATE USER [id-orders-api] FROM EXTERNAL PROVIDER;   -- the managed identity's name
ALTER ROLE db_datareader ADD MEMBER [id-orders-api];
ALTER ROLE db_datawriter ADD MEMBER [id-orders-api];
GRANT EXECUTE ON SCHEMA::orders TO [id-orders-api];   -- not db_owner
// no password, no Key Vault lookup, nothing to rotate
var cs = "Server=tcp:sql-orders.database.windows.net,1433;Database=orders;"
       + "Authentication=Active Directory Default;Encrypt=True;";
await using var conn = new SqlConnection(cs);

Follow-ups

  • How do you do this in a deployment pipeline that runs migrations? — The pipeline's federated workload identity is added as a database user with the DDL rights it needs, scoped to the schema. Do not reuse the app's identity for migrations: different privilege, different lifetime.
  • What about local development? — Developers authenticate as themselves through an Entra group that is also a database user, so the same code path works locally with no fallback to SQL authentication. If you keep a SQL login “for dev”, it will end up in production.
  • Can you disable SQL authentication entirely? — Yes, Entra-only authentication is a server-level setting, and it is the control that makes the design real. Without it, someone will create a login and a password next quarter.
  • What if the app is not in Azure? — Workload identity federation from the external platform, or a service principal with a certificate as the fallback. The pattern degrades gracefully; a shared SQL password does not.

Wrong answer: “Store the SQL password in Key Vault and fetch it at startup.” It is a genuine improvement over appsettings and it is still a password: it can be read by anyone with vault access, it must be rotated, and rotation means a restart. It also invites the follow-up “and how does the app authenticate to Key Vault?” — which is a managed identity, so you already had the better answer available.

Migrate a 4 TB on-premises SQL Server to Azure with under 15 minutes of downtime. How? principal

Answer. Online migration with continuous sync, and a cutover that is a DNS or connection-string flip rather than a restore. Concretely: choose the target first (Managed Instance if there is any instance-level dependency, SQL Database otherwise) and prove compatibility with the Data Migration Assistant or the Azure SQL migration extension, fixing blockers in the source weeks ahead. Then use Azure Database Migration Service in online mode — for Managed Instance this is log-shipping based (full backup, then continuous log restore from a storage account); for SQL Database it is a change-data-capture pipeline. Seed the initial 4 TB days in advance over ExpressRoute or by shipping backups to blob storage, let the log tail catch up, and monitor the lag until it is seconds. The cutover window then contains only: stop writes at the source, wait for the final log to apply, run the “complete migration” step, repoint the application, smoke-test, open the gates. Everything else — statistics, index rebuild, users and logins, jobs, linked servers — is done before the window, because those are the tasks that turn 15 minutes into three hours.

Example

T-6w  DMA assessment; fix deprecated syntax, cross-db refs, unsupported features
T-4w  Provision MI in the right subnet; ExpressRoute path proven; logins/jobs scripted
T-2w  Full backup to blob; DMS online migration starts; continuous log restore
T-1w  Rehearsal cutover on a copy; measure every step with a stopwatch; write the runbook
T-2d  Freeze schema changes at the source
T-0   [00:00] stop app writes  [00:02] last log restored, lag = 0  [00:04] complete migration
      [00:06] repoint connection strings / flip DNS  [00:08] smoke tests  [00:12] traffic on
Rollback: source is untouched and still current until writes resume - flip back within the window

Follow-ups

  • What is the most common cause of overrun? — Post-cutover work nobody scheduled: missing logins (orphaned users), statistics not updated so the first queries pick terrible plans, and jobs that were never migrated. Script all three and run them before the window.
  • How do you handle rollback? — Keep the source authoritative and unwritten-to until you commit; rollback is repointing the app. Once you allow writes to Azure, rollback means reverse replication, which almost nobody has built — so define the exact point of no return in the runbook.
  • What about performance after the move? — Query Store on the source gives you the pre-migration plan baseline; enable it on the target and compare. Expect the compatibility level and cardinality estimator differences to change some plans, and pin the level initially if you need a like-for-like cutover.
  • How do you validate 4 TB moved correctly? — Row counts and checksums per table computed on both sides before the freeze, plus a business-level reconciliation query the data owners agree on. “It looked fine” is not a validation.

Wrong answer: “Take a full backup, copy it to blob storage, and restore it during a maintenance window.” For 4 TB that is hours of copy plus hours of restore, and it is the offline pattern the question is designed to rule out. It is the right answer only when the business genuinely grants a long window — and the interviewer wants to see whether you check that assumption or assume it.

05Storage

Storage accounts underpin almost everything else in Azure — Functions, AKS, backups, logs, data lakes — so the questions test whether you understand redundancy, access control and cost per transaction rather than just “where do I put a file”. Screener depth is blob versus file versus disk and the redundancy acronyms; senior depth is SAS design, lifecycle policy and the throughput limits that cause real incidents; principal depth is exfiltration control, immutability for compliance, and designing a data lake nobody can accidentally make public.

Docs: Storage redundancy · Blob access tiers · Shared access signatures

Explain LRS, ZRS, GRS, GZRS and RA-GRS. Which do you pick and why? screener

Answer. All of them keep three synchronous copies; the difference is where. LRS puts them in one datacentre — cheapest, survives a disk or rack failure, does not survive a zone. ZRS spreads them across three availability zones in the region, synchronously — this is the sensible default for anything production in a multi-zone region, and the price difference over LRS is small. GRS is LRS in the primary plus asynchronous replication to a paired secondary region (RPO typically under 15 minutes, no SLA on it), where the data is inaccessible unless Microsoft or you fail over. GZRS is ZRS in the primary plus that async secondary — the strongest common option. RA- prefixes (RA-GRS, RA-GZRS) add a read-only secondary endpoint you can use immediately, at extra cost. The decision rule: ZRS or GZRS for production data; LRS for anything reproducible (build artefacts, caches, replicated logs); and never assume geo-redundancy is a disaster recovery plan on its own, because failover is account-wide, changes your redundancy to LRS afterwards, and has an RPO you do not control.

Example

DataChoiceReasoning
Customer documents, regulatedGZRS + immutability policyZone-safe now, region-safe later, WORM for audit
Application logs shipped to a workspaceLRSAlready duplicated in the workspace; cheapest
Terraform stateZRS + versioning + soft deleteSmall, critical, must survive a bad apply
ML training data, re-derivableLRS, cool tierRecomputable; optimise for storage cost
Static site assets behind a CDNZRSOrigin availability matters; egress served by CDN

Follow-ups

  • What is customer-managed failover and what does it cost you? — You can initiate a failover to the secondary; the RPO is whatever had replicated (check the Last Sync Time property), the account becomes LRS in the new primary, and you must re-enable geo-redundancy afterwards. It is a real capability with a real data-loss window, and you should have measured it before you need it.
  • Are all services available on ZRS? — Not all account types and features combine freely; premium block blob, some file tiers and some regions differ. Check the matrix rather than assuming, especially for Azure Files and for disks (which have their own ZRS variants).
  • Does redundancy protect against deletion? — No. Replication faithfully replicates a delete. Soft delete for blobs and containers, blob versioning, point-in-time restore and immutability policies are the controls against human error; redundancy is only about infrastructure failure.
  • What is the paired-region caveat? — Some newer Azure regions have no pair, and Microsoft's guidance has shifted toward availability zones plus your own multi-region design rather than relying on pairing. If your DR plan says “the paired region”, check that your region has one.

Wrong answer: “We use GRS, so we're covered for disaster recovery.” GRS gives you a copy you cannot read and cannot fail over to on your own timeline without accepting an unknown RPO, and it does nothing for the far more likely failure — someone deleting a container. Naming soft delete and versioning in the same breath is what separates a real answer.

How do you grant a third party time-limited access to one blob? Compare the SAS types. mid

Answer. A shared access signature is a signed URL granting specific permissions on specific resources for a specific window. There are three kinds. An account SAS is signed by the account key and can grant access across services — powerful, blunt, and revocable only by rotating the key. A service SAS is also key-signed but scoped to one service, and can be tied to a stored access policy on the container, which is the only way to revoke a key-signed SAS without rotating the key. A user delegation SAS is signed with a key obtained from Entra ID on behalf of a principal, so it inherits that principal's RBAC permissions, expires with the delegation key (maximum seven days), is fully attributable in logs, and — critically — works on an account where shared key access is disabled. For the question as asked, the right answer is a user delegation SAS with read-only permission, a short expiry, and ideally an IP range restriction; generated on demand by your API using its managed identity.

Example

var blob = new BlobClient(new Uri(url), new DefaultAzureCredential());
// 1. get a delegation key from Entra (needs Storage Blob Delegator on the account)
var key = await blobService.GetUserDelegationKeyAsync(
    DateTimeOffset.UtcNow, DateTimeOffset.UtcNow.AddHours(1));

var sas = new BlobSasBuilder(BlobSasPermissions.Read, DateTimeOffset.UtcNow.AddMinutes(15))
{
    BlobContainerName = "invoices", BlobName = "2026/09/INV-8842.pdf",
    Protocol = SasProtocol.Https, IPRange = new SasIPRange(IPAddress.Parse("203.0.113.7"))
};
var uri = new UriBuilder(blob.Uri)
    { Query = sas.ToSasQueryParameters(key, accountName).ToString() }.Uri;

Follow-ups

  • How do you revoke a SAS? — User delegation SAS: revoke the delegation key (immediate, account-wide) or remove the underlying RBAC. Service SAS with a stored access policy: change or delete the policy. Anything else: rotate the account key, which breaks every other consumer. That asymmetry is the whole argument for delegation SAS.
  • What expiry is reasonable? — Minutes for a download link, hours at most for an upload; long-lived SAS tokens end up in bookmarks, tickets and screenshots. Azure Policy can enforce a maximum SAS expiry interval on the account, and Defender for Storage alerts on unusual SAS use.
  • Should you disable shared key access entirely? — Yes, for anything greenfield: set allowSharedKeyAccess: false so only Entra-authorised access and user delegation SAS work. The blockers are usually old SDKs and tools that only speak connection strings — finding them is the migration, and it is a common real-world project.
  • How do you let a browser upload directly? — Issue a short-lived write-only user delegation SAS for a single blob name your API chose, with CORS configured on the account. Never hand the browser a container-scoped write SAS; that is a free file-hosting service for whoever finds it.

Wrong answer: “Generate an account SAS with a one-year expiry and give it to them; it's read-only so it's safe.” It is a bearer credential with a year's life, unrevocable without rotating the key that everything else uses, and unattributable in the logs. The single word that fixes this answer is “delegation”.

A storage account is throttling under load. What are the limits and what do you do? senior

Answer. The account is the scale unit, and it has hard ceilings: on the order of 20,000 requests per second and an ingress/egress bandwidth cap per account (region-dependent, commonly 60 Gbps egress for a standard GPv2 account), with a further per-blob limit of roughly 500 requests per second and about 60 MB/s on a single blob. Throttling appears as HTTP 503 ServerBusy or 500 OperationTimedOut, and the partition layer's key is the account+container+blob name, which is why the classic pathological pattern — sequential or timestamp-prefixed blob names — concentrates load on one partition range. The fixes, in order of effectiveness: distribute the key space (hash or random prefix in the blob name, or shard across multiple accounts), use premium block blob accounts for high-transaction low-latency work, cache reads in a CDN or Front Door so the origin sees a fraction of the traffic, and batch or aggregate small writes rather than issuing one request per event. Then implement retry with exponential backoff and jitter, because the SDK's default retry can turn a brief throttle into a self-sustaining stampede.

Example

Symptom: 503 ServerBusy at ~19k req/s on one account, egress only 4 Gbps.
Diagnosis (KQL over StorageBlobLogs):
  StorageBlobLogs
  | where StatusText has "ServerBusy"
  | summarize count() by bin(TimeGenerated, 1m), tostring(split(Uri, "/")[4])
  -> 92% of throttled requests on one container, keys all "2026-09-08T…"

Fix: blob name  "2026-09-08T12:00:01Z-evt.json"   (hot partition range)
  -> "a7f3/2026-09-08T12:00:01Z-evt.json"         (2-byte hash prefix, 65k ranges)
Plus: 4 accounts behind a consistent-hash router for headroom; premium block blob for the hot tier.

Follow-ups

  • Why does the blob name matter? — Azure Storage range-partitions on the full key, so lexicographically adjacent names land in the same partition range. A high-entropy prefix spreads them; a timestamp prefix guarantees they collide. This is the single most useful piece of storage internals in interviews.
  • Standard versus premium block blob? — Premium is SSD-backed with much lower latency and higher transaction ceilings, priced higher per GB but often cheaper for transaction-heavy small-object workloads because standard bills per 10,000 operations. Compute the blended cost, do not assume premium is more expensive.
  • How do you avoid this in a data lake? — ADLS Gen2 (hierarchical namespace) changes the partitioning model and directory operations become atomic, but per-account limits still apply; large lakes shard by account per domain or per zone (raw/curated/served) for both throughput and blast radius.
  • What does the SDK do by default? — Retries with exponential backoff on 503/500. That is right for transient throttling and wrong for sustained overload — set a retry budget and a circuit breaker so the client sheds load instead of amplifying it.

Wrong answer: “Increase the storage account's performance tier or request a quota increase.” Some limits are raisable by support, but the request-rate ceiling is architectural and a quota ticket will not fix a hot partition — you will hit the same wall a week later. The interviewer is looking for the key-distribution insight.

How do you manage blob lifecycle and access tiers to control cost? mid

Answer. Four tiers, trading storage price against access price and latency: Hot (highest storage cost, lowest transaction cost), Cool (30-day minimum retention, cheaper storage, more expensive reads), Cold (90-day minimum, cheaper still), and Archive (offline, cheapest storage, 180-day minimum, and a rehydration of hours — standard priority up to 15 hours, high priority typically under an hour for smaller blobs). You automate movement with a lifecycle management policy: JSON rules matching a prefix or blob index tags, moving blobs between tiers or deleting them after N days since creation, last modification, or last access (last-access tracking must be enabled and adds cost of its own). The traps that make this a real question rather than a documentation lookup are the early-deletion charges — deleting or re-tiering a Cool blob before 30 days bills you the remainder — and the fact that rehydration from Archive is not a setting you can flip in an incident. So the design question is always “what is the recovery expectation for this data”, and Archive is only correct when the answer is “hours are fine”.

Example

{ "rules": [{
  "name": "invoices-tiering", "enabled": true,
  "type": "Lifecycle",
  "definition": {
    "filters": { "blobTypes": [ "blockBlob" ], "prefixMatch": [ "invoices/" ] },
    "actions": { "baseBlob": {
        "tierToCool":    { "daysAfterModificationGreaterThan": 30 },
        "tierToCold":    { "daysAfterModificationGreaterThan": 120 },
        "tierToArchive": { "daysAfterModificationGreaterThan": 365 },
        "delete":        { "daysAfterModificationGreaterThan": 2557 } },   // 7y retention
      "snapshot":   { "delete": { "daysAfterCreationGreaterThan": 90 } },
      "version":    { "delete": { "daysAfterCreationGreaterThan": 90 } } }
  } }] }

The snapshot and version rules are the ones people forget, and they are frequently the majority of the bill on an account with versioning enabled.

Follow-ups

  • When does tiering cost you money? — Moving to a cooler tier is a write operation, and reading from a cool tier costs more per operation. For small, frequently-read blobs, Cool can be more expensive overall than Hot. Model it on your actual read pattern.
  • How do you find what is actually costing you? — Storage insights plus a blob inventory report (a scheduled CSV/Parquet of every blob with size, tier and last-access) queried in a notebook or Fabric. Guessing at a multi-hundred-terabyte account is how you optimise the wrong container.
  • Archive versus deleting? — If nobody can name the scenario in which the data is read, retention policy — not tiering — is the conversation. Archiving data you will never read is a cheaper version of the wrong decision.
  • What about immutable storage? — Time-based retention or legal-hold policies (WORM) at container or version level, optionally locked so even an account owner cannot shorten them. That is how you satisfy financial-services record-keeping, and it interacts with lifecycle rules: a delete action cannot remove a blob under retention.

Wrong answer: “Put everything in Archive after 30 days — storage is basically free there.” It ignores the 180-day minimum (early deletion is billed in full), the rehydration latency, and the per-operation cost of getting data back. The first incident where someone needs a document restored “now” ends this policy.

Azure Files, Blob, NetApp Files or managed disks — which for what? mid

Answer. By access protocol and semantics, not by size. Blob is object storage over HTTP: massive scale, cheap, versioned, no file-system semantics — right for anything your application accesses through an SDK. Azure Files is a managed SMB/NFS share: right for lift-and-shift where the application expects a mounted drive, for shared configuration, and for Windows workloads with Entra/AD-based ACLs; the premium tier gives provisioned IOPS, the standard tier is transaction-billed. Azure NetApp Files is the high-end NFS/SMB option — sub-millisecond latency, very high throughput, snapshots and cross-region replication — for workloads like SAP HANA, HPC scratch, or a large EDA/render farm, at a much higher price and with capacity pools you buy in bulk. Managed disks are block storage attached to one VM (or shared, for clustering): Premium SSD v2 is the modern default because IOPS, throughput and size are independently configurable rather than tied to a disk size tier; Ultra Disk for the extreme end. The interview trap is a candidate who reaches for Files because the app “needs a file system” without asking whether a small refactor to blob would remove a whole class of latency and cost.

Example

WorkloadStorageWhy
User-uploaded images served via CDNBlob (hot) HTTP-native, cheap, CDN-friendly
Legacy app writing to \\share\inAzure Files premiumSMB with no code change; provisioned IOPS
SQL Server on a VM, 20k IOPSPremium SSD v2IOPS decoupled from capacity; cheaper than P80
SAP HANA data volumeNetApp Files (Ultra)Sub-ms latency, certified, snapshots
AKS pods needing RWXAzure Files (NFS or SMB CSI)ReadWriteMany; disks are RWO only

Follow-ups

  • Why is Premium SSD v2 the default now? — On Premium SSD v1 you bought a size tier (P30, P40…) to get IOPS, so people over-provisioned capacity to buy performance. v2 lets you set capacity, IOPS and throughput independently, which is usually both faster and cheaper. It does have caveats — no host caching, and availability-set/zone constraints — worth naming.
  • ReadWriteMany in Kubernetes? — Disks are ReadWriteOnce; if multiple pods need the same volume you need Azure Files (or NetApp/Blob CSI). Candidates hit this the first time they scale a Deployment with a disk PVC and wonder why the second pod is stuck pending.
  • What about Azure Files identity? — SMB shares support Entra Kerberos or on-prem AD DS for NTFS ACLs, which is what makes them a genuine drop-in for a file server. Storage-key-based mounting is the legacy fallback and worth eliminating for the same reasons as any shared key.
  • Where does Azure File Sync fit? — Tiering an on-prem Windows file server into Azure Files with local caching: the standard answer for “we want the cloud copy but the branch office needs LAN speed”, and a common migration stepping stone.

Wrong answer: “Use Azure Files — it's just blob storage with a file interface, so it's the same thing with more convenience.” They have different scale limits, different pricing models, and different consistency and latency behaviour; SMB round trips over a network are not free, and a chatty file-based integration that was fine on a local disk can become the bottleneck.

How do you stop data exfiltration from a storage account? senior

Answer. Layer four controls, because no single one is sufficient. Identity: disable shared key access so there is no account key to steal, use Entra RBAC with data-plane roles scoped to containers, and prefer user delegation SAS with short expiries — optionally enforcing a maximum SAS expiry with policy. Network: publicNetworkAccess: Disabled plus private endpoints for each sub-resource, so the account is unreachable from the internet at all; where full private is impossible, network rules limited to specific VNets and a small IP allowlist, with the “allow trusted Microsoft services” exception understood rather than blindly ticked. Egress: the more subtle direction — a compromised workload copying data to an attacker's storage account. Control that with firewall FQDN rules narrowed to your own accounts, and with the storage-specific exfiltration protections in Azure Firewall Premium. Detection: Defender for Storage (malware scanning and sensitive data discovery), diagnostic logs to a workspace, and alerts on anomalous download volume, first-time principals, and SAS usage from new IPs. Then prove it: an access review of who holds data-plane roles, and a quarterly test that the public endpoint really is unreachable.

Example

resource sa 'Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts@2023-05-01' = {
  name: 'stordersprodweu'
  sku: { name: 'Standard_ZRS' }
  kind: 'StorageV2'
  properties: {
    allowSharedKeyAccess: false          // no account keys at all
    allowBlobPublicAccess: false         // no anonymous containers, ever
    publicNetworkAccess: 'Disabled'      // private endpoints only
    minimumTlsVersion: 'TLS1_2'
    defaultToOAuthAuthentication: true
    sasPolicy: { sasExpirationPeriod: '0.01:00:00', expirationAction: 'Log' }
    networkAcls: { defaultAction: 'Deny', bypass: 'None' }
  }
}

Follow-ups

  • What does bypass: AzureServices actually allow? — A set of first-party services to reach the account regardless of network rules, subject to their own identity. It is convenient and it is a hole; prefer resource instance rules (allow this specific Data Factory or Synapse workspace) which are far narrower.
  • How do you find accounts with anonymous access today? — Azure Resource Graph across the estate: query for allowBlobPublicAccess true and for containers with public access level set. Then enforce with a policy deny so no new one appears; the classic breach story starts with one forgotten container.
  • Does immutability help? — Against destruction, not against reading. Time-based retention with a locked policy protects against ransomware and insider deletion, which is a different threat from exfiltration — name both separately rather than conflating them.
  • What is the cost of full private? — Private endpoints per sub-resource per VNet, DNS zone management, and broken developer workflows (nobody can browse the account from a laptop). Solve the last one with Bastion or a jump path, not by re-enabling public access “temporarily”.

Wrong answer: “We turn on the storage firewall and allow the office IP range.” It stops nothing that runs inside your own network, does nothing about egress, and quietly breaks when someone works from home — at which point the range gets widened. Firewall rules are one layer of four, and alone they are theatre.

Design storage for a multi-tenant SaaS holding customer documents. principal

Answer. Start from the isolation requirement, because it decides everything else. If tenants merely need logical separation, use one account (or a sharded set) with a container per tenant, an Entra-authorised API in front, and per-request user delegation SAS scoped to a single blob — tenants never touch storage directly. If a tenant contract requires customer-managed keys, a distinct data residency, or provable physical separation, you need an account per tenant, which brings a subscription-limit and automation problem (accounts per subscription, private endpoint counts, provisioning time) and therefore a vending pipeline. Between those, a hybrid: a shared account for the long tail, dedicated accounts for enterprise tenants, with the tenant record holding the account name so routing is data, not code. Regardless of shape: encrypt with CMK per tenant where required (an encryption scope lets you do per-container keys inside one account, which is the underrated middle option), enable versioning and soft delete, set an immutability policy where the contract demands retention, log every data-plane access with the tenant id, and design deletion up front — “delete all of tenant X” is a contractual obligation and it is much harder if their data is interleaved.

Example

Shared tier (long tail, ~2,000 tenants)
  storage account: stdocssharedweu01..04   (sharded by hash(tenantId) % 4)
  container per tenant: t-{tenantId}       (delete container == delete tenant)
  encryption scope per tenant where CMK is contracted
Dedicated tier (enterprise, ~40 tenants)
  storage account per tenant, own CMK in the tenant's own Key Vault, own private endpoint
  provisioned by the same pipeline that vends the tenant record
Access path (both tiers)
  client -> API (Entra, tenant claim) -> authorise -> mint 5-min delegation SAS for ONE blob
  client never holds a container-scoped credential; every mint is logged with tenantId + oid

Follow-ups

  • Why not a container per tenant in one account forever? — Account-level request and bandwidth limits are shared, so one heavy tenant throttles the rest; and per-account limits on containers, encryption scopes and private endpoints eventually bind. Sharding early is much cheaper than resharding later.
  • How do you prove tenant isolation to a security reviewer? — The authorisation test suite: replay tenant A's requests with tenant B's token and assert 403 for every route, run in CI. A design diagram proves nothing; a failing test in the pipeline does.
  • What does per-tenant CMK actually buy? — Crypto-shredding (destroy the key, the data is unreadable) and a contractual control the tenant can revoke. The cost is key lifecycle management, and the operational hazard is a tenant disabling their key and taking their own service down — which needs monitoring and a documented response.
  • How do you handle a data-residency requirement? — Account per region with the tenant record naming the region, and a routing layer that never falls back across borders. The hard part is the secondary systems — logs, backups, search indexes, telemetry — which is where residency programmes actually fail.

Wrong answer: “Give each tenant their own SAS URL to their container and let their client talk to storage directly.” It removes your authorisation layer, makes revocation slow, puts a long-lived credential in a client you do not control, and destroys per-request auditing. Direct-to-storage is fine for a single blob with a short-lived, narrowly-scoped token minted per request — the container-scoped version is the mistake.

06Resource organisation & governance

Governance questions are how interviewers find out whether you have run an estate or only built in one. Screener depth is knowing what a resource group and a tag are; senior depth is Azure Policy effects, remediation and the rollout of a control without breaking teams; principal depth is landing zones, subscription vending, and the political question of how you enforce standards on people who do not report to you. Expect at least one “how would you stop this happening again” question, which is always really a policy question.

Docs: Azure Policy · Azure landing zones · Azure Resource Graph

What belongs in a resource group, and what does the boundary actually give you? screener

Answer. A resource group is a lifecycle and management boundary: everything in it should be deployed, updated and deleted together, because deleting the group deletes the contents. It gives you a scope for RBAC, for policy assignment, for locks and for deployments, and a cheap way to answer “what is this system made of”. It gives you almost nothing else — it is not a security boundary in the network sense, not a billing boundary (subscriptions and tags are), and resources in it can freely reference resources anywhere else in the tenant. The location of a resource group only stores its own metadata; resources in it can live in other regions, though for resilience you generally want the group's metadata region to be one you can still reach. The practical rule interviewers want: one resource group per workload per environment, plus separate groups for things with a different lifetime — the network, the shared data store that outlives the app, and anything you would be horrified to delete with the app.

Example

rg-orders-prod-weu          app service plan, apps, app insights, config      (redeployable)
rg-orders-data-prod-weu     SQL server + db, storage, key vault               (survives redeploys)
rg-orders-net-prod-weu      private endpoints, NICs, DNS records              (owned by platform)
rg-platform-connectivity    hub vnet, firewall, gateway, private dns zones    (different owner entirely)

The test: "if I delete this resource group at 2am, what is unrecoverable?"
If the answer is "customer data", it is in the wrong group.

Follow-ups

  • Can you move a resource between groups or subscriptions? — Often, not always: many resource types are unsupported or have conditions, the operation locks both groups while it runs, and the resource ID changes, which breaks anything referencing it — policy exemptions, role assignments at resource scope, monitoring queries. Treat a move as a small migration, not a rename.
  • What does a resource lock do? — CanNotDelete or ReadOnly at group or resource scope, inherited downward, and it applies to everyone including Owners. It is the standard guard on production data resources; the gotcha is that ReadOnly blocks legitimate operations that are implemented as writes (listing keys, scaling), so it causes more surprises than CanNotDelete.
  • How does this interact with IaC? — Deployment scope is usually the resource group, so the group boundary is also your deployment blast radius. Deployment Stacks add denySettings, which is the modern way to protect stack-managed resources from out-of-band edits — a deny assignment you do not have to hand-craft.
  • What about the region of the group? — It holds the group's metadata only. In a regional outage you may be unable to manage resources whose group metadata lives in the failed region even if the resources are elsewhere — a genuine, if rare, argument for putting platform groups in your most stable region.

Wrong answer: “We put everything for a team in one resource group so it's easy to find.” It conflates ownership with lifecycle, and it means the database and the stateless app share a delete blast radius and a lock policy. The follow-up — “what happens when you tear down and redeploy the app?” — is where it falls apart.

Explain Azure Policy effects and how you would roll out a new control safely. mid

Answer. Policy evaluates resources at ARM request time and periodically thereafter, and the effect decides what happens. Audit and AuditIfNotExists record non-compliance without blocking, Deny rejects the request outright, Modify alters the request (adding or removing tags, setting a property), DeployIfNotExists runs a remediation template when a related resource is missing — the effect behind “every resource must have diagnostic settings” — Manual records an attestation for controls that are not machine-checkable, and Disabled turns a definition off without deleting the assignment. The rollout sequence is what interviewers actually care about: assign as Audit at the target scope, look at the compliance report to size the existing violation set, communicate with owners and give a deadline, remediate the existing estate (remediation tasks for Modify/DeployIfNotExists need a managed identity with the right role), and only then flip to Deny — with exemptions, not exclusions, for the cases that genuinely cannot comply, because an exemption has an expiry date and a reason attached.

Example

{
  "if": { "allOf": [
    { "field": "type", "equals": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts" },
    { "field": "Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/allowBlobPublicAccess", "notEquals": false }
  ]},
  "then": { "effect": "[parameters('effect')]" }     // Audit today, Deny in 30 days
}
# size the problem before you break anyone
az policy state summarize --policy-assignment no-public-blob \
  --query "value[0].results.nonCompliantResources"
# remediate what Modify/DeployIfNotExists can fix, with the assignment's identity
az policy remediation create -n fix-diag --policy-assignment require-diag-settings

Follow-ups

  • Exclusion or exemption? — An exclusion removes a scope from the assignment permanently and invisibly; an exemption is a first-class object with a category (waiver or mitigated), an optional expiry, and a description. Always exemption: it shows up in compliance reporting and it expires.
  • Why did my Deny policy not stop an existing resource? — Deny applies to requests. Pre-existing resources show as non-compliant but keep running; they only fail on the next write. That is a feature (no instant outage) and a trap (you think you are protected and you are not).
  • How do you handle initiative sprawl? — Group definitions into initiatives (policy sets) aligned to a control framework, assign at management-group scope with parameters per environment, and version them. Fifty individual assignments at subscription scope is unmanageable and impossible to audit.
  • What is the evaluation delay? — Roughly 30 minutes for a new or changed assignment to take effect, and a compliance scan every 24 hours otherwise (you can trigger one). So “I assigned it and nothing happened” is usually impatience, not a bug.

Wrong answer: “Assign the deny policies at the root management group so everything is compliant from day one.” You will break every existing deployment simultaneously, including the platform's own, and the political fallout ends the governance programme. Audit-first is not timidity; it is the only way to learn what your estate actually looks like.

Design a tagging strategy. What do you tag, and how do you make it stick? mid

Answer. Tag for the questions you will be asked, and keep the mandatory set small — five or six at most, because every additional required tag lowers compliance. The usual set: owner (a team alias, never a person), costCentre, environment, application (or service ID from a catalogue), dataClassification, and criticality. Making it stick is a three-part mechanism, not a policy document: Modify policies to inherit tags from the resource group or subscription so most resources get them without anyone typing anything, Deny on the handful that must be explicit, and reporting that makes the gap visible — a weekly Resource Graph query of untagged spend sent to the owners. Then close the loop with cost: if untagged resources land in an “unallocated” bucket that someone is accountable for, tags get fixed. The reason tagging comes up in interviews is that it is the cheapest test of whether a candidate has tried to run cost allocation across teams — everybody agrees tags matter and almost nobody achieves compliance without automation.

Example

// Resource Graph: what is untagged, and who is nearest to responsible?
resources
| where isnull(tags['costCentre']) or tags['costCentre'] == ''
| extend rg = tostring(split(id, '/')[4]), sub = subscriptionId
| summarize resources = count() by sub, rg, type
| order by resources desc
| take 50
// inherit rather than demand: Modify effect, remediated across the estate
{ "if": { "field": "tags['costCentre']", "exists": "false" },
  "then": { "effect": "modify", "details": {
    "roleDefinitionIds": [ "/providers/microsoft.authorization/roleDefinitions/<contributor>" ],
    "operations": [ { "operation": "add", "field": "tags['costCentre']",
                      "value": "[resourceGroup().tags['costCentre']]" } ] } } }

Follow-ups

  • Which resources cannot be tagged? — Some resource types and all child resources of certain services do not support tags, and tags do not flow to every cost record. So tags are a good allocation tool and a bad isolation tool — never rely on them for security or for exact chargeback of shared services.
  • How do you allocate shared costs? — Tags cannot split a shared AKS cluster or a hub firewall. Use a documented allocation rule (by request count, by node hours via OpenCost/Kubecost-style tooling, or a flat platform charge) and publish it; arguing about shared cost is a recurring FinOps conversation you should pre-empt.
  • Case sensitivity? — Tag names are case-insensitive for retrieval but stored as written, and values are case-sensitive; Prod and prod become two lines in a cost report. Enforce an allowed-values policy, not just presence.
  • What is the alternative to tags for grouping? — Subscription and resource group structure, which is enforceable and cannot be mistyped. The strong answer uses structure for the coarse dimensions (environment, business unit) and tags for the fine ones.

Wrong answer: “We require twelve tags on every resource, enforced with a deny policy.” Deployments start failing on tag typos, teams add junk values to get past the gate, and your data is worse than if you had asked for four. Fewer mandatory tags with inheritance and reporting beats a long list with a hard gate every time.

What is an Azure landing zone, and would you adopt the accelerator or build your own? senior

Answer. A landing zone is the pre-built, policy-governed environment a workload lands in: management group hierarchy, subscription topology, identity and RBAC model, networking (hub, DNS, egress), management and monitoring (workspaces, diagnostic settings, backup), and the guardrails expressed as policy. Microsoft's Azure landing zone accelerator ships this as code — and since January 2026 the Bicep flavour is GA as a set of Azure Verified Modules: a starter module composed of roughly nineteen AVM resource and pattern modules, deployed and lifecycle-managed with Deployment Stacks so removing a resource from the template actually removes it. The classic ALZ-Bicep repo was archived on 16 February 2026 with limited support for a further year, so “we use ALZ-Bicep” is now a migration statement. My default is to adopt the accelerator and diverge deliberately: you get a reviewed policy baseline and a structure every Azure consultant will recognise, and the cost is learning someone else's abstractions. Build your own only when you have a genuinely unusual constraint — and even then, steal the policy set.

Example

What the accelerator gives you on day one
  MG tree: root / platform (identity, management, connectivity) / landing zones (corp, online)
           / sandbox / decommissioned
  ~150 policy definitions + initiatives mapped to common control frameworks
  hub network (or Virtual WAN), firewall, private DNS zones, Bastion
  Log Analytics workspace + diagnostic settings policies + Defender plans
  deployed as AVM modules under a Deployment Stack (denySettings protects the platform)

What you still own
  subscription vending, workload landing-zone templates, cost model, the exception process

Follow-ups

  • What is the risk of adopting it wholesale? — Policies you did not read blocking teams on day two, and a management-group tree that does not match how your company is organised. Deploy the structure, run the policies in Audit, and turn on Deny in tranches.
  • Bicep or Terraform for the landing zone? — Both are supported by AVM. Bicep is first-party, has no state file to protect, and Deployment Stacks now give it lifecycle deletion; Terraform has a stronger multi-cloud and module ecosystem and an existing skills base in many teams. Pick the one your platform team will actually maintain, and say that reason out loud.
  • How do workloads get into it? — Subscription vending: a pipeline that creates the subscription, places it in the right management group, applies budget, peers the network, links DNS zones, and grants the team's group. Without vending, the landing zone decays within two quarters.
  • What if the company already has 60 ungoverned subscriptions? — Do not big-bang. Create the management group tree, move subscriptions in with Audit-only policy, publish a compliance scorecard per team, and enforce as each team remediates. Governance retrofits succeed by making the gap visible, not by blocking.

Wrong answer: “Landing zones are a Microsoft marketing framework; we just create subscriptions as needed and apply policies later.” “Later” means retrofitting network, identity and logging into running production, which is the most expensive way to do all three. The interviewer is checking whether you have paid that bill.

How do you answer “what do we have running, and is it compliant?” across 60 subscriptions? senior

Answer. Azure Resource Graph, not the portal and not a script that loops over subscriptions. Resource Graph is a Kusto-queryable index of every ARM resource in every subscription you can read, updated continuously, and it answers estate-wide questions in seconds where an ARM enumeration would take an hour and hit throttling. Pair it with three other feeds: Policy compliance state (also queryable in Resource Graph, via policyresources) for the “is it compliant” half, Cost Management exports for the money, and Advisor plus the Service Retirement workbook for the “what is about to break” half — the last of which is how you find the Basic public IPs, the Application Gateway v1s and the unsupported Kubernetes versions before they turn into an incident. Then make it a product, not a query: a scheduled export into a workspace or a Fabric/Power BI model, a per-team scorecard, and alerts on the deltas that matter. The lead-level point is that governance without a feedback loop is a document; the loop is what changes behaviour.

Example

// every public IP still on the retired Basic SKU, with owner tag, across the tenant
resources
| where type =~ 'microsoft.network/publicipaddresses'
| where sku.name =~ 'Basic'
| project name, resourceGroup, subscriptionId, location,
          owner = tostring(tags['owner']), attachedTo = properties.ipConfiguration.id
| join kind=leftouter (resourcecontainers
    | where type == 'microsoft.resources/subscriptions'
    | project subscriptionId, subName = name) on subscriptionId
| order by subName asc

// compliance in the same language
policyresources
| where type == 'microsoft.policyinsights/policystates'
| where properties.complianceState == 'NonCompliant'
| summarize count() by tostring(properties.policyDefinitionName), subscriptionId

Follow-ups

  • What are Resource Graph's limits? — It indexes ARM properties, not data-plane state: it knows a storage account exists and its configuration, not what is in it. It also has query throttling and paging limits, and some properties lag by a minute or two.
  • How do you track retirements systematically? — Subscribe to Azure Service Health planned-maintenance and health advisories with an action group, and run the Service Retirement workbook monthly. In 2026 alone the estate-relevant list includes Application Gateway v1 (retired 28 April 2026), Azure Front Door classic (31 March 2027), and a batch of AI services retiring 1 October 2026 — none of which announce themselves in your monitoring.
  • How do you make teams act on the report? — Route findings to the owning team's own backlog with a due date derived from the retirement date, and escalate on age rather than severity alone. A central list nobody owns is where compliance goes to die.
  • What about resources outside ARM? — Entra objects, Microsoft 365, and data-plane contents need their own inventory (Graph API, Purview). Say so, because “Resource Graph shows everything” is not true and the gap is usually identity.

Wrong answer: “We run a nightly PowerShell script that loops through subscriptions and exports to CSV.” It is slow, it throttles, it silently misses subscriptions the script's identity cannot read, and nobody reads the CSV. It also signals unfamiliarity with the one Azure tool built exactly for this.

A team deployed a public-facing VM with RDP open to the internet. How do you make sure it cannot happen again? senior

Answer. Fix it in layers, from the fastest to the most durable, and say which layer each control belongs to.
