Immediate: close the NSG rule, check Defender for Cloud and the sign-in logs for exploitation, rotate anything exposed. Preventive: an Azure Policy deny at management-group scope on NSG rules allowing 3389/22 from Internet or *, plus a deny on public IPs attached to NICs in the corp landing zone — that is the control that makes the class impossible rather than the instance fixed. Alternative path: give the team something better, because a control with no replacement gets circumvented — Azure Bastion for browser-based RDP/SSH with no public IP, or just-in-time VM access in Defender for Cloud where a port opens for a bounded window with an approval. Detective: Defender for Cloud recommendations and a Resource Graph query in the weekly scorecard to catch anything the policy cannot see. Cultural: put the guardrail in the workload template so the paved road is the compliant one, and make the exception process fast enough that people use it instead of a workaround.

Example

{ "if": { "allOf": [
    { "field": "type", "equals": "Microsoft.Network/networkSecurityGroups/securityRules" },
    { "field": "Microsoft.Network/networkSecurityGroups/securityRules/access", "equals": "Allow" },
    { "field": "Microsoft.Network/networkSecurityGroups/securityRules/direction", "equals": "Inbound" },
    { "anyOf": [
      { "field": "Microsoft.Network/networkSecurityGroups/securityRules/sourceAddressPrefix",
        "in": [ "*", "Internet", "0.0.0.0/0", "/0" ] } ] },
    { "value": "[if(contains(field('Microsoft.Network/networkSecurityGroups/securityRules/destinationPortRange'), '3389'), 'yes', 'no')]",
      "equals": "yes" }
  ]},
  "then": { "effect": "deny" } }

Follow-ups

  • Why is a policy better than a code review? — Review catches what someone looks at; policy catches what someone does, including portal clicks at 23:00 during an incident. Both, but the enforceable one is policy.
  • What does Bastion cost and when is it not worth it? — It is an hourly-billed resource per VNet (Developer and Basic SKUs lowered the entry point). For a handful of dev VMs the honest alternative may be a private-only VM reached over the VPN — the point is that some paved path must exist.
  • How do you handle the legitimate exception? — A time-boxed policy exemption with a named owner, an expiry, and a linked compensating control (source IP restriction, JIT). Exemptions with expiries convert “we'll fix it later” into a calendar entry.
  • What if the team pushes back on the deny? — Show the data: the number of exposed hosts across the estate, the internet-wide scan rate for 3389, and the incident cost. Governance arguments are won with evidence, not authority — and the paved-road alternative is what converts opposition into adoption.

Wrong answer: “Send an email reminding everyone of the standard and add it to the onboarding wiki.” It will happen again next quarter, with a different team. Every governance question is really asking “do you know the difference between a rule and a control”.

How do you build a workload landing zone that teams actually want to use? principal

Answer. Treat the platform as a product with users who can leave. That means three commitments. Fast: a team asks for an environment and gets one the same day, from a self-service pipeline — subscription created, placed, budgeted, networked, DNS linked, RBAC granted, observability wired, and a starter repository with working IaC and CI. If it takes two weeks and a ticket, teams route around you into a shadow subscription. Opinionated but escapable: a paved road that makes the compliant thing the easy thing (modules that default to private endpoints, zone redundancy, diagnostics on), with a documented, time-boxed exception process for the cases it does not fit — controls people cannot escape get subverted, controls with a visible exit get respected. Accountable: publish SLOs for the platform itself (vending time, policy exception turnaround, platform incident response), measure adoption, and run the equivalent of user research with the teams. The principal-level insight is that governance and developer experience are the same programme: every guardrail you add must be paid for with friction you remove somewhere else.

Example

Self-service request (a PR to the platform repo, reviewed by policy-as-code, not by a human queue)
  workload: orders   env: prod   region: weu   criticality: 2   dataClass: confidential
Pipeline does:
  1. create subscription, place under MG landingzones/corp
  2. apply budget EUR 8k + alerts at 60/80/100%, tags from the request
  3. peer to hub, link private DNS zones, apply UDR to firewall
  4. deploy Log Analytics diagnostic settings policy, Defender plans
  5. create rg-orders-prod-weu, grant grp-orders-engineers Contributor there only
  6. open a PR on the team repo with a working Bicep skeleton + CI using federated identity
Platform SLO: 4 business hours from merge to handover; exception decisions within 2 business days.

Follow-ups

  • How do you measure whether it is working? — Time-to-first-deploy for a new service, percentage of workloads on the paved road, number of standing policy exemptions, and the platform's own incident count. If shadow IT is growing, the platform is failing regardless of its compliance score.
  • What do you centralise and what do you federate? — Centralise what is genuinely shared and dangerous (network, identity, policy, egress, logging destination). Federate everything inside the workload's own resource group. The failure mode of over-centralising is a platform team that becomes a ticket queue.
  • How do you evolve the guardrails without breaking everyone? — Version the modules and the policy initiative, announce changes with a deprecation window, roll out in Audit first, and provide an automated migration where possible. Treat your internal platform's API compatibility as seriously as a public one.
  • What is the hardest part? — Not the technology; it is agreeing who is accountable when a guardrail blocks a revenue-critical launch. Decide that escalation path in advance, in writing, with a named decision-maker — otherwise it is decided at 22:00 by whoever shouts loudest.

Wrong answer: “We enforce standards centrally and teams must request changes through the platform team.” It is a description of a bottleneck, and interviewers at lead level have all watched one form. The answer they are listening for treats the developer as the customer and the guardrail as the cost.

07Reliability, zones & DR

Reliability questions separate people who have read the SLA from people who have been on call during a zone incident. Screener depth is region, zone and SLA; senior depth is designing for a specific RTO/RPO with the failover mechanism named per service; principal depth is deciding how much availability is worth buying, running the drill, and the organisational question of who declares a failover. This is also where interviewers most often ask you to defend a number.

Docs: Availability zones · Azure reliability · WAF reliability pillar

Region, availability zone, availability set, update and fault domain — define them and say which you use. screener

Answer. A region is a set of datacentres within a metro; an availability zone is one or more physically separate datacentres within that region with independent power, cooling and networking, and typically under 2 ms of latency between zones. Fault domains are racks sharing power and network within a datacentre; update domains are groups the platform reboots separately during planned maintenance. An availability set spreads VMs across fault and update domains within a single zone — so it protects against a rack failure and a maintenance reboot, but not a zone outage. Modern practice: use availability zones, and for VMs use a Virtual Machine Scale Set in Flexible orchestration mode spread across zones, which supersedes availability sets entirely (Flex gives you zone spread, per-instance control, mixed sizes and fault-domain spread in one construct). Availability sets are legacy; you will meet them in existing estates, and the migration to VMSS Flex is a real modernisation item.

Example

resource vmss 'Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachineScaleSets@2024-07-01' = {
  name: 'vmss-api'
  location: 'westeurope'
  zones: [ '1', '2', '3' ]                      // zone spread, not just a set
  properties: {
    orchestrationMode: 'Flexible'               // Flex, not Uniform, for new workloads
    platformFaultDomainCount: 1                 // 1 = max spread across zones
    zoneBalance: true                           // fail deployment rather than pile into one zone
  }
}

Follow-ups

  • What is the SLA difference? — Historically: ~99.9% single VM with premium storage, ~99.95% availability set, ~99.99% across zones. The exact numbers move, but the shape is what matters — each step buys one more nine and costs an extra instance.
  • Are zone numbers the same for everyone? — No. Logical zone 1 in your subscription may be a different physical zone from someone else's, which is why Microsoft added zone peering IDs. It matters when you coordinate with a partner or compare incident reports.
  • Are all services zone-capable? — No, and it varies by SKU. Azure Firewall now deploys zone-redundant by default in multi-zone regions and existing non-zonal deployments are being migrated through 2026; App Service supports zone redundancy on Premium plans; some services are zonal (pinned to one zone) rather than zone-redundant, which is a very different guarantee. Check per service.
  • What about cross-zone data charges? — Inter-zone bandwidth within a region is billed in some cases and is not free by default in the way intra-zone traffic is; for very chatty east-west workloads it is a real, if usually small, cost. Do not let it drive you to a single zone.

Wrong answer: “We put the VMs in an availability set, so we're covered for a datacentre failure.” An availability set lives inside one zone; a zone-level power or network failure takes out the entire set. This is the most common reliability misconception in Azure interviews, and it dates back to designs written before zones existed in most regions.

How do you compute a composite SLA, and what do you do with the number? mid

Answer. For components in series — every one must work for a request to succeed — multiply the individual SLAs; for redundant components in parallel, the combined unavailability is the product of the individual unavailabilities. So a request path of Front Door (99.99%), App Service (99.95%), SQL (99.99%) and Key Vault (99.99%) is 0.9999 × 0.9995 × 0.9999 × 0.9999 ≈ 99.92%, or roughly 35 minutes of allowed downtime a month — noticeably worse than any single component. Two things follow. First, adding dependencies to the hot path costs availability, which is a concrete argument for caching a Key Vault secret rather than fetching it per request, or for degrading gracefully when a non-essential service is down. Second, the SLA is a financial commitment about a credit, not a prediction of your uptime: your actual availability is dominated by your own deployments and code, which no SLA covers. Use the composite number to find the weakest link and to sanity-check an SLO you are about to promise — not to promise it.

Example

Series (all must work):
  Front Door 99.99  x  App Service 99.95  x  Azure SQL 99.99  x  Key Vault 99.99
  = 0.9999 x 0.9995 x 0.9999 x 0.9999 = 0.99920  -> 99.92%  ~ 35 min/month

Remove Key Vault from the hot path (cache the secret for its lifetime):
  = 0.9999 x 0.9995 x 0.9999 = 0.99930           -> 99.93%  ~ 30 min/month

Parallel (either works), same service in two regions:
  unavailability = (1 - 0.9995)^2 = 0.00000025   -> 99.999975% for that tier alone
  ...but only if failover actually works, which the SLA does not measure.

Follow-ups

  • Why is the parallel number misleading? — It assumes independent failures and instantaneous, correct failover. In practice failures correlate (a bad deployment, a shared dependency, a DNS mistake) and failover is the thing that has never been tested. Quote it, then discount it heavily.
  • What is the difference between an SLA and an SLO? — The SLA is Microsoft's contractual commitment with a service credit; the SLO is the target you set for your users and measure yourself, usually stricter and based on user-visible success rate. Error budgets come from the SLO, not the SLA.
  • Where do deployments fit? — They are typically the largest single source of downtime, and no SLA covers them. That is why the reliability conversation always ends up at deployment strategy — slots, canaries, and fast rollback.
  • Do SLAs still require multi-instance configurations? — Yes for several services: a single-instance App Service plan or a single VM without premium storage does not attract the higher SLA at all. Read the SLA's conditions, because the headline number usually assumes a configuration you have to pay for.

Wrong answer: “All our services are 99.99%, so the system is 99.99%.” Series composition always lowers it, and the interviewer will hand you the multiplication if you do not do it yourself. Getting this wrong reads as never having been asked to justify an availability target to a business stakeholder.

Design a multi-region deployment with an RTO of 15 minutes and an RPO of 1 minute. senior

Answer. Those numbers rule out backup-and-restore and rule in continuous replication with warm standby. Concretely: active-passive with a warm secondary. Traffic enters through Azure Front Door (or Traffic Manager for non-HTTP) with health probes and priority routing. The secondary region runs the full application stack, scaled down but deployed by the same pipeline on every release, so it is never stale. Data: Azure SQL failover group (async, RPO ~5 s, RTO ~30 s) or Cosmos DB with a second region; storage as GZRS or, better, application-level replication for anything you must control; caches and search indexes rebuilt or replicated depending on how long they take. Configuration and secrets replicated (Key Vault has its own regional failover story, but you should have the secondary's own vault). The RTO of 15 minutes is then dominated not by technology but by decision time — so the design must include an explicit trigger (automatic on health probe failure, or a named on-call role authorised to declare), a runbook, and rehearsals. RPO of 1 minute means asynchronous replication is acceptable but you must measure replication lag continuously and alert when it exceeds the budget.

Example

                     Azure Front Door (health probe /health/ready every 30s)
                     priority 1: weu        priority 2: neu
weu (active)                                neu (warm)
  App Service P2v4 x6, zone-redundant         App Service P2v4 x2, zone-redundant
  Azure SQL BC, zone-redundant  ---failover group (async, lag < 5s)---> readable secondary
  Cosmos: write region weu, read region neu (single write region, session consistency)
  Storage GZRS + app-level copy of the must-have container
  Key Vault weu  + Key Vault neu (same secrets, deployed by pipeline)
RTO budget:  detect 1 min | decide 4 min | SQL failover 1 min | scale out 5 min | verify 4 min = 15
RPO budget:  SQL failover group lag alert at 30s; Cosmos session token loss accepted
Rehearsal:   quarterly, in business hours, with real traffic shifted - measured, not simulated.

Follow-ups

  • Why not active-active? — Active-active removes the failover step but requires either multi-region writes with conflict resolution or partitioning users by region, plus double the running cost. It is the right answer when RTO must be near zero or when latency demands local writes; here 15 minutes is generous enough that warm standby is the cheaper, simpler, more testable choice — and saying why you rejected it is the point.
  • What usually blows the RTO? — Capacity in the secondary region (you cannot scale out instantly if the region is under load from everyone else's failover), DNS/TTL propagation for anything not behind Front Door, and human decision latency. Reserve capacity if the RTO is genuinely hard.
  • How do you keep the secondary from rotting? — Deploy to it on every release and send it a slice of real traffic (say 5%) continuously. A standby that only receives traffic during a disaster is a standby that fails during a disaster.
  • What is the failback plan? — Often forgotten and usually harder than failover, because the primary's data is now stale and writes have accumulated in the secondary. Decide in advance whether you re-seed the primary or simply promote the secondary permanently.

Wrong answer: “Enable geo-redundant storage and geo-replication on the database, and we can restore in the other region if needed.” GRS gives you an unreadable copy with an uncontrolled RPO, and “restore” for a real database is hours. The numbers in the question are chosen specifically to make backup-based answers fail arithmetic.

What is the difference between Azure Front Door, Traffic Manager, Application Gateway and Load Balancer? mid

Answer. Two axes: global versus regional, and HTTP versus any protocol. Front Door is global and HTTP(S): an anycast edge with over a hundred points of presence, TLS termination at the edge, caching, WAF, path-based and weighted routing, and fast failover because it is a live reverse proxy. Traffic Manager is global and DNS-based, so it works for any protocol — but failover is bounded by DNS TTL and client caching, which is why it is the wrong tool for fast HTTP failover and the right one for, say, a database endpoint or a non-HTTP service. Application Gateway is regional and HTTP(S): layer-7 routing, WAF, session affinity, mutual TLS, and backend health probes inside a VNet. Load Balancer is regional and layer 4: TCP/UDP, extremely high throughput, no content awareness. The canonical production shape is Front Door at the edge for global routing, caching and WAF, then Application Gateway or an ingress controller inside the region — and the thing to say unprompted is that Application Gateway v1 was retired on 28 April 2026, so any v1 you find is an urgent migration, and Front Door (classic) retires on 31 March 2027.

Example

ScopeLayerFailover speedWAFCaching
Front Door Std/Premglobal7 (HTTP)seconds (proxy)yes (Premium)yes
Traffic ManagerglobalDNSTTL-bound (minutes)nono
Application Gateway v2regional7 (HTTP)secondsyesno
Load Balancer (Std)regional4secondsnono

Follow-ups

  • Do you need both Front Door and Application Gateway? — Only if the regional layer earns its place: private backends inside a VNet, per-region path routing, mTLS to backends, or an existing WAF policy you cannot move. Front Door alone with Private Link origins is increasingly sufficient, and one fewer hop is one fewer thing to fail.
  • How do you stop someone bypassing Front Door and hitting the origin directly? — Restrict the origin to the AzureFrontDoor.Backend service tag and validate the X-Azure-FDID header against your profile ID, or use Private Link origins so there is no public path at all. Service tag alone allows any Front Door tenant, including an attacker's.
  • Why is Traffic Manager still used? — Non-HTTP protocols, and endpoints that are not web apps at all. Also nested profiles for complex geographic routing. Its weakness is always DNS caching, which no configuration fixes.
  • What does Front Door caching do to your architecture? — It offloads origin traffic dramatically for static content and can serve stale content during an origin outage, which is a real availability feature. It also introduces cache-invalidation as a new failure mode; version your asset URLs rather than relying on purges.

Wrong answer: “Traffic Manager for global failover — it routes users to the healthy region automatically.” It updates DNS, and clients, resolvers and some SDKs cache DNS well past the TTL, so “automatic” can mean many minutes of errors. If the requirement is fast HTTP failover, the answer is Front Door.

Your application must survive a failed deployment. What does that look like on Azure? senior

Answer. Deployment is the most likely cause of an outage, so the design goal is that a bad release is detected in seconds and reverted without a redeploy. Layers: deployment slots on App Service (deploy to a staging slot, warm it with the app-initialization pings, swap — the swap is a routing change, so the rollback is another swap and takes seconds), or on containers, revisions in Container Apps and rolling updates with readiness probes in AKS. Progressive exposure: route a small percentage of traffic to the new version (slot traffic percentage, Container Apps revision weights, Front Door weighted routing, or a service mesh) and hold it there long enough for the metrics to be meaningful. Automatic rollback tied to signals, not opinions: error rate, p95 latency, dependency failures, business metrics. Decoupled database changes: expand-migrate-contract so the previous version still runs against the new schema, because the one thing you cannot swap back is a dropped column. And feature flags so a risky behaviour can be disabled without any deployment at all, which is the fastest rollback there is.

Example

# App Service: warm, canary, swap, and an instant rollback path
az webapp deployment slot create -g rg-orders -n app-orders --slot staging
az webapp deploy -g rg-orders -n app-orders --slot staging --src-path ./app.zip
# send 10% of production traffic to the staging slot and watch
az webapp traffic-routing set -g rg-orders -n app-orders --distribution staging=10
# healthy? swap (routing change, seconds). unhealthy? set staging=0 and nothing happened.
az webapp deployment slot swap -g rg-orders -n app-orders --slot staging
# rollback = swap again; the previous build is sitting in the staging slot

Follow-ups

  • What settings should be slot-specific? — Anything that must not travel with the swap: connection strings to environment-specific resources, the Application Insights instrumentation for a separate environment, and any “this is staging” flag. Mark them sticky, or the swap moves the wrong configuration into production.
  • Why does swap warm-up matter? — Without it, the swap exposes a cold instance to full production traffic and the first requests time out — an outage caused by the rollback mechanism. WEBSITE_SWAP_WARMUP_PING_PATH and app initialization make the platform wait for readiness.
  • What is expand-migrate-contract? — Add the new column/table (expand) and deploy code that writes both and reads the old; migrate data; deploy code that reads new; only then drop the old (contract), in a later release. Each step is independently reversible, which is the whole point.
  • How long should a canary bake? — Long enough to cover a full traffic cycle for the signal you care about — for most APIs, tens of minutes; for anything with a daily batch or a weekly pattern, longer. A two-minute canary catches only crashes.

Wrong answer: “We can always redeploy the previous version from the pipeline if something goes wrong.” A redeploy takes minutes, may fail for the same reason the deploy failed, and does nothing about a schema change. Rollback must be a routing decision, not a build.

How do you actually test disaster recovery? senior

Answer. By failing over for real, on a schedule, in business hours, with the people who would be on call. Everything less is a document review. A workable programme has three tiers: tabletop quarterly (walk the runbook, find the stale phone numbers and the undocumented dependency), component failover monthly (fail over the database with a failover group, restart a zone's worth of instances, drop a dependency and confirm graceful degradation), and full regional failover once or twice a year with production traffic and a measured RTO. Azure Chaos Studio is the managed way to inject the faults — zone down, VM shutdown, network latency, AKS pod failure — with the safety of a defined blast radius and a stop button. The organisational half matters as much: define who can declare a failover without an approval chain, write the runbook so someone who has not read it can execute it, and treat every drill as an incident with a real post-mortem. Track the measured RTO over time; if it is not improving, the drills are theatre.

Example

Monthly component drill (30 min, business hours, announced)
  10:00 declare drill; observability dashboard on the wall
  10:05 az sql failover-group set-primary --name fog-orders --resource-group rg-data-neu
  10:06 measure: connection errors, duration, first successful write in neu
  10:20 fail back; measure again (failback is usually slower - that is the finding)
  10:30 write down: measured RTO, what surprised us, one fix with an owner and a date

Annual full drill: shift 100% of Front Door traffic to neu for 4 hours of real production load.
The purpose is to discover the thing nobody wrote down - a hardcoded region, a missing
private DNS link, a certificate that only exists in weu, a quota that is too low in neu.

Follow-ups

  • What always breaks the first time? — Capacity quotas in the secondary region, a dependency pinned to the primary by hostname or connection string, certificates or secrets that were created by hand once, and the alerting itself (which frequently only monitors the primary).
  • How do you get permission to run a real drill? — Start with a low-risk component in non-production, publish the findings, and use them to make the business case. Framing it as risk reduction with named findings beats asking for downtime on principle.
  • Does Azure Site Recovery still matter? — For IaaS and hybrid, yes: it replicates VMs and gives you recovery plans with ordered boot and scripts. For PaaS-native architectures, the failover mechanisms live in the services themselves and ASR is not the tool.
  • What is the metric that matters? — Measured RTO and measured RPO from drills, tracked over time, alongside the number of manual steps in the runbook. Reducing manual steps is usually the fastest way to reduce RTO.

Wrong answer: “We have a documented DR plan and we review it annually.” Every organisation that has had a bad regional incident had one of those. The interviewer is listening for the word “drill” and for a number you measured rather than promised.

How much availability should we buy? Argue for spending less. principal

Answer. Availability is bought in increasingly expensive increments, and the right level is where the marginal cost of the next nine exceeds the marginal cost of the downtime it prevents. So the argument starts with data, not architecture: what does an hour of downtime actually cost — lost revenue, SLA credits, regulatory exposure, support load, reputational effect — and how much downtime do we currently have, from what causes? In most organisations the answer is that self-inflicted outages (deployments, configuration, capacity, expiring certificates) dwarf platform failures by an order of magnitude, which means the cheapest available nine is usually bought with deployment safety, better testing and alerting, not with a second region. The second observation is that availability targets should differ by workload: a checkout path and an internal admin tool do not deserve the same investment, and pretending otherwise means overspending on one and underspending on the other. The defensible principal answer is a tiered model — criticality classes with defined RTO/RPO, mandated patterns per class, and an explicit, written acceptance that tier-3 systems will be down for a day in a regional outage.

Example

TierExampleRTO / RPOPatternRough cost multiple
1Checkout, auth15 min / 1 minMulti-region warm, zone-redundant, drilled quarterly2.2x
2Core APIs, back office4 h / 15 minSingle region zone-redundant + geo-replica, restore tested1.3x
3Internal tools, reporting24 h / 24 hSingle region, backups, redeploy from IaC1.0x

Follow-ups

  • How do you make the tier stick? — Encode it: the tier is a tag and a landing-zone parameter that selects the module defaults (zone redundancy on/off, backup retention, alert routing). If the tier is only a spreadsheet, everything drifts to tier 1 by aspiration and tier 3 by budget.
  • What is the cheapest reliability money you can spend? — Zone redundancy where the service supports it (often a checkbox and a small premium), a health endpoint that is actually meaningful, deployment slots with automatic rollback, and alerting on the symptom rather than the cause. All of it costs less than a second region.
  • When is multi-region genuinely required? — A regulatory obligation, a contractual RTO, a business that stops when the system stops, or latency requirements that need local presence. “It feels safer” is not on the list, and neither is a competitor's marketing page.
  • How do you handle a stakeholder who wants 99.999%? — Price it, including the drills and the engineering time, and show what it removes from the roadmap. Five nines is 26 seconds of downtime a month; almost no organisation's change process can survive that, and making the trade-off visible usually settles the conversation.

Wrong answer: “Always design for multi-region — you never regret redundancy.” You regret it every month on the bill, every release in the extra complexity, and during the incident where the untested failover makes things worse. Interviewers at principal level are specifically probing whether you can say no to reliability spend with a reason.

08Messaging & eventing

Azure has four messaging services that overlap enough to make “which one and why” a reliable interview question, and the follow-ups are always about delivery guarantees, ordering and poison messages — which is really a distributed-systems interview. Screener depth is naming them; senior depth is choosing correctly and handling duplicates and failures; principal depth is designing an event-driven estate, schema evolution, and knowing when a queue is the wrong answer entirely.

Docs: Service Bus · Event Hubs · Event Grid · Messaging choices

Service Bus, Event Hubs, Event Grid, Storage Queues — when do you use each? screener

Answer. Service Bus is an enterprise message broker: queues and topics, competing consumers, ordering via sessions, transactions, scheduled delivery, duplicate detection, and dead-lettering. Use it for commands and workflows where each message matters individually and you need delivery guarantees. Event Hubs is a partitioned append-only log (Kafka-protocol compatible): very high throughput telemetry and streams, consumers read by offset and checkpoint, messages are retained for a window and replayable. Use it for streams, not commands. Event Grid is a discrete-event router: reacts to Azure resource events and custom events, HTTP push to subscribers with retries and dead-lettering, plus namespaces with a pull model and MQTT for IoT. Use it for reactive notifications and Azure-native glue. Storage Queues is the simple, cheap, huge-capacity option with at-least-once delivery, a visibility timeout, and none of the advanced features. The one-line discriminator interviewers want: messages have an intended recipient and matter individually (Service Bus); events are facts broadcast to whoever cares (Event Grid); streams are high-volume ordered records read by position (Event Hubs).

Example

ScenarioServiceWhy
“Charge this order”Service Bus queueCommand; needs dedupe, retry, DLQ, order per customer
“Order was placed” to 5 subscribersService Bus topic or Event GridTopic if subscribers need broker semantics; Grid if HTTP push is enough
2 M device readings/minuteEvent HubsPartitioned throughput, replay, Kafka clients
Blob created → run a functionEvent GridNative Azure resource events, no polling
Simple background work, cost-sensitiveStorage QueueCheap, 500 TB capacity, no features to misuse

Follow-ups

  • Service Bus topic or Event Grid for pub/sub? — Topic when subscribers need broker features (competing consumers per subscription, sessions, long retention, transactional receive). Event Grid when subscribers are HTTP endpoints that just need to be told, and you want the platform to handle retries and fan-out at scale.
  • Which tier of Service Bus? — Premium for production: dedicated resources, predictable latency, VNet/private endpoint support, larger messages (up to 100 MB), and geo-DR. Standard is multi-tenant with throttling that shows up as latency spikes under load — fine for dev, risky for a hot path.
  • Why would you use Storage Queues in 2026? — Cost and capacity: hundreds of millions of messages for very little, no namespace to manage. The trade is no topics, no sessions, no dead-letter queue (you implement it), and 64 KB messages.
  • Where does Event Grid's MQTT support fit? — Event Grid namespaces added an MQTT broker and a pull delivery model, which makes it a credible IoT ingress without IoT Hub for simpler scenarios. Naming it shows you have looked at the service since 2022.

Wrong answer: “They're all message queues; pick whichever, the code is similar.” The delivery guarantees, ordering semantics, retention and cost models differ by an order of magnitude, and the wrong choice surfaces as a production correctness bug — duplicate charges, lost telemetry, or a queue that cannot replay. This question exists to check you have actually operated one of them.

Explain at-least-once delivery and what it forces you to build. mid

Answer. Every Azure messaging service delivers at least once: a consumer can receive the same message more than once because the acknowledgement can be lost after the work was done, a lock can expire mid-processing, or a rebalance can re-deliver. It forces idempotent consumers. In practice that means one of three patterns: a natural idempotency key enforced by the store (insert with a unique constraint on the message or business ID and treat a conflict as success), an explicit processed-messages table checked and written in the same transaction as the work, or state transitions written as conditional updates (“set status to Paid where status is Pending”) so replaying is a no-op. Service Bus additionally offers duplicate detection — the broker suppresses messages with a repeated MessageId within a configurable window (up to 7 days on Premium) — which protects against duplicate sends but does nothing about duplicate deliveries, and candidates conflate the two constantly. Exactly-once end-to-end does not exist across a network boundary; what exists is at-least-once delivery plus idempotent effects.

Example

// idempotency at the store, in the same transaction as the effect
await using var tx = await conn.BeginTransactionAsync();
var inserted = await conn.ExecuteAsync(
    "INSERT INTO processed_messages (message_id, handler) VALUES (@id, 'charge') " +
    "ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING", new { id = msg.MessageId }, tx);
if (inserted == 0) { await args.CompleteMessageAsync(msg); return; }   // already done

await payments.ChargeAsync(order, idempotencyKey: msg.MessageId, tx);  // provider dedupe too
await tx.CommitAsync();
await args.CompleteMessageAsync(msg);

Follow-ups

  • What is peek-lock versus receive-and-delete? — Peek-lock (the default) hides the message for a lock duration and requires an explicit complete, so a crash means redelivery; receive-and-delete removes it on read, so a crash means loss. Receive-and-delete is only acceptable for data you can afford to drop.
  • What happens if processing outlives the lock? — The lock expires, the message is redelivered, and now two workers are doing the same job. Renew the lock while working (the SDK can do it automatically), or make the handler fast and move slow work into a durable process you can resume.
  • Does duplicate detection make my consumer idempotent? — No. It deduplicates sends within a window on the broker. Redelivery after a lock expiry has the same MessageId and is supposed to arrive again. Only the consumer can be idempotent.
  • How do you make an outgoing HTTP call idempotent? — Pass an idempotency key the provider honours (most payment APIs do), or record intent before calling and reconcile afterwards. A blind retry of a non-idempotent side effect is how double charges happen.

Wrong answer: “We enable duplicate detection on the Service Bus queue, so duplicates are handled.” It handles the sender resending the same MessageId, within a window, on that queue. It does not handle redelivery after a lock timeout, a consumer crash, or a retry from a different producer — which are the actual sources of duplicates in production.

How do you handle a poison message, and how do you design the dead-letter path? mid

Answer. Service Bus dead-letters automatically once a message exceeds MaxDeliveryCount (default 10), and also on expiry, on a session or size violation, and on your explicit call — and the dead-letter queue is a real sub-queue with its own address, not a metaphor. The design work is in what happens next, because a DLQ nobody watches is a silent data-loss channel. So: alert on DLQ depth greater than zero with the message-count metric (not on a schedule — immediately), record the DeadLetterReason and DeadLetterErrorDescription, and build a documented replay path that lets an operator fix the cause and resubmit with the original message ID and correlation preserved. Distinguish two failure classes explicitly: transient (downstream timeout — retry with backoff, do not consume delivery attempts too fast, consider abandoning with a delay via scheduled re-enqueue) and permanent (a malformed payload or a business rule violation — dead-letter immediately with a reason rather than burning ten deliveries over an hour). Getting that distinction into the handler is what separates a resilient consumer from one that hammers a failing dependency.

Example

processor.ProcessMessageAsync += async args =>
{
    Order? order;
    try { order = args.Message.Body.ToObjectFromJson<Order>(); }
    catch (JsonException ex)
    {   // permanent: never going to parse. Don't burn 10 deliveries over an hour.
        await args.DeadLetterMessageAsync(args.Message, "UnparseableBody", ex.Message);
        return;
    }
    try { await handler.HandleAsync(order, args.CancellationToken); await args.CompleteMessageAsync(args.Message); }
    catch (TimeoutException)
    {   // transient: give the dependency room, keep the delivery count meaningful
        await args.AbandonMessageAsync(args.Message);
    }
};
// alert: ServiceBus DeadletteredMessages > 0 for 5 minutes -> page the owning team

Follow-ups

  • What does the DLQ cost you if you ignore it? — Dead-lettered messages still consume the entity's quota and can eventually fill it, at which point sends start failing. So an unmonitored DLQ becomes an outage of the whole queue, not just of the failed messages.
  • How do you replay safely? — A tool (or function) that reads from the DLQ, optionally transforms, and re-sends to the main queue with the original MessageId so duplicate detection and consumer idempotency still apply. Manual portal resubmission does not scale and loses metadata.
  • How does this differ for Event Hubs? — There is no DLQ: a consumer that cannot process an event must decide to skip (and checkpoint past it) or stop. The usual pattern is to write failures to a side queue or storage and continue, because blocking a partition blocks everything behind it.
  • And for Event Grid? — Retries with exponential backoff for up to 24 hours by default, then dead-letter to a storage container you configure. If you do not configure one, failed events are simply dropped — a very common silent-loss configuration gap.

Wrong answer: “Set MaxDeliveryCount high so messages get plenty of chances to succeed.” It converts a permanent failure into a long, expensive retry storm against a dependency that is already unhealthy, and delays the alert. The right lever is classifying the failure, not raising the count.

How do you guarantee ordering, and what does it cost? senior

Answer. Ordering is per-partition or per-session, never global, and buying it always costs parallelism. In Service Bus you enable sessions and set a session ID (typically the aggregate key — customer, order, device): all messages with the same session are delivered in order to a single consumer holding a session lock, which means throughput for that key is one consumer deep and a slow message blocks the key's queue. In Event Hubs ordering is per partition, and the partition key hashes to a partition; one consumer per partition per consumer group, so parallelism equals partition count and a hot key is a hot partition you cannot split. The design consequence is the interesting part: choose the ordering key as coarsely as correctness allows — usually per-entity, never global — and ask whether you need ordering at all, because the common alternatives are cheaper. Version or sequence numbers with last-writer-wins, commutative operations, or state machines that reject out-of-order transitions all give you correctness without serialising throughput.

Example

// Service Bus sessions: ordered per customer, parallel across customers
await sender.SendMessageAsync(new ServiceBusMessage(body) { SessionId = order.CustomerId });

var processor = client.CreateSessionProcessor(queue, new ServiceBusSessionProcessorOptions
{
    MaxConcurrentSessions = 32,          // 32 customers in flight
    MaxConcurrentCallsPerSession = 1,    // strictly ordered within a customer
    SessionIdleTimeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30)
});

// Event Hubs: same idea, partition key instead of session
await producer.SendAsync(events, new SendEventOptions { PartitionKey = deviceId });

Follow-ups

  • What happens when one session is hot? — That key is processed serially while others are idle; throughput collapses for that customer and the consumer count does not help. The fix is a finer key if the domain allows, or batching within the session.
  • Can you change the Event Hubs partition count? — On dedicated and premium tiers you can increase partitions, but existing key→partition mappings change, so ordering guarantees do not survive the change for in-flight keys. On standard, partition count is fixed at creation — a decision you live with.
  • How do you handle a message that must wait for an earlier one? — Deferral: complete nothing, defer the message with its sequence number, store that number, and retrieve it when the predecessor lands. It is the correct primitive and almost nobody knows it exists.
  • Is exactly-once ordered processing possible? — Not as a delivery guarantee. What you can build is ordered delivery per key plus idempotent, order-tolerant application logic — and the strongest answer says the application should tolerate out-of-order arrival wherever the domain permits, because that removes a whole class of coupling.

Wrong answer: “Use a single partition / single consumer so everything is in order.” It is correct and it caps your throughput at one consumer forever, which is usually discovered at the worst possible moment. Global ordering is almost never a real requirement — per-entity ordering is.

Design the ingestion path for 500,000 events per second of device telemetry. senior

Answer. Event Hubs, sized by partitions and throughput units, with a stream processor downstream and a clear separation between hot and cold paths. Sizing: a throughput unit is 1 MB/s or 1,000 events per second ingress and twice that egress, so 500k events/s at ~500 bytes each is roughly 250 MB/s ingress — well past the standard tier, so this is Premium or Dedicated (dedicated capacity units, or the self-serve premium tier) rather than TUs with auto-inflate. Partitions must be enough to parallelise the consumers — at least 32, likely more — and the partition key must be the device or tenant so ordering per device holds and the load spreads. Downstream: Event Hubs Capture writes raw batches straight to ADLS/blob in Avro for the cold path with no code, and a stream processor (Azure Stream Analytics, Databricks/Flink, or a Functions consumer with the batched Event Hubs trigger) handles the hot path. Consumers checkpoint per partition, so choose a checkpoint interval that balances reprocessing on failure against storage write load. Then the operational answer: back-pressure and cost. Producers must handle throttling, retention defines your replay window, and at this volume the bill is dominated by capacity plus egress, so co-locating the processor in the same region is not optional.

Example

Producers (devices) -> Event Hubs Dedicated, 64 partitions, partitionKey = deviceId
   |                       retention 7 days (replay window = how long you can be broken)
   |-- Capture --------> ADLS Gen2, Avro, 5-min windows        [cold: batch, ML, audit]
   |-- Stream Analytics -> anomaly detection -> Service Bus     [hot: alerts, commands]
   |-- Functions (batched trigger, maxEventBatchSize 500)
           -> aggregate 1-min windows -> Cosmos DB (pk = deviceId) [serving]

Sizing check: 500k evt/s x 500 B = 250 MB/s ingress, 500 MB/s if two consumer groups read it all.
Consumer parallelism: 64 partitions = at most 64 concurrent readers per consumer group.
Checkpoint every 30 s: worst-case reprocessing 30 s x 500k = 15 M events - is that acceptable?

Follow-ups

  • Why not IoT Hub? — If devices need per-device identity, cloud-to-device commands, device twins, or provisioning at scale, IoT Hub is the right front door and it can route to Event Hubs. If they are just producing telemetry with a shared credential, Event Hubs alone is simpler and cheaper.
  • How do you handle a consumer that falls behind? — Measure lag (the difference between the last enqueued sequence number and the checkpoint), alert on it, and scale consumers up to the partition count — beyond that you must add partitions or make the processing cheaper. Retention is your buffer; when lag approaches retention you are about to lose data.
  • Kafka clients? — Event Hubs exposes a Kafka endpoint, so existing Kafka producers and consumers work with a connection-string change. That is often the deciding factor for a team with a Kafka codebase, and worth naming.
  • What is the cost trap at this scale? — Egress and multiple consumer groups: every consumer group reads the full stream, so five consumer groups is five times the egress. Aggregate once and fan out from the aggregate where possible.

Wrong answer: “Use Service Bus with a topic and multiple subscriptions; it scales.” Service Bus is a broker with per-message overhead, locks and dead-lettering — features that are exactly wrong at half a million events a second, and its throughput ceiling is orders of magnitude below this. Choosing the broker for a stream is the classic mismatch this question tests.

How do you evolve an event schema without breaking consumers? senior

Answer. Treat the event as a published API with a compatibility contract. Rules: only make backward-compatible changes in place — add optional fields, never remove or rename a field, never change a type or the meaning of a value, never make an optional field required. Consumers must ignore unknown fields (tolerant reader) and must not assume field order. For anything that breaks those rules, publish a new event version — either a version field in the payload with the producer emitting both for a deprecation window, or a new event type/topic entirely — and give consumers a measured migration period with telemetry showing who is still on the old version. Formalise it with a schema registry: Event Hubs has a built-in Avro schema registry, and CloudEvents gives you a standard envelope (type, source, subject, datacontenttype, dataschema) that Event Grid uses natively, so the version lives in the envelope rather than being guessed from the body. The operational half: consumers should validate on ingest and route unparseable events to a quarantine rather than crashing, so a producer mistake degrades one consumer instead of taking down the pipeline.

Example

// CloudEvents envelope: the version is in the type, not buried in the body
{ "specversion": "1.0",
  "type": "com.contoso.orders.OrderPlaced.v2",
  "source": "/contoso/orders/eu",
  "subject": "orders/8842",
  "id": "0f6b...",
  "time": "2026-09-08T09:12:44Z",
  "dataschema": "https://schemas.contoso.com/orders/OrderPlaced/2.avsc",
  "data": { "orderId": "8842", "customerId": "c-19", "totalMinor": 4999, "currency": "EUR",
            "channel": "web" } }        // "channel" added in v2: optional, defaulted by readers

Follow-ups

  • How do you know who still consumes v1? — Emit consumer telemetry (a dimension for the schema version processed) or, at minimum, require registration of subscribers. Deprecating an event you cannot attribute is how you find out who used it, in production.
  • Thin events or fat events? — A thin event (“order 8842 changed”) keeps coupling low but forces every consumer to call back, creating load and a consistency window; a fat event carries the state and removes the callback but couples consumers to your schema. Most estates end up thin for notifications and fat for domain events with a documented contract — say the trade-off, do not pick a dogma.
  • What is the role of the outbox pattern? — Writing the event to a table in the same transaction as the state change, with a relay publishing it afterwards, so you never have the “committed the change, failed to publish” split. It is the standard answer for reliable publication and it belongs in this conversation.
  • Should events carry PII? — Prefer not: retention windows, replay and multiple consumers make deletion obligations very hard. Carry identifiers and let authorised consumers fetch the sensitive parts through an API with its own access control.

Wrong answer: “We version the topic — orders-v2 — and consumers move when they can.” It works right up to the point where nobody moves and you run both forever, doubling publication cost and creating two sources of truth. Versioning needs a deprecation date, telemetry on usage, and someone accountable for the cut-over.

When is a queue the wrong answer? principal

Answer. When the caller needs an answer, when the work is cheaper than the message, and when the queue is being used to hide a capacity problem. Specifically: synchronous need — if the user is waiting for a result, a queue converts a 200 ms call into a polling problem and a UX question; a synchronous call with a timeout and a circuit breaker is simpler and more honest. Trivial work — enqueueing, storing, delivering, locking and completing a message can cost more than doing the work inline. Masking overload — an unbounded queue in front of a saturated downstream does not add capacity, it converts fast failures into slow ones and hides the real problem behind a growing backlog; the queue depth becomes the incident. Ordering-critical, low-volume flows where sessions serialise everything anyway and you have added a broker for no parallelism benefit. The principal-level framing is that a queue buys you three specific things — temporal decoupling, load levelling and retry semantics — and if none of the three applies, it is added infrastructure with added failure modes.

Example

SymptomWhat the queue is hidingBetter move
Backlog grows daily, never drainsConsumer capacity < producer rateScale consumers or shed load at the producer
Users poll for a result for 10 sA synchronous call was neededCall directly; queue only the slow tail
DLQ full of the same errorA dependency contract changedFail fast and alert; stop retrying
Ordering forces one consumerNo parallelism gainedIn-process handling with a durable log

Follow-ups

  • How do you decide the queue is healthy? — Not by depth alone but by age of the oldest message and by drain rate versus arrival rate. A queue with 100,000 messages draining in 30 seconds is fine; a queue with 200 messages where the oldest is an hour old is broken.
  • What is load levelling actually worth? — It converts a peak into a longer, flatter period of work, which lets you size for the average instead of the peak. That is a genuine cost saving — but only if the latency of the delayed work is acceptable to the business, which is a product decision, not an architecture one.
  • What about the Claim Check pattern? — For large payloads: put the blob in storage and the reference in the message. It is the standard answer to “our messages are 40 MB” and it also fixes cost, since brokers charge by operation and size.
  • When would you replace a queue with a durable workflow? — When the flow has multiple steps, timeouts, compensation and human approvals. Durable Functions or Logic Apps model that explicitly; hand-rolling it from queues plus state tables is a well-known way to build an unobservable state machine.

Wrong answer: “Queues always improve resilience, so put one in front of everything.” Every queue adds a component, a delivery guarantee to reason about, an idempotency requirement, a DLQ to monitor, and end-to-end latency. Resilience comes from bounded queues with back-pressure and load shedding, not from buffering without limit.

09Infrastructure as code

Every Azure role above mid-level is asked how infrastructure is defined and promoted, because it is the clearest proxy for whether an estate is reproducible. Screener depth is naming Bicep, ARM and Terraform; senior depth is module design, state and secret handling, and how a change reaches production; principal depth is drift, deletion semantics, and standardising IaC across teams who each have a favourite. The 2026-current answer must include Deployment Stacks and Azure Verified Modules — their absence dates a candidate immediately.

Docs: Bicep · Deployment stacks · Azure Verified Modules

Bicep, ARM templates or Terraform — how do you choose, and what does each cost you? screener

Answer. ARM JSON is the underlying API contract; nobody should author it by hand any more, but you read it when debugging. Bicep is a first-party DSL that transpiles to ARM: same-day support for new resource types and API versions, no state file to store or protect (ARM's own resource graph is the state), strong typing and IDE support, and — since Deployment Stacks — a real answer to deletion. Terraform brings a mature module ecosystem, a plan/apply workflow people trust, multi-provider support (Azure plus DNS, GitHub, Datadog, a SaaS API), and a state file that is both its power and its liability: it must be stored, locked, secured (it can contain secrets) and repaired when it drifts. Choose Bicep when you are Azure-only and want the least machinery; choose Terraform when you span providers or when the team's existing skills and modules are there. Both are supported by Azure Verified Modules, Microsoft's reviewed module library, so the module-quality argument no longer favours one. The honest tie-breaker in an interview is: which will this team maintain in two years?

Example

BicepTerraform
Statenone (ARM is the state)state file: storage, locking, secrets
Deletes removed resourcesyes, via Deployment Stacksyes, natively
New Azure featuresday oneprovider lag, occasionally weeks
Non-Azure resourcesno (deployment scripts as a hack)yes, hundreds of providers
Preview of changeswhat-ifplan (more reliable)
Verified modulesAVM (GA for landing zones, Jan 2026)AVM

Follow-ups

  • Is what-if as good as plan? — Not quite: it has known noise on certain resource types and cannot always predict provider-side defaults, so people learn to ignore parts of it — which is exactly how a real change gets missed. It has improved a lot, but if a reliable preview is a hard requirement, that is a genuine point for Terraform.
  • What is the risk of Terraform state? — It contains secrets in plaintext for some resources, it can be corrupted or lost, and two pipelines running concurrently without locking will damage it. Remote state in a locked, RBAC-restricted, versioned storage account with soft delete is the minimum; treat it as production data.
  • Can you mix them? — Yes, and many estates do: Terraform for the platform, Bicep for workloads, or vice versa. The rule is that exactly one tool owns each resource; two tools managing the same resource is a fight neither wins.
  • What about Pulumi or the CDKs? — Legitimate when the team wants a general-purpose language and has the discipline to keep infrastructure code boring. The risk is that a Turing-complete language invites logic that should not be in infrastructure, and reviewability drops.

Wrong answer: “Terraform, because it's cloud-agnostic.” The Azure provider's resources are Azure-specific; nothing about a Terraform codebase moves to another cloud without a rewrite. Terraform's real advantages are the workflow, the ecosystem and multi-provider composition — saying “agnostic” signals you have not actually ported anything.

What are Deployment Stacks and what problem do they solve? mid

Answer. A classic ARM/Bicep deployment is additive: it creates and updates what the template declares and — in incremental mode — ignores everything else, so removing a resource from your template leaves it running forever. Complete mode deletes non-declared resources within the resource group, but it is blunt and dangerous. A Deployment Stack is a resource that owns a set of resources: it tracks what it deployed, so when you remove something from the template the stack deletes or detaches it according to actionOnUnmanage. That closes the drift gap that made people reach for Terraform's state model. Stacks also carry denySettings, which apply a deny assignment to the managed resources so they cannot be modified or deleted out of band — this is the supported way to protect platform resources from a well-meaning Owner in the portal, and it is why the Bicep Azure landing zone accelerator (GA January 2026) deploys through stacks. Scope can be resource group, subscription or management group, which makes them usable for platform-level deployments, not just workloads.

Example

az stack group create \
  --name orders-prod --resource-group rg-orders-prod \
  --template-file main.bicep --parameters main.prod.bicepparam \
  --action-on-unmanage deleteAll \        # deleteResources | deleteAll | detachAll
  --deny-settings-mode denyWriteAndDelete \
  --deny-settings-excluded-actions Microsoft.Web/sites/config/list/action \
  --deny-settings-apply-to-child-scopes

# remove a resource from main.bicep, redeploy the stack -> the resource is actually deleted
az stack group show -n orders-prod -g rg-orders-prod --query "resources[].id"

Follow-ups

  • What is the danger of deleteAll? — Exactly what it says: a template mistake that omits a resource deletes it, including a database. Use detachAll for stateful stacks or protect the data resources in a separate stack with locks, and always review what-if before applying to production.
  • How do denySettings differ from a resource lock? — A lock is applied to a scope and can be removed by anyone with the right permission; deny settings are managed by the stack, apply to the stack's resources, and support excluded actions and excluded principals, so you can permit the CI identity while blocking humans.
  • Do stacks replace Terraform? — They close the deletion gap, which was Terraform's clearest advantage in an Azure-only estate. They do not give you a plan file, a provider ecosystem, or state you can inspect offline. It narrows the gap rather than closing the argument.
  • What happens if two stacks manage the same resource? — Don't. Ownership must be unambiguous; the second stack will fight the first's deny settings and the failure mode is confusing. One resource, one owner, is the rule for stacks exactly as for state files.

Wrong answer: “We use complete-mode deployments to clean up removed resources.” Complete mode deletes everything in the resource group that is not in the template — including resources created by other teams, other pipelines, or the platform. It is the reason many organisations banned it, and stacks exist precisely because it was the only previous option.

How do you structure IaC for many environments without copy-paste? mid

Answer. One template, parameter files per environment, and modules for anything used more than twice. Concretely: a main.bicep per workload that composes modules; a .bicepparam per environment holding only what differs (SKU, instance counts, region, feature toggles, whether zone redundancy is on); shared modules — ideally Azure Verified Modules, published to a Bicep registry or an ACR — for the resources every team creates the same way. The differences between environments should be values, never branches in the template: an if (environment == 'prod') in a module means production runs code paths that non-production never exercises, which defeats the point of having environments. Where a difference is structural (prod has a second region, dev does not), express it as a module included or not at the top level, so it is visible in the composition rather than buried. Promote by moving the same artefact — the compiled template plus the environment's parameters — through the pipeline, so what you tested is what you deploy.

Example

infra/
  main.bicep                 # composition only: modules + wiring
  main.dev.bicepparam
  main.test.bicepparam
  main.prod.bicepparam
  modules/
    app.bicep                # our conventions on top of AVM
    data.bicep
  bicepconfig.json           # registry aliases, linter rules as errors
// main.prod.bicepparam - values, not logic
using './main.bicep'
param environment   = 'prod'
param location      = 'westeurope'
param appSku        = { name: 'P2v4', capacity: 3 }
param zoneRedundant = true
param sqlTier       = 'BusinessCritical'
param enableDrRegion = true          // structural difference, expressed as a flag on composition

Follow-ups

  • Where do you draw the module boundary? — Around a unit with a coherent lifecycle and a small interface: “a web app with its plan, diagnostics and private endpoint” is a good module; “every resource in the workload” is a template, not a module. If a module takes twenty parameters, it is a template with extra steps.
  • How do you version shared modules? — Publish to a registry (ACR or the public AVM registry) with semantic versions and pin consumers to a version. Referencing main of a shared repo means someone else's merge changes your production deployment.
  • How do you handle resource naming? — A naming module or a deterministic function from workload/environment/region, so names are computed rather than typed. It also gives you idempotence when a resource must be recreated.
  • What about secrets in parameter files? — Never in the file. Use Key Vault references in the parameter file (getSecret on a vault the deploying identity can read) or pass them from the pipeline's secret store at deploy time. A secret in a parameter file is a secret in git history.

Wrong answer: “We keep a separate copy of the templates per environment so each team can tweak theirs.” Within two months dev and prod have diverged in ways nobody can enumerate, and the environments stop being a test of each other. This is the single most common IaC anti-pattern and interviewers ask about it precisely because it feels pragmatic.

How do you detect and handle infrastructure drift? senior

Answer. Detect it by running the tool's own comparison on a schedule against production and alerting on any difference: az deployment group what-if or a stack redeploy in preview for Bicep, terraform plan -detailed-exitcode for Terraform, both in a nightly pipeline whose failure is a ticket. Complement it with Azure Policy for the properties you truly care about, because policy catches drift that your template does not describe (someone enabling public network access on a resource you do not manage) and it can prevent rather than report. Then decide the response policy in advance, because the technical detection is the easy half: either re-converge (the pipeline reapplies and overwrites manual changes — correct for platform resources) or reconcile (someone must fold the change back into code — necessary when the manual change was a legitimate incident fix). Prevent most of it structurally: deny settings on stacks, resource locks, and RBAC that gives humans Reader in production while the pipeline identity holds Contributor. If engineers cannot change production by hand, drift becomes rare enough to investigate individually.

Example

- name: nightly drift check
  run: |
    az deployment group what-if -g rg-orders-prod \
      --template-file main.bicep --parameters main.prod.bicepparam \
      --no-pretty-print > whatif.json
    # any Modify/Delete change means production no longer matches main
    jq -e '[.changes[] | select(.changeType != "Ignore" and .changeType != "NoChange")] | length == 0' \
      whatif.json || { echo "::error::drift detected"; exit 1; }

Follow-ups

  • What causes most drift? — Incident fixes made in the portal at 03:00 and never backported, and platform-side defaults changing under you. The first is cultural (make backporting part of the incident's follow-up actions), the second is why you pin API versions.
  • Is all drift bad? — No. Autoscale changing instance counts, a service adding a system-managed child resource, or a certificate rotating are expected. A drift check that cries wolf gets muted, so tune the ignore list deliberately and document why each entry is there.
  • How do you handle resources created outside IaC? — Import them (Terraform import, or simply describing them in Bicep and redeploying, which is idempotent) or explicitly declare them out of scope. A permanent shadow estate is the thing to avoid.
  • What does this look like at 60 subscriptions? — Centralise the report, not the remediation: one dashboard of drift by workload, owned by each team, with the platform team owning only platform resources. Central remediation of other people's workloads does not scale and creates resentment.

Wrong answer: “We don't have drift because everything goes through the pipeline.” Every estate has drift; the ones that claim not to have it simply have not looked. The follow-up is “when did you last run what-if against production?” and the answer is usually never.

How does a template deploy secrets, certificates and configuration without leaking them? senior

Answer. Ideally the template never touches the secret's value at all. Three mechanisms, in order of preference. First, eliminate the secret: managed identity for the app's access to storage, SQL, Service Bus and Key Vault means there is nothing to deploy. Second, reference rather than copy: App Service and Container Apps support Key Vault references (@Microsoft.KeyVault(SecretUri=...)) so the configuration holds a pointer and the platform resolves it at runtime with the app's identity, and Bicep's getSecret() on an existing vault passes a value into a @secure() parameter without it appearing in the deployment. Third, if a value genuinely must be set at deploy time, mark the parameter @secure() so it is scrubbed from the deployment history — noting that this only protects the ARM record, not your pipeline logs, so the pipeline must mask it too. Certificates follow the same shape: issue and store in Key Vault with autorotation, and reference from App Service, Application Gateway or Front Door by vault URI so renewal does not need a deployment.

Example

@secure()
param adminPassword string          // scrubbed from deployment history

resource kv 'Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults@2023-07-01' existing = { name: 'kv-orders-prod' }

resource app 'Microsoft.Web/sites@2023-12-01' = {
  name: 'app-orders'
  identity: { type: 'UserAssigned', userAssignedIdentities: { '${uai.id}': {} } }
  properties: { siteConfig: { appSettings: [
    // the app setting holds a POINTER; the platform resolves it with the app's identity
    { name: 'Payments__ApiKey'
    , value: '@Microsoft.KeyVault(SecretUri=${kv.properties.vaultUri}secrets/payments-api-key)' }
    // no connection string at all - the app uses its managed identity
    { name: 'Sql__ConnectionString'
    , value: 'Server=tcp:sql-orders.database.windows.net;Database=orders;Authentication=Active Directory Default;' }
  ] } }
}

Follow-ups

  • What leaks even with @secure()? — Anything you interpolate into an output, a nested deployment name, or a non-secure parameter; and pipeline logs if the runner echoes the value. Outputs are the classic leak: an output is stored in the deployment history in plaintext.
  • How does a Key Vault reference behave when the vault is unreachable? — App settings resolve at start and on a schedule; a vault outage or a broken network path means the app starts with a missing setting. Design the failure — fail fast with a clear message rather than a null reference deep in a handler.
  • How do you rotate a secret that many apps reference? — Reference the secret without a version so apps pick up the current version, then rotate in the vault and let the platform refresh (App Service polls periodically; restart to force). Versioned references are pinned and will not rotate — a common surprise.
  • What about certificates for custom domains? — App Service managed certificates for simple cases, or Key Vault certificates with autorotation referenced by Front Door/Application Gateway. Manually uploaded PFX files are the pattern that produces the annual expiry outage.

Wrong answer: “We pass the secrets in as parameters from the pipeline's variable group — they're encrypted at rest there.” It works, and it means every secret exists in a second system, is visible to anyone who can edit the pipeline, and rotates only when someone remembers. The strong answer starts by asking why the secret exists at all.

You inherit an estate built entirely by hand in the portal. How do you get it into code? principal

Answer. Not by exporting everything. Portal export produces unusable ARM (hardcoded IDs, missing dependencies, defaults you do not want), so the sequence is: inventory with Resource Graph so you know the true scope; triage into what must be reproducible (production workloads, platform), what should simply be deleted (there is always 20–30% nobody owns), and what can stay manual for now; codify forward — new resources are only created through code, enforced by taking Contributor away from humans in production; and backfill by rebuild rather than by import wherever a resource is stateless. Rebuilding an App Service from a template and swapping traffic is faster and produces better code than reverse-engineering the existing one. Stateful resources (databases, storage accounts with data, Key Vaults) get described in code and adopted in place — Terraform import, or a Bicep deployment that is idempotent against the existing resource. Sequence by risk, deliver visible wins early (one workload fully in code, with a pipeline, in the first month), and be explicit with leadership that this is a multi-quarter programme whose deliverable is the ability to rebuild.

Example

Month 1  Inventory (Resource Graph) + ownership map + kill list. One pilot workload in Bicep,
         deployed by a federated pipeline, with a stack and drift check. Publish the diff.
Month 2  Platform first: network, DNS, policy, log workspace into code (they change least and
         break everything). Deploy through stacks with denySettings.
Month 3+ One workload per team per sprint, using shared modules. Rebuild stateless, adopt stateful.
         Remove human Contributor in prod as each workload lands. Non-prod goes first, always.
Guardrail: from day one, a policy that denies resource creation outside the deployment identities
           in the pilot subscriptions - the scope of that policy expands as coverage grows.
Definition of done: a quarterly game day where we rebuild a workload into an empty subscription.

Follow-ups

  • Why not export and import everything? — The exported templates are not maintainable, so you end up with code you do not understand describing resources you did not design. Codifying by rebuilding forces you to learn the system, and produces templates a human can review.
  • How do you handle the resources nobody owns? — Tag them, announce a decommission date, shut them down (not delete) for two weeks, and see who complains. It is crude and it is the only method that reliably works.
  • How do you keep the team's morale through a long programme? — Ship a visible capability every month — a pipeline, a rebuilt environment, a drift dashboard — and tie each to an incident it would have prevented. Pure “technical debt” framing loses funding by quarter two.
  • What proves you are done? — Rebuilding a production-equivalent environment from an empty subscription in a measured time, as a scheduled exercise. Coverage percentages are a proxy; the rebuild is the test.

Wrong answer: “Export the ARM templates from the portal for every resource group and commit them.” You get thousands of lines of unreadable JSON that will not redeploy, and a false sense that the estate is now “in code”. Interviewers ask this question because that is the first instinct and it is a trap.

10Observability & Azure Monitor

Observability questions probe whether you can find out what is happening in production and whether you can pay for it. Screener depth is metrics versus logs and where Application Insights fits; senior depth is KQL, distributed tracing, sampling and alert design; principal depth is ingestion cost control, an SLO-based alerting strategy, and standardising telemetry across teams. Azure Monitor has changed substantially — OpenTelemetry, table plans, DCR-based collection — so this is a strong place to show currency.

Docs: Azure Monitor · Azure Monitor OpenTelemetry · Logs data platform

What are the pieces of Azure Monitor, and where does telemetry actually go? screener

Answer. Two stores with different economics. Metrics are a time-series database: numeric, pre-aggregated, cheap, fast, retained for 93 days by default, and the right basis for alerts that must fire in under a minute. Logs are a Log Analytics workspace queried with KQL: schema-on-write tables holding traces, requests, dependencies, exceptions, platform diagnostics and activity logs, priced by ingestion volume and retention. Everything else is a producer or a consumer of those two. Producers: platform metrics (automatic), diagnostic settings (per-resource, routing resource logs to a workspace, storage or Event Hubs), the Azure Monitor Agent configured by Data Collection Rules for VMs, and the SDK for application telemetry. Application Insights is no longer a separate store — classic components retired on 29 February 2024 and every component is now workspace-based, writing into a Log Analytics workspace, which is why cross-signal KQL joins work at all. Consumers: alerts, workbooks, dashboards, Managed Grafana, and the Prometheus-compatible managed service for Kubernetes metrics.

Example

// one query across app telemetry and platform logs, because it is all one workspace now
let window = 30m;
requests
| where timestamp > ago(window) and success == false
| join kind=leftouter (
    AzureDiagnostics
    | where timestamp > ago(window) and Category == "SQLSecurityAuditEvents"
    | project TimeGenerated, correlation_id = tostring(customDimensions.correlationId)
  ) on $left.operation_Id == $right.correlation_id
| summarize failures = count() by name, resultCode
| order by failures desc

Follow-ups

  • Why do diagnostic settings matter so much? — Without one, a resource's logs are simply not collected — they are not stored anywhere by default and cannot be recovered retroactively. That is why “enable diagnostic settings on everything” is a landing-zone policy with DeployIfNotExists, not a per-team decision.
  • What replaced the Log Analytics agent? — The Azure Monitor Agent with Data Collection Rules. The legacy agent was retired on 31 August 2024 and its data upload may stop at any time after 2 March 2026, so any VM still running it is an active risk, not a tidy-up.
  • One workspace or many? — Default to few: cross-service queries and correlation are the whole value, and RBAC can be applied per table or per resource. Split for data residency, for very different retention or cost ownership, or for a security workspace with restricted access.
  • Where does the activity log fit? — Control-plane operations (who deployed, who deleted), retained 90 days by default and exportable to a workspace via a diagnostic setting at subscription or management-group scope. It is the audit trail, and it is not collected into your workspace unless you say so.

Wrong answer: “Application Insights stores application telemetry and Log Analytics stores infrastructure logs; they're separate systems.” True before 2024, wrong now — workspace-based Application Insights writes into the same workspace, which is precisely what makes end-to-end correlation possible. Saying it the old way suggests you have not touched the platform in a few years.

Write a KQL query to find the slowest dependency causing p95 latency in an API. mid

Answer. The instinct to demonstrate is joining requests to their dependencies by operation_Id and summarising by percentile, then attributing the request's latency to the dependency that dominated it. KQL specifics interviewers listen for: summarize percentile() or percentiles() rather than avg() (averages hide the tail you were asked about), bin() for time bucketing, make-series when you want a chartable series, and awareness that join in KQL defaults to inner-unique — which silently drops rows and is the most common cause of a query that looks right and is not. Also worth saying out loud: filter early and on indexed columns (timestamp, then where on strings), because a workspace query that scans a week of data is slow and, on some table plans, billed.

Example

let window = 24h;
let slowOps =
    requests
    | where timestamp > ago(window) and name startswith "POST /api/orders"
    | summarize p95 = percentile(duration, 95), n = count() by operation_Name
    | where n > 100;
dependencies
| where timestamp > ago(window)
| summarize dep_p95  = percentile(duration, 95),
            dep_sum  = sum(duration),
            calls    = count(),
            failures = countif(success == false)
          by operation_Name, target, type
| join kind=inner slowOps on operation_Name
| extend share_of_request = round(100.0 * dep_p95 / p95, 1)
| project operation_Name, target, type, calls, dep_p95, p95, share_of_request, failures
| order by share_of_request desc
| take 20

Follow-ups

  • Why not average? — The p95 question is about the tail; an average is dominated by the fast majority and moves barely at all when 5% of requests take ten seconds. Alerting on averages is how latency regressions go unnoticed for weeks.
  • What is the join gotcha? — join without a kind is innerunique, which de-duplicates the left side. For telemetry correlation you almost always want kind=inner or kind=leftouter, and forgetting it produces plausible, wrong numbers.
  • How do you find high-cardinality culprits? — Summarise by a dimension and use top-nested or arg_max to drill in without exploding the result set; and check customDimensions for the fields your app added, which is where the useful dimension usually lives.
  • How do you make this repeatable? — Save it as a workbook parameterised by service and window, and reference it from the runbook. A query that lives in someone's clipboard is not observability.
