Wrong answer: “Look at the Application Insights performance blade and read off the slowest operation.” It is a fine starting point and it is not an answer to “write a query”; the interviewer is testing whether you can go beyond the pre-built views when the question is specific to your system.

How do you instrument a distributed system on Azure so a request can be traced end to end? senior

Answer. Standardise on OpenTelemetry and let the Azure Monitor OpenTelemetry Distro export to Application Insights: it is the supported path, it gives you the same instrumentation across languages, and it means the vendor decision is an exporter change rather than a rewrite. The mechanics that make tracing work: W3C Trace Context propagation (traceparent/tracestate headers) across every hop including through queues, where the context must be carried in message properties rather than headers; consistent service.name and service.version resource attributes so the map is readable; and span attributes carrying the identifiers you will actually search by — tenant, order, correlation ID. Then decide sampling deliberately (below), and add the two signals traces alone do not give you: structured logs correlated by trace ID, and metrics for the things you alert on, because alerting off traces is expensive and slow. The senior-level detail is that propagation across asynchronous boundaries is where most estates break — a message published in one trace and consumed later must link, not start a new trace.

Example

builder.Services.AddOpenTelemetry().UseAzureMonitor(o =>
{
    o.ConnectionString = cfg["ApplicationInsights:ConnectionString"];
    o.SamplingRatio = 0.2f;                       // fixed-rate; see the sampling question
});
builder.Services.ConfigureOpenTelemetryTracerProvider((sp, t) => t
    .AddSource("Contoso.Orders")
    .SetResourceBuilder(ResourceBuilder.CreateDefault()
        .AddService("orders-api", serviceVersion: BuildInfo.Version)));

// carry the trace across the queue boundary - headers do not exist here
var msg = new ServiceBusMessage(body);
Activity.Current?.Id is { } id ? msg.ApplicationProperties["traceparent"] = id : default;

Follow-ups

  • What breaks correlation most often? — Async hops (queues, timers, background jobs) that start a fresh activity, and third-party HTTP clients that strip headers. Test correlation explicitly: a synthetic request that must appear as one trace across all hops, asserted in CI.
  • Do you still need the classic Application Insights SDK? — It works and is in maintenance; OpenTelemetry is where the investment is. For a new service, OTel; for an existing one, migrate when you touch it rather than as a project.
  • How do you handle logs versus traces? — Emit structured logs with the trace ID attached so a log search leads to a trace and back. Do not try to reconstruct traces from logs; that is what span links exist for.
  • What about Kubernetes? — Managed Prometheus for metrics and Container Insights for logs, plus the OTel collector as a DaemonSet or sidecar if you want to preprocess and reduce volume before egress. The collector is also the cheapest place to drop noisy attributes.

Wrong answer: “Log the correlation ID in every service and grep across logs when we need to trace something.” It is what teams do before tracing exists, and it collapses as soon as there are more than a few services or asynchronous hops. The interviewer is checking whether you know the difference between correlation by convention and distributed tracing with a propagated context.

Our Log Analytics bill tripled. What do you do? senior

Answer. Find the volume first, then decide per table whether to reduce, re-tier or drop. The query is straightforward — Usage summarised by DataType over the last month — and the answer is almost always concentrated: AppTraces from a debug logger left on, ContainerLogInsights from stdout in a chatty cluster, AzureDiagnostics from a firewall or Application Gateway, or a diagnostic setting sending every category rather than the ones anyone reads. The levers, in order of impact: stop collecting what nobody queries (categories in diagnostic settings, log levels, noisy dependencies), transform at ingestion with a DCR transformation to drop columns or filter rows before they are billed, re-tier — Basic and Auxiliary table plans cost far less to ingest in exchange for limited query features and a search-job access model, which is exactly right for verbose logs kept for compliance — and shorten retention, moving the long tail to archive or a storage account. Then prevent recurrence: a commitment tier if the steady volume justifies it, a daily cap on non-production workspaces, and an alert on daily ingestion growth so the next tripling is caught in a day rather than a bill.

Example

Usage
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d) and IsBillable == true
| summarize GB = sum(Quantity) / 1000 by DataType, bin(TimeGenerated, 1d)
| summarize total_GB = sum(GB), daily_avg = avg(GB) by DataType
| order by total_GB desc
| take 15
// then: who inside that table?
AppTraces
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| summarize count(), bytes = sum(estimate_data_size(*)) by AppRoleName, SeverityLevel
| order by bytes desc

Follow-ups

  • What is a DCR transformation? — A KQL snippet applied at ingestion that can filter rows and project away columns before billing. It is the highest-leverage control because it works for platform logs you do not control the source of — for example dropping successful health-probe rows from a gateway's access log.
  • When is the Basic or Auxiliary plan right? — High-volume, low-query-value data you must retain: verbose application traces, network flow logs, firewall logs. You lose alerting on the table and full KQL, and you access it with search jobs — acceptable for forensics, unacceptable for the tables your alerts read.
  • Do commitment tiers help? — Yes, if the volume is stable: committing to a daily GB level discounts the per-GB rate meaningfully. Do the reduction work first, then commit — committing to your bloated volume locks in the waste.
  • What about Application Insights sampling? — Adaptive sampling reduces volume automatically while preserving rates by scaling counts; fixed-rate sampling is predictable and easier to reason about across services. Either way, ensure it is consistent across services so a sampled trace is not half-present.

Wrong answer: “Set a daily cap on the workspace so it stops ingesting when we hit the limit.” A cap in production means telemetry stops mid-incident — precisely when you need it. Caps belong in non-production; production needs reduction, tiering and alerting on growth.

Design an alerting strategy that doesn't page people at 3 a.m. for nothing. senior

Answer. Alert on user-visible symptoms with a severity that maps to an action, and route everything else to a dashboard or a ticket. Concretely: define an SLO per service (success rate and latency measured at the entry point), alert on burn rate of the error budget rather than on a raw threshold — a fast-burn rule (for example, 2% of the monthly budget consumed in an hour) pages, a slow-burn rule (10% in six hours) opens a ticket. Cause-based alerts (CPU, memory, queue depth) exist only when they are actionable and predictive, and they should be tickets, not pages. Then engineer the plumbing: action groups per team with a real escalation path, alert processing rules to suppress during maintenance windows, dynamic thresholds where the metric is seasonal, and dimension-aware log alerts so one alert per affected service rather than one per instance. Finally, treat alerts as code with an owner and review them monthly — retire anything that fired and was ignored, because an alert nobody acts on trains people to ignore all of them.

Example

// fast-burn: SLO 99.9% -> budget 0.1%. Burning 14.4x means the month's budget goes in ~2 days.
let slo = 0.999;
requests
| where timestamp > ago(1h)
| summarize total = count(), bad = countif(success == false or duration > 1500)
          by cloud_RoleName
| extend error_rate = todouble(bad) / total
| extend burn_rate  = error_rate / (1.0 - slo)
| where burn_rate > 14.4 and total > 100      // volume guard: don't page on 3 requests
| project cloud_RoleName, error_rate, burn_rate, total

Follow-ups

  • Why a volume guard? — At 04:00 a service might serve five requests; one failure is a 20% error rate and a page for nothing. Every rate-based alert needs a minimum-volume condition, and forgetting it is the single most common cause of noise.
  • Metric alert or log alert? — Metric alerts are near-real-time and cheap; log alerts are flexible and slower (evaluation frequency plus ingestion latency, often 3–5 minutes end to end). Page on metrics where you can, use log alerts where the condition needs a query.
  • How do you avoid alert storms? — Alert processing rules to suppress by resource or time window, correlation at the notification layer, and alerting at the service level rather than the instance level. One incident should produce one page.
  • Who owns an alert? — The team that can fix it, named in the alert itself along with a link to the runbook. An alert routed to a shared mailbox is an alert that will be ignored.

Wrong answer: “Alert on CPU above 80%, memory above 90%, and any 5xx.” None of those is necessarily user-visible, all of them fire during normal operation, and within a month the on-call rotation has muted the channel. Interviewers ask this question specifically to see whether you think in symptoms or in resource counters.

How do you drive observability standards across twelve teams who all do it differently? principal

Answer. Make the standard the path of least resistance, and enforce only the small set that enables cross-team work. Practically: ship an internal library or OTel collector configuration that sets the resource attributes, propagation, sampling and exporter correctly by default, so adopting the standard is deleting code rather than writing it. Define the minimum contract — service name and version, trace propagation, a small set of required span attributes (tenant, environment, correlation), a standard health endpoint, and an SLO per service — and verify it automatically: a conformance check in CI, plus a platform dashboard showing which services are compliant. Provide the shared assets that make compliance valuable: a common workbook, a service catalogue that links to each service's dashboards and runbooks, and one alerting pipeline. Then be pragmatic about the rest — do not standardise log message formats or dictate a logging library; that fight costs more than it returns. The principal-level point is that standards spread through usefulness and defaults, and the only ones worth enforcing are the ones that break correlation if a single team opts out.

Example

Mandatory (enforced in CI, breaks the build)
  - OTel resource: service.name, service.version, deployment.environment
  - W3C trace context propagated, including across queues
  - health endpoints: /health/live, /health/ready
  - one SLO defined, with the alert rule generated from it
Provided (opt out if you must, but you will write it yourself)
  - platform telemetry library with the exporter + sampling configured
  - shared workbook, shared action groups, incident dashboard template
  - cost dashboard per service, so a team sees its own ingestion
Not standardised
  - log message format, logging library, dashboard aesthetics
Adoption metric: % of services on the platform library, published monthly. Not a mandate - a chart.

Follow-ups

  • What if a team refuses? — Find out why; usually the default breaks something specific. If it is genuinely preference, let them opt out of the optional parts and hold the line only on the correlation contract, then show them the cost of being outside the shared tooling during their next incident.
  • How do you handle cost accountability? — Give each team a view of its own ingestion volume and cost, and make it part of their budget. Central cost with no attribution guarantees over-logging; attribution alone fixes most of it without a policy.
  • How do you avoid the platform team becoming the observability helpdesk? — Documentation, examples, and office hours; plus dashboards teams can fork rather than request. If every query goes through you, you have built a dependency, not a platform.
  • What is the one thing you would enforce absolutely? — Trace propagation. Everything else can be inconsistent and still useful; a broken trace context makes every other team's telemetry less valuable, which is exactly the criterion for a mandate.

Wrong answer: “Publish a standards document and require sign-off in architecture review.” Documents do not change defaults, and review gates create resentment without adoption. The measurable difference between a platform and a policy is whether the compliant path is also the easy one.

11Secrets, keys & encryption

Asked less often than identity but almost always as a follow-up to it, and the questions get specific fast: what is in the vault, who can read it, how does it rotate, and what happens when the key is destroyed. Screener depth is “use Key Vault”; senior depth is RBAC versus access policies, rotation without downtime, and CMK; principal depth is key custody, crypto-shredding and the operational risk of customer-managed keys.

Docs: Key Vault · Managed HSM · Encryption at rest

Key Vault: RBAC or access policies, and what else must be on? screener

Answer. Use Azure RBAC for the data plane; access policies are the legacy model. RBAC gives you management-group and subscription-level inheritance, PIM eligibility, deny assignments, and consistency with everything else in Azure; access policies are a per-vault list with a fixed permission vocabulary, no inheritance, and a 1,024-entry ceiling, and they cannot be managed with the same tooling. Beyond the authorisation model, three settings must be on and are effectively non-negotiable: soft delete (now always enabled and non-disableable) and purge protection, which together mean a deleted vault or secret is recoverable and cannot be permanently destroyed during the retention window — the control that stops both accidents and a destructive attacker; and network restriction, meaning a private endpoint with public access disabled, or at minimum firewall rules. Then use the least-privilege roles rather than the convenient ones: Key Vault Secrets User to read secrets, not Key Vault Administrator, and scope them to the vault, not the resource group.

Example

resource kv 'Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults@2023-07-01' = {
  name: 'kv-orders-prod'
  properties: {
    sku: { family: 'A', name: 'standard' }
    tenantId: tenant().tenantId
    enableRbacAuthorization: true      // RBAC, not accessPolicies
    enableSoftDelete: true
    softDeleteRetentionInDays: 90
    enablePurgeProtection: true        // cannot be turned off once on - by design
    publicNetworkAccess: 'Disabled'
    networkAcls: { defaultAction: 'Deny', bypass: 'None' }
  }
}
// the app reads secrets, nothing more
resource ra 'Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments@2022-04-01' = {
  scope: kv
  name: guid(kv.id, uai.id, 'kvsecretsuser')
  properties: { principalId: uai.properties.principalId, principalType: 'ServicePrincipal'
              , roleDefinitionId: subscriptionResourceId('Microsoft.Authorization/roleDefinitions'
                , '4633458b-17de-408a-b874-0445c86b69e6') }   // Key Vault Secrets User
}

Follow-ups

  • What is the downside of purge protection? — You cannot reuse a vault name for the retention period, which breaks ephemeral environments that create and destroy vaults with deterministic names. Use a suffix, or a shared vault for ephemeral environments — do not turn purge protection off in production to solve a dev problem.
  • What are the throttling limits? — Key Vault has per-vault transaction limits (in the low thousands per 10 seconds, lower for HSM key operations), so an app fetching secrets on every request will be throttled at scale. Cache secrets in memory for their lifetime; the SDK's caching plus Key Vault references handle this for you.
  • Should each app have its own vault? — Usually yes, per application per environment: the vault is the blast radius and the throttling unit. A single shared vault means one team's traffic throttles another's and RBAC becomes a spreadsheet.
  • Secrets, keys or certificates? — Secrets are arbitrary values; keys are cryptographic material you never extract (you ask the vault to sign/wrap); certificates are managed objects with issuance and autorotation. Storing a private key as a “secret” loses the guarantee that it never leaves the vault.

Wrong answer: “We use access policies because they're simpler and give finer-grained permissions.” They are neither — RBAC has more granular built-in roles and supports inheritance, PIM and deny. Access policies are what you will find in old vaults, and migrating is a normal modernisation task.

How do you rotate a secret with zero downtime? mid

Answer. With two valid credentials at once, so there is never an instant when the old one is dead and the new one is not yet everywhere. The canonical pattern for services with dual keys (storage, Service Bus, Cosmos DB, Event Hubs, Redis): applications read the secret from Key Vault by unversioned URI and cache it briefly; the rotation job regenerates the secondary key at the source, writes it as a new version in the vault, waits long enough for every consumer's cache to expire, then regenerates the primary next cycle — so at any moment both the deployed value and the source-side key are valid. For credentials without a secondary (a third-party API key), the equivalent is to obtain a new key while the old one is still active, publish it, verify adoption via telemetry, and only then revoke. Automate the whole thing with an Event Grid event on SecretNearExpiry triggering a function, set an expiry on every secret so the event fires, and alert if a rotation does not complete. The deeper answer, though, is to reduce the number of things needing rotation: managed identities and federated credentials have nothing to rotate, and every secret you eliminate is a rotation job you do not have to write.

Example

Day 0   app reads kv://kv-prod/secrets/sb-connection  (no version -> current)
        source: PRIMARY key active (in use), SECONDARY key active (unused)
Day 30  rotation function (triggered by SecretNearExpiry event):
          1. regenerate SECONDARY at the source
          2. write it as a NEW VERSION of the secret in Key Vault
          3. both keys are valid; apps pick up the new value within the cache TTL
          4. verify adoption: telemetry shows no auth failures for 24h
Day 31    regenerate PRIMARY (now unused) -> it becomes the spare for the next cycle
Alert     secret expiry < 7 days and no new version written -> page the owning team

Follow-ups

  • Why does the unversioned URI matter? — A versioned reference pins the app to that exact version, so rotation has no effect and you discover it when the old key is revoked. This is a genuinely common production incident.
  • What about certificates? — Key Vault certificates with an issuer configured can autorotate, and consumers (App Service, Front Door, Application Gateway) that reference the vault pick up the new version. Manual PFX uploads are how you get an annual outage at 02:00 on a Sunday.
  • How do you know the rotation worked? — Monitor authentication failures at the resource and the age of the newest secret version. “The job ran without error” is not evidence that consumers adopted the value.
  • What if a secret leaks? — Then rotation is an incident, not a schedule: revoke immediately, accept the outage of anything still holding it, and audit access logs for use. Having practised the routine rotation is what makes the emergency one survivable.

Wrong answer: “Update the secret in Key Vault and restart the apps.” It is a deliberate outage, it does not scale past a handful of services, and it fails entirely when a consumer is a third party or a scheduled job that is mid-run. The dual-credential window is the whole technique.

Explain encryption at rest on Azure and when customer-managed keys are worth it. senior

Answer. Every Azure storage and database service encrypts at rest by default with Microsoft-managed keys, using envelope encryption: data encryption keys protect the data, and a key encryption key protects the DEKs. Customer-managed keys replace the KEK with a key in your Key Vault or Managed HSM, which buys you three things: the ability to revoke access to your own data by disabling the key (crypto-shredding), an auditable key lifecycle you control, and a compliance box that some regulators and customers require. What it costs: an availability dependency — if the key is deleted, disabled, or the service loses access, the resource becomes unavailable, sometimes irrecoverably — plus key rotation operations, and the need for the key vault to be as available as the data. Managed HSM goes further with FIPS 140-3 Level 3 single-tenant HSMs and a security-domain model where Microsoft genuinely cannot recover your keys; that is the right answer for regulated key custody and the wrong answer for a team that has never operated key material. The judgment interviewers listen for: CMK when a named requirement demands it, not by default, and always with monitoring of key availability.

Example

Layers, from the bottom
  1. platform disk encryption (always on, Microsoft-managed)
  2. service-level encryption at rest: DEK per data unit, wrapped by a KEK
       KEK = Microsoft-managed (default) | customer-managed in Key Vault | CMK in Managed HSM
  3. optional double encryption at rest (infrastructure encryption) for some services
  4. application-level encryption: Always Encrypted (SQL), client-side blob encryption
       -> the only layer where Azure never sees plaintext

Operational rules when you turn CMK on
  - purge protection MUST be on (services refuse CMK without it)
  - the vault must be at least as available as the data (zone-redundant, monitored)
  - alert on key expiry, key disable, and access failures - these are outages, not warnings
  - document who can disable the key: that person can take production down

Follow-ups

  • What does CMK actually protect against? — Not a Microsoft insider reading your disks (platform encryption already covers the physical layer); it protects against your data remaining readable after you revoke, and it gives you the audit trail. Be precise here — overclaiming the threat model is a common tell.
  • What is Always Encrypted, and when? — Column-level encryption where the keys live with the client, so SQL never sees plaintext for those columns; with secure enclaves, some comparisons and pattern matching still work. Right for a small number of highly sensitive columns, wrong as a blanket policy — it breaks indexing and many query shapes.
  • What about confidential computing? — Encryption in use: AMD SEV-SNP or Intel TDX-backed confidential VMs and confidential containers on AKS, where memory is encrypted and attestable. Relevant for multi-party data scenarios and for regulators asking about data in use; it is a real and current Azure capability worth naming.
  • How do you rotate a CMK? — Create a new key version; services that support autorotation pick it up, others need a re-wrap operation. Test it, because a broken rotation is discovered as a service that cannot start.

Wrong answer: “We use customer-managed keys everywhere because it's more secure.” It adds an availability dependency and an operational burden without changing the threat model unless you have a revocation or custody requirement. The follow-up — “what threat does that mitigate that platform encryption does not?” — is where the answer has to be specific.

A secret has leaked into a public GitHub repository. Walk me through the response. senior

Answer. Assume it is compromised the moment it was pushed, and work three tracks in parallel. Contain: revoke or rotate the credential immediately — for an Azure resource key that is a regenerate, for a service principal secret a delete, for a SAS a delegation-key revoke or account-key rotation — accepting the availability impact rather than deferring it. Deleting the commit is not containment; the value must be assumed harvested within minutes by automated scanners. Investigate: determine what the credential could reach and check whether it was used — Entra sign-in logs for a service principal, storage and Key Vault diagnostic logs for data access, activity log for control-plane operations, Defender for Cloud alerts — with the exposure window running from push time to revocation. Remediate: purge the secret from history if it is a private repo worth cleaning, then fix the class — pre-commit and server-side secret scanning with push protection, a policy that the value should never have existed (managed identity or federated credential instead), and a check on whether the same secret exists in other repos, in wikis, in tickets and in CI logs. Finally, write it up honestly, including how long detection took, because that number is the thing you actually need to improve.

Example

// was the leaked service principal used, and from where?
AADServicePrincipalSignInLogs
| where TimeGenerated between (datetime(2026-09-05 14:10) .. now())
| where ServicePrincipalId == "8f1c...b204"
| summarize attempts = count(), first = min(TimeGenerated), last = max(TimeGenerated)
          by IPAddress, ResultType, tostring(ResourceDisplayName)
| order by attempts desc
// and what did it touch on the data plane?
StorageBlobLogs
| where TimeGenerated > datetime(2026-09-05 14:10)
| where RequesterObjectId == "8f1c...b204"
| summarize ops = count(), bytes = sum(ResponseBodySize) by OperationName, CallerIpAddress

Follow-ups

  • How fast must you revoke? — Minutes. Public-repo scanners find credentials in seconds, and Microsoft's own secret scanning may notify you before you notice. Any plan that starts with “first we assess the impact” has the order wrong.
  • What if revoking causes an outage? — Take the outage. Decide the trade-off before the incident by classifying credentials, so no one is negotiating at 22:00 about whether a leaked production key is worth ten minutes of downtime.
  • How do you prevent recurrence? — Push protection and secret scanning on the repository, pre-commit hooks locally, CI failing on detected secrets, and — the structural fix — removing the secret's reason to exist. Detection alone is a treadmill.
  • What about secrets in container images and logs? — Both are common leak paths that repo scanning misses: build-arg secrets baked into layers, and exception messages that print connection strings. Scan images and add redaction to the logging pipeline.

Wrong answer: “Force-push to remove the commit and rewrite history — then the secret is gone.” Forks, clones, caches, CI logs and the GitHub API have all seen it, and scanners archive it. History rewriting is hygiene after revocation, never instead of it.

Design key custody for a regulated workload where the customer must be able to revoke access. principal

Answer. Give the customer control of the KEK and design the whole system so a revocation is a clean, understood failure rather than a mystery outage. Concretely: Managed HSM (or the customer's own Key Vault in their tenant) holding the key encryption key, with the service granted only wrap/unwrap; per-customer keys so revocation affects one tenant; and the data encrypted with per-tenant DEKs wrapped by that KEK. Access from your tenant to theirs uses cross-tenant CMK support or a federated identity with a narrow role. Then engineer the operational reality: monitor key availability continuously and alert the customer and your on-call when a key is disabled, degrade that tenant explicitly (a clear “access to your data is disabled by your key policy” state, not a 500), and document the recovery path with the retention window. Add the governance pieces regulators actually ask for: separation of duties so no single person can both access the data and destroy the key, an auditable log of every key operation exported to the customer, a tested key-rotation procedure, and a documented crypto-shredding process that satisfies deletion obligations. Finally, be explicit in the contract about what revocation means: irreversible after the retention window, and their responsibility.

Example

Customer tenant                          Your tenant
  Managed HSM (customer-owned)             Service, per-tenant DEKs
  key: kek-tenant-4471                     data encrypted with DEK, DEK wrapped by kek-tenant-4471
  role granted: Crypto Service Encryption  identity: federated, wrap/unwrap only, no export
  customer can disable the key at any time

Behaviours you must design
  key disabled      -> tenant enters ReadDenied state within 60s; users see an explicit message
  key re-enabled    -> automatic recovery, no support ticket
  key deleted       -> soft-delete window (documented, e.g. 90 days), then unrecoverable
  key unavailable   -> alert BOTH sides; distinguish from a service outage in status pages
Separation of duties: HSM role assignments reviewed quarterly; no principal holds both
data-plane access to the service and Crypto Officer on the HSM.

Follow-ups

  • What is the biggest operational risk? — A customer disabling their key by accident, usually during their own key-rotation work, and raising a severity-1 with you. Pre-agree the runbook and make the error message name the cause, or you will spend the outage proving it is not your fault.
  • How does this interact with backups? — Backups must be encrypted with the same key or a key under the same custody, or revocation leaves readable copies — which defeats the control and fails an audit. This is the detail that catches most implementations.
  • Does crypto-shredding satisfy a deletion request? — Often yes if the key is genuinely destroyed and no plaintext copies exist, but it depends on the regulator and the jurisdiction; get it in writing from legal rather than asserting it. Also enumerate the places plaintext could persist — caches, search indexes, logs, analytics copies.
  • What if the customer wants their own HSM on-premises? — Azure Key Vault supports bring-your-own-key import from a supported HSM, and Managed HSM has a security domain you can hold. External key stores where the key never enters Azure are far more limited in service support — say what is and is not possible rather than promising.

Wrong answer: “We enable CMK on the storage account and give the customer access to the key vault.” One shared key means one customer's revocation breaks everyone, the vault lives in your tenant so custody is not really theirs, and nothing has been designed for the disabled-key state. The question is about the failure design as much as the cryptography.

12Cost management & FinOps

Cost questions appear in almost every lead and principal loop, because a senior engineer who cannot reason about spend is expensive. Screener depth is naming reservations and knowing where Cost Analysis lives; senior depth is finding and fixing a specific overspend with a number; principal depth is building accountability — showback, budgets, unit economics and the conversations that follow. The strongest answers are always the ones with arithmetic in them.

Docs: Cost Management · Savings plans · Reservations

Reservations, savings plans, spot and Hybrid Benefit — what are they and when do you use each? screener

Answer. Reserved instances commit you to a specific VM family, region and term (1 or 3 years) for the largest discount, with limited flexibility (instance-size flexibility within a family; exchange and refund rules have tightened). Savings plans for compute commit you to an hourly spend for 1 or 3 years and apply automatically across VM families, regions and even services (VMs, App Service, Container Instances, Functions Premium) — slightly less discount, much more flexibility, and the right default when your estate changes shape. Spot gives very large discounts for evictable capacity, right for batch, CI agents, and stateless workers that tolerate a 30-second eviction notice. Azure Hybrid Benefit is licensing rather than capacity: if you own Windows Server or SQL Server licences with Software Assurance, you stop paying for the licence component in Azure — frequently the single largest discount available to an enterprise, and the one candidates forget. They compose: apply Hybrid Benefit first, then cover the stable base with savings plans or reservations, then let the peak run at pay-as-you-go, and put anything interruptible on spot.

Example

Estate: 40 D8as_v5 running 24/7, peak +25 for 6h/day, 60 CI agents (bursty), 12 SQL Server VMs

Hybrid Benefit on the 12 SQL VMs and all Windows VMs   -> largest single line, applied first
Savings plan covering ~34 instances' hourly spend      -> base load, flexible across families
Pay-as-you-go for the remaining ~6 + the daily peak    -> keep headroom uncommitted
Spot for the 60 CI agents                              -> evictable by definition
Rule of thumb: commit to 60-70% of steady state, never to the peak, never to a workload
whose architecture you plan to change within the term.

Follow-ups

  • Reservation or savings plan? — Reservation when the workload is genuinely fixed in family and region for the term (a database VM, a stable production fleet). Savings plan when you expect to change SKUs, migrate to containers, or move regions. The extra few percent of discount is not worth stranding a commitment.
  • What happens if you under-use a commitment? — You pay for it regardless. Refund and exchange policies are limited, so an over-aggressive commitment is a real, non-recoverable loss — which is why the correct level is the base you are confident about, not the average.
  • How do you decide the spot bid and handle eviction? — Use the capacity-based eviction policy and price history for the SKU, design workers to checkpoint and to drain on the eviction event, and always keep a non-spot fallback pool for anything with a deadline.
  • Where else does Hybrid Benefit apply? — Windows and SQL Server on VMs, SQL Managed Instance and Azure SQL Database vCore tiers, and Linux subscriptions for RHEL/SUSE. Check what licences the company already owns before designing — it changes the comparison between IaaS and PaaS materially.

Wrong answer: “Buy 3-year reservations for everything to maximise the discount.” It locks the architecture you are about to change, strands money when you containerise or move region, and ignores that the flexible instrument exists. The interviewer is testing whether you weigh discount against optionality.

The Azure bill went up 40% this month. How do you find out why? mid

Answer. Work top-down by the dimensions the billing data actually has, and compare like for like. In Cost Analysis, group by service first over a daily granularity to see whether it is a step change (something new was deployed on a date) or a ramp (growth or a leak); then by resource group and resource to localise it; then by meter, which is where the real cause hides — “Storage” going up could be capacity, transactions, egress or restore operations, and only the meter tells you which. Check the usual suspects in parallel: a new environment nobody decommissioned, log ingestion, egress (especially cross-region traffic introduced by a deployment), a Cosmos or SQL tier change, orphaned resources (unattached disks, idle public IPs, empty App Service plans, unused gateways), and a reservation that expired. For anything beyond a quick look, export the cost data (the Cost Management export to storage, or the amortised cost dataset) and query it properly, because the portal's UI cannot answer “which resources grew most in absolute terms week over week” well.

Example

1. Cost Analysis, daily, group by ServiceName, last 60 days
     -> step change on 12 Aug: "Azure Monitor" from EUR 40/day to EUR 310/day
2. Group by ResourceId within that service
     -> one Log Analytics workspace
3. Group by Meter
     -> "Data Ingestion" - not retention, not queries
4. In the workspace: Usage | summarize by DataType
     -> AppTraces, 210 GB/day, from one AppRoleName
5. Root cause: a debug log level shipped on 12 Aug in a release
Fix: log level back to Information, DCR transformation dropping health-probe traces,
     alert on daily ingestion growth > 25% so the next one is caught in 24 hours.

Follow-ups

  • Actual or amortised cost? — Actual shows the reservation purchase as a lump on the day you bought it; amortised spreads it over the term and attributes it to the resources that used it. For per-team showback you want amortised; for cash flow, actual. Mixing them is how cost reports lose credibility.
  • Where does egress hide? — Cross-region traffic introduced by a resource moving or a dependency being added, NAT Gateway data processing, private endpoint data, and Front Door/CDN egress. It rarely appears as an obvious line and is often the fastest-growing one.
  • What are the classic orphans? — Unattached managed disks, public IPs with no association, empty App Service plans, idle gateways and firewalls in non-production, and old snapshots. A monthly Resource Graph query for these pays for itself.
  • How do you stop it recurring? — Budgets with action groups at 60/80/100% per subscription, an anomaly alert on the subscription, and a weekly cost delta report to owners. Detection latency is the metric — a 40% rise found in a month is a failure of tooling, not of the team that caused it.

Wrong answer: “Look at the invoice and see which subscription grew.” It is the first step and stops three levels above the cause; the invoice cannot tell you it was ingestion rather than retention. The interviewer is listening for “meter” and for a specific diagnostic path.

How do you attribute cost to teams and make them care? senior

Answer. Attribution comes from structure first and tags second: a subscription per workload per environment gives you unambiguous, un-typoable allocation for the majority of spend, and tags fill in the rest. Shared costs — the hub firewall, ExpressRoute, a shared AKS cluster, the Log Analytics workspace — need an explicit, published allocation rule (by node hours, by ingestion volume, by request count, or a flat platform charge), because arguing about them monthly destroys trust in the whole model. Then make it visible and repeated: a monthly showback per team with the trend and the top five movers, a budget per subscription with alerts routed to the team rather than to finance, and — the part that actually changes behaviour — a unit cost metric such as cost per thousand orders or cost per active tenant, because absolute cost rising while the business grows is fine and only the unit metric tells you whether efficiency is improving. Add a lightweight optimisation ritual (a monthly review of Advisor recommendations and the top movers, with owners and dates) and make the savings visible to the team that earned them.

Example

Direct (85% of spend)      subscription -> team, unambiguous
Shared (15%)               published rule, reviewed twice a year:
  hub firewall + ER        by spoke count, weighted by data processed
  Log Analytics            by ingested GB per resource (already attributable)
  AKS platform cluster     by namespace CPU+memory requests x hours
  platform team overhead   flat per-workload charge, disclosed
Reported monthly:  spend, delta vs last month, top 5 movers, unit cost (EUR / 1k orders),
                   and the three Advisor recommendations worth the most.
Escalation: a budget breach is a conversation with the owning team's lead, not a finance email.

Follow-ups

  • Showback or chargeback? — Showback (visibility without a ledger transfer) changes behaviour in most organisations at a fraction of the administrative cost. Chargeback is worth it when teams have real budget authority and can trade cloud spend against other spending; otherwise it generates accounting theatre.
  • How do you split a shared Kubernetes cluster? — By namespace resource requests × hours, which is the number teams can actually control, plus a share of the cluster overhead. Tools in the OpenCost/Kubecost family do this; the important part is publishing the method, not the tool.
  • What is the risk of unit-cost metrics? — Choosing a denominator that does not track value (requests, when the business cares about orders) and then optimising the wrong thing. Pick the denominator with the product owner.
  • How do you handle non-production spend? — Auto-shutdown schedules, cheaper SKUs by policy, scale-to-zero services, and a hard budget with an actual enforcement action. Non-production is usually 20–40% of spend and almost always the easiest place to save real money.

Wrong answer: “We tag everything with a cost centre and send finance the report.” Tag compliance is never complete, finance cannot action an engineering cost, and nothing reaches the person who wrote the loop that costs money. Attribution only works when the number lands in front of the team that can change it, repeatedly.

Where does Azure spend usually leak, and what do you fix first? senior

Answer. In roughly descending order of frequency: non-production running at production hours and sizes (nothing shuts down at night); over-provisioned compute sized for a peak that never arrives, with no autoscale; log and telemetry ingestion from debug logging and un-tuned diagnostic categories; orphaned resources — unattached disks, idle public IPs, empty plans, forgotten gateways and old snapshots; storage in the wrong tier with no lifecycle policy, plus versions and snapshots nobody counts; egress and cross-region traffic introduced accidentally by a dependency; and expired or unapplied commitments. What to fix first is whichever combines a large number with a low risk, which is nearly always non-production schedules and orphans — they are days of work and no architectural risk. Then autoscale and right-sizing, guided by measured P95 utilisation rather than by feel. Leave the architectural changes (moving to consumption pricing, re-platforming) for when the easy money is gone, and always land a control alongside each fix — a policy, a schedule, an alert — so the saving does not decay back over two quarters.

Example

// orphans across the estate, in one query
resources
| where type == 'microsoft.compute/disks' and properties.diskState == 'Unattached'
    or (type == 'microsoft.network/publicipaddresses' and isnull(properties.ipConfiguration))
| project type, name, resourceGroup, subscriptionId,
          sizeGb = properties.diskSizeGB, sku = sku.name, tags
| order by type asc

// empty App Service plans still billing
resources
| where type == 'microsoft.web/serverfarms'
| where properties.numberOfSites == 0 and sku.tier !in ('Free','Dynamic')
| project name, resourceGroup, sku = sku.name, capacity = sku.capacity

Follow-ups

  • How much is typically recoverable? — In an estate that has never been optimised, 20–30% without architectural change is a realistic first pass. Claiming much more usually means counting savings that will be re-spent on growth.
  • What is the risk of aggressive right-sizing? — Cutting headroom you needed for a seasonal peak or a failover. Right-size against P95 over a period that includes your peak, keep failover capacity out of the exercise, and change one dimension at a time.
  • How do you keep savings from decaying? — Every fix ships with a control: a policy denying expensive SKUs in non-production, an auto-shutdown schedule applied by policy, a budget alert, a monthly orphan report. Without that, the same money reappears within two quarters.
  • Does Advisor find all of this? — It finds a good share (right-sizing, idle resources, reservation recommendations) and misses the workload-specific things — log volume, egress patterns, tier choices. Use it as a baseline, not as the programme.

Wrong answer: “Move everything to serverless and consumption pricing to only pay for what we use.” For steady load, consumption pricing is usually more expensive than committed capacity, and the migration cost is real. Consumption wins for spiky and low-average workloads; presenting it as a universal saving shows you have not modelled it.

Leadership asks you to cut cloud spend 30% in a quarter. What do you do? principal

Answer. Separate what is achievable safely in a quarter from what is not, and say so with numbers rather than accepting or refusing the target. The sequence: week 1, get the data — amortised cost by service, resource and team, plus utilisation — and produce a ranked list of opportunities with an estimated saving, an effort, and a risk for each. Weeks 2–4, execute the zero-risk tier: non-production schedules and decommissioning, orphan cleanup, storage lifecycle and tiering, log ingestion reduction, Hybrid Benefit anywhere it is not applied. That alone is often 10–20%. Weeks 4–8, the low-risk tier: right-sizing against measured utilisation, autoscale where it is missing, and commitment purchases on the base load — noting that a savings plan reduces the run rate immediately but is itself a cash commitment, which finance must agree. Weeks 8–12, the architectural tier, of which only the small items land in the quarter. Then the honest part: name what you would have to stop doing to reach the remainder, and what you would be trading — reliability tiers, environments, retention, or roadmap. A principal-level answer includes the sentence “here is the last 8% and here is what it costs us”.

Example

ActionEst. savingEffortRiskLands
Non-prod shutdown schedules + decommission 3 stale environments9%1 weeknonewk 2
Log ingestion: levels, DCR transforms, Basic-tier tables5%1 weeklowwk 3
Orphans + storage lifecycle + snapshot cleanup3%3 daysnonewk 3
Hybrid Benefit applied to 12 unlicensed VMs4%2 daysnonewk 4
Right-size + autoscale on 3 fleets (P95-based)6%3 weeksmediumwk 8
Savings plan on the stable base5%1 weekcommitmentwk 9
Total safely achievable~32%wk 9

Follow-ups

  • What if the safe list only reaches 18%? — Then present the gap with options and their costs: drop a DR region for tier-3 systems, shorten log retention, consolidate environments, delay a capacity purchase. Let leadership choose the trade rather than choosing it silently — and get the decision in writing, because it will be revisited after the next incident.
  • How do you avoid breaking things while moving fast? — Change one dimension at a time, non-production first, with an explicit rollback and a monitoring window per change. A cost programme that causes an outage loses its mandate immediately.
  • How do you keep the team engaged? — Publish the running total and attribute wins to the people who found them. Cost work is unglamorous, and visible progress is what sustains it past week four.
  • What do you do about the commitment decision? — Bring finance in early: a savings plan is a balance-sheet commitment, not an engineering decision, and buying one without them is how a cost programme creates a different problem.

Wrong answer: “We'll migrate to reserved instances and containerise workloads to hit the target.” Containerisation does not save money in a quarter and often costs more during migration; reservations reduce run rate but require the workloads to be stable, which they will not be if you are simultaneously re-platforming them. The two halves of that answer contradict each other, and interviewers notice.

13AKS & containers

AKS questions appear whenever the team runs Kubernetes, and they are Azure-specific: networking modes, identity, upgrades, scaling and the managed add-ons — the generic Kubernetes questions belong in a Kubernetes interview. Screener depth is knowing what AKS manages for you; senior depth is CNI choice, workload identity, upgrade strategy and autoscaling; principal depth is multi-tenancy, platform ownership and the honest cost of running clusters. Currency matters here: node auto-provisioning with Karpenter reached GA in early 2026 and AKS Automatic is GA.

Docs: Azure Kubernetes Service · Node auto-provisioning · Workload identity

What does AKS actually manage, and what are you still responsible for? screener

Answer. Microsoft manages the control plane — API server, etcd, scheduler, controller manager — and it is free on the Free tier, with the Standard tier adding a financially-backed SLA and higher scale, and Premium adding long-term support for a Kubernetes version. You are responsible for everything on and around the nodes: node pool sizing and SKUs, node image and Kubernetes version upgrades (AKS provides the mechanism, you own the cadence and the disruption budget), workload configuration, networking mode, ingress, storage classes, RBAC inside the cluster, cost, and every add-on's behaviour. The nuance worth stating is that “managed” ends at the API server: a cluster left alone will fall out of the supported version window within about a year, since Azure supports roughly the three most recent minor versions with about a twelve-month window, and an unsupported cluster loses support and eventually gets force-upgraded. AKS Automatic (GA) moves a large part of this back to Microsoft — node auto-provisioning, auto-upgrades, preconfigured monitoring, security defaults — and is now the sensible default for teams that do not want to own the cluster's operations.

Example

ConcernAKS StandardAKS Automatic
Control planeMicrosoftMicrosoft
Node provisioningyou (node pools / cluster autoscaler)NAP (Karpenter), preconfigured
Version upgradesyou choose and scheduleauto-upgrade channels by default
Monitoring & Prometheusyou enableon by default
Security defaultsyou configureEntra + Azure RBAC, policy, workload identity on
Escape hatchfull controlfewer knobs by design

Follow-ups

  • What is the version support window? — Roughly the three most recent minor versions, with about twelve months of support per version; the Premium tier adds Long Term Support for two years on designated versions. As of 2026, LTS 1.29 is deprecated and 1.32 is available through LTS — clusters below the window are a live risk, not a backlog item.
  • What breaks if you never upgrade? — You lose support and security patches, then Azure force-upgrades to a supported version, which is exactly the unplanned disruption you were avoiding. Upgrade cadence is a commitment you make when you choose AKS.
  • Free, Standard or Premium tier? — Free for dev and experiments; Standard for anything production (SLA and scale); Premium only when you genuinely need to stay on a version for two years, usually for a certified or slow-moving workload.
  • When is AKS Automatic the wrong choice? — When you need capabilities its opinionated configuration removes — specific CNI configurations, unusual node customisation, or an existing platform built around your own provisioning. For a new platform, start with Automatic and step down only when something blocks you.

Wrong answer: “AKS is managed Kubernetes, so Microsoft handles operations and we just deploy.” The control plane is managed; the upgrade treadmill, node patching, capacity, cost and every add-on are yours. This answer is the single clearest signal that a candidate has used AKS in a demo and not run one for a year.

Azure CNI, CNI Overlay or kubenet — which networking mode and why? mid

Answer. Azure CNI Overlay is the current default recommendation: nodes get IPs from your VNet subnet, pods get IPs from a separate overlay CIDR that is not consumed from the VNet, so a large cluster costs a node-sized subnet rather than a pod-sized one, and the same overlay range can be reused across clusters. Pods can reach VNet resources through the node's identity via NAT, and the scale limits are high. Azure CNI (flat / VNet-integrated) gives every pod a real VNet IP, so pods are directly routable and reachable from outside the cluster and can be targeted by NSGs individually — the right choice when something outside must connect to a pod directly, and the wrong one if you have not planned an enormous address space, because IPs are pre-allocated per node by max-pods. Kubenet is legacy — simple, IP-cheap, route-table based, with scale and feature limitations — and is on its way out; finding it in an existing cluster means planning a migration, which requires recreating node pools. Alongside the mode, choose the data plane: Azure CNI powered by Cilium gives eBPF-based routing, better performance at scale and network-policy enforcement in the data plane, and is the modern choice for new clusters.

Example

az aks create -g rg-platform -n aks-prod \
  --network-plugin azure --network-plugin-mode overlay \
  --network-dataplane cilium \
  --pod-cidr 10.244.0.0/16 \            # overlay: NOT from the VNet, reusable per cluster
  --vnet-subnet-id <snet-nodes /22> \   # only nodes consume VNet IPs
  --network-policy cilium \
  --enable-managed-identity --enable-oidc-issuer --enable-workload-identity \
  --outbound-type userDefinedRouting     # egress via the hub firewall

Follow-ups

  • How many VNet IPs does flat Azure CNI consume? — Roughly (max-pods + 1) per node, pre-allocated whether or not the pods exist. With max-pods 30 and 100 nodes that is over 3,000 addresses for one cluster, before scaling headroom — which is how teams eat a /20 by accident.
  • Do network policies work in every mode? — Policy support depends on the plugin and data plane; Cilium and Azure Network Policy Manager both work with CNI, and Calico is the third option. Choose it at cluster creation, because changing it later means recreating the cluster or the node pools.
  • What about private clusters? — A private API server endpoint removes the public control-plane address, which most enterprises require; the cost is that your CI runners and admin workstations need a network path (private endpoint, VPN, or the AKS command invoke feature).
  • How do pods reach PaaS services privately? — Private endpoints resolved through the VNet's private DNS zones, exactly as for any other workload; the pods' overlay addresses are irrelevant because egress is SNATed to the node. This is the answer to “can overlay pods use private link?” — yes.

Wrong answer: “Use Azure CNI so pods get real IPs — it's the more capable option.” It is more capable in one narrow sense (direct pod addressability) and it costs you an address plan that most estates cannot afford. Unless you can name the requirement that needs routable pods, Overlay is the better default and saying so is the current answer.

How does a pod authenticate to Azure services? mid

Answer. Microsoft Entra Workload ID, which uses the cluster's OIDC issuer and federated identity credentials. Mechanically: enable the OIDC issuer and workload identity on the cluster; create a user-assigned managed identity and add a federated credential trusting system:serviceaccount:<namespace>:<name> from that issuer; annotate the Kubernetes service account with the identity's client ID; label the pod so the webhook injects the projected service account token and the environment variables; and the Azure SDK's WorkloadIdentityCredential exchanges the projected token for an Azure access token. No secret, no node-level identity, and — critically — the identity is scoped to a service account, so two workloads on the same node have different permissions. The predecessor, AAD Pod Identity, intercepted IMDS traffic with a node-level component and has been deprecated and retired in favour of this; finding it in a cluster is a migration item. The failure you will debug most often is a mismatch between the federated credential's subject and the actual namespace/service-account pair.

Example

export ISSUER=$(az aks show -g rg-platform -n aks-prod --query oidcIssuerProfile.issuerUrl -o tsv)
az identity federated-credential create --name orders-api \
  --identity-name id-orders --resource-group rg-identity \
  --issuer "$ISSUER" --subject "system:serviceaccount:orders:orders-api" \
  --audiences api://AzureADTokenExchange
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: orders-api
  namespace: orders
  annotations:
    azure.workload.identity/client-id: "8f1c-....-b204"
---
spec:
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: { azure.workload.identity/use: "true" }   # without this, no token is injected
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: orders-api

Follow-ups

  • What is the most common failure? — The subject string not matching exactly (wrong namespace, wrong service account name) producing AADSTS70021, or the pod label missing so no token is projected and the SDK silently falls back to another credential in the chain — which is why pinning the credential type in production is worth doing.
  • How does this compare to a node-level managed identity? — The kubelet identity is shared by every pod on the node, so any pod can use it. That is the security problem workload identity exists to solve, and it is why AAD Pod Identity's IMDS interception was a stopgap.
  • How do you pull images from ACR? — Attach the registry to the cluster so the kubelet identity has AcrPull, which is a node-level concern and appropriate there. Application access to Azure data services stays on workload identity.
  • What about secrets in the cluster? — The Secrets Store CSI driver with the Key Vault provider mounts secrets from Key Vault into pods using the same workload identity, so you avoid Kubernetes Secrets (which are only base64-encoded in etcd unless you configure encryption).

Wrong answer: “Use AAD Pod Identity — assign the managed identity to the pod with an AzureIdentityBinding.” It is deprecated and retired, and it worked by intercepting node-level IMDS traffic, which was both fragile and a security compromise. Naming it as the current approach is a clear currency signal.

How do you scale an AKS cluster, and what is node auto-provisioning? senior

Answer. Three independent layers. Pod-level: the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler on CPU/memory or custom metrics, and KEDA (a managed add-on) for event-driven scaling on queue length, Event Hubs lag, Service Bus depth or a cron — KEDA is what lets a consumer scale to zero and back, which HPA alone cannot. Node-level, classic: the cluster autoscaler adds and removes nodes within fixed node pools, so you choose the VM SKUs in advance and the autoscaler only changes the count. Node-level, modern: node auto-provisioning, which is Karpenter's Azure provider managed by AKS — it looks at pending pods and provisions right-sized nodes from a permitted SKU family list, consolidates underused nodes, and handles spot. NAP reached GA on AKS in early 2026 and is preconfigured in AKS Automatic; it removes most node-pool capacity planning and typically improves bin-packing and cost. Whatever the layer, the two things that make scaling behave are accurate resource requests (the scheduler and every autoscaler reason from requests, not from usage) and PodDisruptionBudgets, without which consolidation and upgrades will evict more than you intended.

Example

# NAP: describe the shape of nodes you will accept, not the pools
apiVersion: karpenter.sh/v1
kind: NodePool
metadata: { name: general }
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      requirements:
        - { key: karpenter.sh/capacity-type, operator: In, values: ["on-demand"] }
        - { key: karpenter.azure.com/sku-family, operator: In, values: ["D","E"] }
        - { key: kubernetes.io/arch, operator: In, values: ["amd64"] }
      nodeClassRef: { name: default, group: karpenter.azure.com, kind: AKSNodeClass }
  disruption:
    consolidationPolicy: WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized
    consolidateAfter: 5m
  limits: { cpu: "400" }

Follow-ups

  • Why do resource requests matter so much? — They are the only input to scheduling and to autoscaler decisions. Requests set far above real usage waste money on nodes you do not need; requests far below cause noisy-neighbour CPU throttling and OOM kills. Measure and set them per workload — this is where most cluster cost lives.
  • What does KEDA add over HPA? — Scaling on external event sources and scale-to-zero. A worker consuming a Service Bus queue can sit at zero replicas overnight and scale on queue depth, which HPA cannot express because there is no pod metric when there are no pods.
  • Should you use spot node pools? — For interruption-tolerant workloads, with taints and tolerations so only opt-in pods land there, plus a PDB and a non-spot pool for the rest. NAP can mix spot and on-demand by requirement, which makes this much easier than it used to be.
  • What is the risk of aggressive consolidation? — Frequent pod churn and rescheduling under load. Tune consolidateAfter, set PDBs that reflect real availability needs, and make sure workloads terminate gracefully — otherwise cost optimisation shows up as latency spikes.

Wrong answer: “Turn on the cluster autoscaler with min 3 max 100 and it handles everything.” The cluster autoscaler only adds nodes of the SKU you already chose, does nothing about wrong resource requests, and will not consolidate a badly bin-packed cluster. Scaling is three layers and the pod layer is where most of the problems are.

Design an AKS upgrade strategy for a cluster running 40 production services. senior

Answer. Two separate cadences with different risk. Node image upgrades are frequent (Microsoft ships them roughly weekly with security patches), low-risk, and should be automated — the NodeImage auto-upgrade channel with a planned maintenance window during low traffic. Kubernetes version upgrades are minor-version steps you must take at least a few times a year to stay in support; those get a pipeline: read the release notes and deprecated-API list, run az aks get-upgrades, upgrade a non-production cluster of identical configuration first, scan manifests for removed APIs, then upgrade production control plane first and node pools afterwards, one pool at a time with surge nodes so capacity does not dip. Protect the workloads throughout with PodDisruptionBudgets, meaningful readiness probes and graceful termination, because a rolling node upgrade is a continuous eviction test. Choose between in-place upgrades (simple, no extra address space, but the cluster is the blast radius) and blue-green clusters (build a new cluster, shift traffic at the ingress or Front Door, keep the old one for rollback) — blue-green is the right answer for a cluster hosting 40 services with a hard availability requirement, at the cost of doubled capacity during the window and a data/state migration story.

Example

# cadence 1: node images, automated, in a maintenance window
az aks update -g rg-platform -n aks-prod --auto-upgrade-channel nodeimage
az aks maintenanceconfiguration add -g rg-platform --cluster-name aks-prod -n default \
  --weekday Sunday --start-hour 2

# cadence 2: Kubernetes version, deliberate
az aks get-upgrades -g rg-platform -n aks-prod -o table
kubectl get --raw /metrics | grep apiserver_requested_deprecated_apis   # who still calls removed APIs?
az aks upgrade -g rg-platform -n aks-prod --kubernetes-version 1.33.x --control-plane-only
az aks nodepool upgrade -g rg-platform --cluster-name aks-prod -n system --max-surge 33%
az aks nodepool upgrade -g rg-platform --cluster-name aks-prod -n user1  --max-surge 33%

Follow-ups

  • How do you find deprecated API usage before it breaks? — The API server's deprecated-API metrics and audit logs, plus static scanning of manifests and Helm charts (kubent-style tooling) in CI. AKS also surfaces deprecation warnings in the portal and blocks some upgrades until you acknowledge them.
  • What does max-surge do? — Adds temporary extra nodes so pods move before capacity is removed; higher surge is faster and needs more quota and IPs. 33% is a reasonable production default; 1 node is the slow, safe default that makes a large pool take all night.
  • How do PDBs interfere? — A PDB that cannot be satisfied blocks draining indefinitely, so an upgrade hangs. Single-replica deployments with minAvailable: 1 are the classic cause; the fix is two replicas, not removing the PDB.
  • Blue-green: what is the hard part? — Anything stateful or cluster-scoped — persistent volumes, cluster-wide operators, webhook configurations, and DNS or ingress IP changes. It is genuinely simpler for stateless services and genuinely painful otherwise, and saying which you have is the honest answer.

Wrong answer: “Enable the stable auto-upgrade channel and let Azure handle Kubernetes versions too.” Automatic minor-version upgrades on a 40-service production cluster will one day remove an API a chart depends on, during a window you did not choose. Automate node images; make version upgrades deliberate and rehearsed.

One big cluster or many? Design multi-tenancy for a platform serving twelve teams. principal

Answer. The axis is trust and blast radius, not efficiency. Namespace-per-team on shared clusters gives the best utilisation and the lowest operational load, and is appropriate when all tenants are internal and mutually trusted: enforce it with namespace-scoped RBAC (Entra groups mapped through Azure RBAC for Kubernetes), ResourceQuotas and LimitRanges, network policies defaulting to deny, Gatekeeper/Azure Policy for admission control, and per-namespace cost attribution. Cluster-per-team or per-domain costs more (control planes are cheap, but the platform work multiplies) and buys real isolation of upgrades, CRDs, cluster-scoped resources and failure domains — the right answer when teams need conflicting cluster-level configuration, or when one team's incident must not touch another's. In practice most mature estates land on a small number of clusters segmented by environment and criticality rather than by team: a production cluster per region, a non-production cluster, and a separate cluster for anything with a different compliance boundary. The principal-level point is that a shared cluster makes the platform team the owner of every tenant's availability, so the model you choose must match the on-call you can actually staff.

Example

Chosen shape: 2 prod clusters (weu, neu) + 1 non-prod + 1 regulated, namespaces per service
Per namespace, applied by the platform's own pipeline (not by teams):
  RoleBinding      grp-orders-engineers -> edit in ns/orders (Azure RBAC for Kubernetes)
  ResourceQuota    cpu 40 / mem 160Gi / pods 200 / pvc 20
  LimitRange       default requests+limits so an unset pod cannot take a node
  NetworkPolicy    default-deny ingress+egress; explicit allows to ns/shared and PaaS CIDRs
  PDB required     admission policy rejects Deployments with replicas>1 and no PDB
Cluster-scoped things teams may NOT create: CRDs, webhooks, ClusterRoles, PriorityClasses
Escape hatch: a team needing those gets its own cluster, provisioned by the same pipeline.

Follow-ups

  • What is the noisy-neighbour risk? — CPU throttling and memory pressure from workloads with no limits, and a single pod exhausting node-level resources like ephemeral storage or PIDs. Quotas, LimitRanges and defaulting admission policies are the controls; without them, one team's bug is everyone's incident.
  • How do you isolate untrusted workloads? — Separate node pools with taints, or a separate cluster. Kubernetes namespaces are not a security boundary against hostile code; if the tenant is untrusted, the answer is cluster-level or stronger isolation (confidential containers, or a separate subscription).
  • Who is on call for the cluster? — The platform team for the cluster and the add-ons; the service team for their workloads. Write the boundary down and give teams the observability to diagnose their own side, or every incident starts with a triage argument.
  • How do you charge for it? — Namespace CPU+memory requests × hours plus a share of overhead, published monthly. It also aligns incentives: teams that set honest requests pay less, which is the behaviour you want.

Wrong answer: “One cluster per team — it's cleaner and the control plane is free.” The control plane is cheap; twelve upgrade cadences, twelve add-on estates, twelve monitoring configurations and twelve sets of node capacity are not. The cost of a cluster is the platform work around it, and that is the number the question is really asking about.

14Serverless & the app platform

This unit is the deep end of what unit 02 chose: once you have picked Functions, App Service or Container Apps, interviewers probe how the runtime actually behaves — triggers and bindings, scaling internals, cold start, orchestration, and configuration. Screener depth is trigger types; senior depth is concurrency control and stateful workflow design; principal depth is the boundary between orchestration and application logic, and when serverless stops being the right shape.

Docs: Azure Functions · Durable Functions · Container Apps

Explain triggers and bindings, and where they help or hurt. screener

Answer. A trigger is what causes a function to run — HTTP, timer, queue, Service Bus, Event Hubs, Event Grid, Cosmos DB change feed, blob — and each function has exactly one. Bindings are declarative connections to data: an input binding fetches something before your code runs, an output binding writes something after it returns, and they remove a lot of boilerplate SDK code. Where they help: the platform handles connection management, batching, checkpointing and retries, and a queue-triggered function with a Cosmos output binding is genuinely a few lines. Where they hurt: bindings hide the failure semantics — an output binding writes after your function returns, so you cannot participate in a transaction, handle a partial failure, or implement a compensating action; they are hard to unit test in isolation; and they can obscure the retry and poison-message behaviour you need to reason about. The mature position is to use triggers everywhere (they are the platform's scaling contract) and to use bindings for straightforward reads and fire-and-forget writes, dropping to the SDK when you need explicit control over ordering, transactions or error handling.

Example

[Function("ProcessOrder")]
[CosmosDBOutput("orders", "processed", Connection = "CosmosConnection")]
public async Task<ProcessedOrder> Run(
    [ServiceBusTrigger("orders", Connection = "SbConnection")] Order order,
    FunctionContext ctx)
{
    // trigger: the platform owns receive, lock renewal, retry, dead-letter
    // output binding: written AFTER this returns - so if the write fails, the message is
    // already completed and the work is lost. Fine for idempotent projections;
    // NOT fine when the write is the effect that must not be lost.
    return await handler.ProcessAsync(order, ctx.CancellationToken);
}

Follow-ups

  • How do connections work with managed identity? — Identity-based connections: instead of Connection pointing at a connection string, point it at a settings prefix with __fullyQualifiedNamespace or __serviceUri and the runtime uses the app's managed identity. This is the current recommended pattern and it removes the last connection strings from a function app.
  • What retries do triggers give you? — It depends on the trigger: Service Bus and queues retry via the broker's delivery count and dead-letter; Event Hubs does not retry individually because it checkpoints by offset; HTTP does not retry at all. The function-level retry policy was deprecated for most triggers precisely because it conflicted with the broker's semantics — know which layer owns the retry.
  • How do you test a function with bindings? — Extract the logic into a plain class the function calls, and unit-test that; test the binding wiring with an integration test. A function whose body is business logic is hard to test by construction.
  • What is the blob trigger caveat? — The classic (polling/log-scan) blob trigger can be delayed by minutes on large containers; the Event Grid-based blob trigger is the current recommendation for low latency and reliability at scale.

Wrong answer: “Use output bindings for everything — they're transactional with the trigger.” There is no transaction: the trigger completes and the output is written separately, so a failed write after a completed message loses data. This is one of the most consequential misunderstandings in Functions, and interviewers ask it in exactly this shape.

What are Durable Functions and when would you use them? mid

Answer. Durable Functions add stateful orchestration on top of Functions: an orchestrator function describes a workflow in code, calling activity functions, and the framework persists progress so the orchestration can survive process restarts and run for days. It works by event sourcing — the orchestrator is replayed from a history of completed events each time it resumes, which is why orchestrator code must be deterministic: no DateTime.Now, no random numbers, no direct IO, no non-deterministic collection ordering; use the context's equivalents. It gives you the standard patterns cleanly: function chaining, fan-out/fan-in with a join, async HTTP with a status endpoint, human interaction with a durable timer and an external event, and the saga/compensation pattern. Entities add durable, addressable state with serialised access, which is useful for per-key counters and small aggregates. Use it when a process has multiple steps, waits, timeouts or compensation; do not use it as a general application framework, and do not use it when a single function or a workflow product (Logic Apps) fits better.

Example

[Function(nameof(OrderSaga))]
public static async Task<string> OrderSaga(
    [OrchestrationTrigger] TaskOrchestrationContext ctx)
{
    var order = ctx.GetInput<Order>()!;
    var reserved = await ctx.CallActivityAsync<bool>(nameof(ReserveStock), order);
    if (!reserved) return "OutOfStock";

    try
    {
        await ctx.CallActivityAsync(nameof(ChargeCard), order);
    }
    catch (TaskFailedException)
    {
        await ctx.CallActivityAsync(nameof(ReleaseStock), order);   // compensation
        return "PaymentFailed";
    }

    // wait up to 72h for a human approval, then time out - a durable timer, not Task.Delay
    using var cts = new CancellationTokenSource();
    var approval = ctx.WaitForExternalEvent<bool>("Approved");
    var timeout  = ctx.CreateTimer(ctx.CurrentUtcDateTime.AddHours(72), cts.Token);
    var winner   = await Task.WhenAny(approval, timeout);
    cts.Cancel();
    return winner == approval && approval.Result ? "Shipped" : "Expired";
}

Follow-ups

  • Why must orchestrators be deterministic? — On every resume the orchestrator replays its history to rebuild state; non-deterministic code produces a different execution path and the framework detects (or worse, silently mis-handles) the divergence. ctx.CurrentUtcDateTime and ctx.NewGuid() exist for this reason.
  • What is the storage cost? — History and state live in the storage account (or the newer Durable Task Scheduler / netherite backends), and a chatty orchestration with thousands of activities generates a lot of transactions. Fan-out width and history size are real cost and performance dimensions.
  • Durable Functions or Logic Apps? — Logic Apps for integration-shaped workflows with many connectors and a designer audience; Durable for logic that belongs in your codebase, in version control, with unit tests. Choosing by who maintains it is a better rule than choosing by capability.
  • How do you version an orchestration in flight? — Carefully: changing orchestrator code while instances are running can break replay. The standard approaches are side-by-side versioned orchestrator names, or draining instances before deploying a breaking change — and this is the most common production problem with Durable.

Wrong answer: “Use Task.Delay in the orchestrator to wait for the approval window.” It blocks a worker, does not survive a restart, and breaks replay determinism. CreateTimer exists precisely because the wait must be durable — and this is the single most common Durable Functions mistake.

How do you control concurrency so a function app doesn't overwhelm its dependencies? senior

Answer. Bound it at three levels, because scaling out is the default and the database is not elastic. Platform level: cap the maximum instance count on the plan (or per-function scale limits on Flex Consumption), so the app physically cannot exceed a known fan-out. Host level: the per-trigger concurrency settings in host.json — maxConcurrentCalls and maxConcurrentSessions for Service Bus, batchSize and newBatchThreshold for queues, maxEventBatchSize for Event Hubs, and maxConcurrentRequests for HTTP — which control how much runs inside each instance. Multiply the two and you have the real ceiling: 100 instances × 32 concurrent calls is 3,200 simultaneous database connections, which is how a 200-connection pool dies. Application level: connection pooling with a bounded size, a semaphore around the scarce resource, retries with exponential backoff and jitter, and a circuit breaker so a struggling dependency gets less traffic rather than more. Then measure the actual dependency's capacity and size the ceiling to it rather than to your throughput ambition.

Example

// host.json - the per-instance half of the arithmetic
{ "version": "2.0",
  "extensions": {
    "serviceBus": { "messageHandlerOptions": { "maxConcurrentCalls": 16, "maxAutoRenewDuration": "00:10:00" },
                    "sessionHandlerOptions": { "maxConcurrentSessions": 8 } },
    "http": { "maxConcurrentRequests": 100, "maxOutstandingRequests": 200 }
  } }
# the platform half - the ceiling nobody can exceed
az functionapp scale config set -g rg-orders -n func-orders --maximum-instance-count 20
# 20 instances x 16 concurrent calls = 320 concurrent handlers.
# SQL pool is 100 connections -> either raise the pool, or lower the ceiling. Do the arithmetic.

Follow-ups

  • What is the symptom of getting this wrong? — Timeouts and connection-pool exhaustion under load while the database's own CPU is moderate, or a downstream API returning 429 in a rising spiral as retries pile on. The tell is that adding capacity makes it worse.
  • Why jitter in the backoff? — Without it, every instance retries at the same moment and you rebuild the thundering herd you were trying to avoid. Full jitter is the standard, and it is a one-line change most codebases are missing.
  • Where does a circuit breaker fit? — Around the dependency call, tripping on a failure rate and shedding fast for a cooldown. It converts a slow cascading failure into a fast, contained one, and it gives the dependency room to recover — which retries alone never do.
  • How does this interact with queue-based load levelling? — The queue absorbs the spike; the concurrency ceiling controls the drain rate. That combination is exactly how you protect a fixed-capacity dependency from a variable-rate producer, and it is the answer to “why not just autoscale?”

Wrong answer: “Let it scale — that's the point of serverless; the database will scale too.” The database will not, and the failure mode is that your elastic tier converts a manageable backlog into a total outage of a shared resource. Bounding concurrency in front of a fixed-capacity dependency is the whole skill here.

How does configuration work across App Service, Functions and Container Apps? mid

Answer. All three surface configuration as environment variables, with platform-specific layers on top.
