App Service and Functions: application settings (environment variables, with the hierarchical __ separator mapping to nested configuration in .NET), connection strings, slot-specific ("sticky") settings that do not travel on a swap, and Key Vault references resolved by the platform using the app's managed identity. Container Apps: environment variables plus secrets stored on the app (or referenced from Key Vault), injected into containers and referenced by name; configuration changes create a new revision, which is a meaningful difference — the change is versioned and rollback is a traffic shift. Azure App Configuration sits above all of them as a central store with labels per environment, feature flags with filters and percentage rollouts, and change notification via Event Grid so apps refresh without a restart — the right answer when the same settings must be shared across many services or when you want feature flags separated from deployment. The critical operational point is to know which changes restart the app: on App Service, changing an app setting restarts it; on Container Apps, it creates a revision.

Example

builder.Configuration.AddAzureAppConfiguration(o => o
    .Connect(new Uri(cfg["AppConfig:Endpoint"]!), new DefaultAzureCredential())
    .Select(KeyFilter.Any, LabelFilter.Null)          // defaults
    .Select(KeyFilter.Any, builder.Environment.EnvironmentName)   // env overrides
    .ConfigureKeyVault(kv => kv.SetCredential(new DefaultAzureCredential()))
    .ConfigureRefresh(r => r.Register("Sentinel", refreshAll: true)
                            .SetRefreshInterval(TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30)))
    .UseFeatureFlags());
// change the "Sentinel" key to publish a coordinated config change with no restart

Follow-ups

  • What should never be an app setting? — Anything secret that could be a managed identity instead, and anything large. App settings are visible to anyone with management access to the app, so they are not a secret store — Key Vault references are the mitigation.
  • How do you avoid a config change taking down production? — Treat configuration as a deployment: version it, review it, roll it out progressively (labels per environment, feature flags with percentage filters), and have a rollback. Container Apps revisions give you this natively; App Service gives it to you through slots.
  • What are sticky settings for? — Anything that must stay with the slot rather than the code: the environment name, a slot-specific database, the Application Insights connection for a staging environment. Getting this wrong means a swap points production at the staging database.
  • Feature flags in App Configuration or in code? — In App Configuration when you want to change behaviour without a deploy and to target percentages or user segments; in code when the flag is short-lived and tied to a release. Either way, flags need an expiry and an owner, or they become permanent branching.

Wrong answer: “Put everything in app settings — it's the simplest and it's encrypted at rest.” It scatters configuration across dozens of apps with no shared source of truth, no history, no environment labels, and a restart on every change. It is fine for one app and untenable for twenty.

Container Apps: explain revisions, ingress and scaling. senior

Answer. A Container App runs inside an environment — the boundary that provides the VNet, the shared Log Analytics workspace and internal DNS, and the unit that apps in it can call each other through. Each deployment or config change creates an immutable revision; in multiple revision mode you can run several at once and split traffic by weight, which gives you blue-green and canary without any extra infrastructure, and rollback is a weight change. Ingress is either external (a managed FQDN with TLS) or internal (reachable only within the environment), with support for HTTP, HTTP/2, gRPC and arbitrary TCP, plus session affinity and IP restrictions. Scaling is KEDA underneath: scale rules on HTTP concurrency, CPU/memory, or any KEDA scaler (Service Bus queue depth, Event Hubs lag, cron), with minReplicas: 0 giving true scale-to-zero for event-driven apps — noting that a zero-replica HTTP app pays a cold start on the next request, which is what the Dedicated workload profiles and a minimum replica of one are for. Jobs cover the run-to-completion case with schedule, event and manual triggers.

Example

# canary: 90/10 by weight, rollback is one command
az containerapp update -n orders -g rg-orders --image acr.azurecr.io/orders:2026.09.2 \
  --revision-suffix v20260902
az containerapp ingress traffic set -n orders -g rg-orders \
  --revision-weight orders--v20260901=90 orders--v20260902=10
# healthy? shift to 100. unhealthy? set the old revision back to 100 - seconds, no redeploy.

# scale on the queue, not on CPU
az containerapp update -n orders-worker -g rg-orders \
  --min-replicas 0 --max-replicas 30 \
  --scale-rule-name sb --scale-rule-type azure-servicebus \
  --scale-rule-metadata queueName=orders messageCount=20 \
  --scale-rule-identity /subscriptions/.../userAssignedIdentities/id-worker

Follow-ups

  • Consumption or Dedicated workload profiles? — Consumption for bursty and scale-to-zero work; Dedicated profiles when you need specific VM sizes, GPUs, or predictable capacity and pricing for steady load. They coexist in one environment, so you can place apps per profile.
  • When would you still choose AKS? — CRDs and operators, a service mesh you configure yourself, DaemonSets, node-level access, GPU scheduling beyond what profiles offer, or an existing Helm-based platform. Container Apps deliberately hides the cluster, and that is both its value and its limit.
  • How do apps talk to each other? — Internal ingress plus the environment's DNS, or Dapr service invocation if you have adopted Dapr (which also gives pub/sub, state and bindings as sidecars). Dapr is optional; do not adopt it just because it is available.
  • What is the scale-to-zero catch for HTTP? — The first request after idling pays a container start. For user-facing APIs set minReplicas: 1; keep zero for workers and internal jobs where a few seconds is free.

Wrong answer: “Container Apps is just a simpler AKS, so we can migrate to AKS later with no changes.” The image is portable; the platform features are not — revisions, Dapr components, scale rules, ingress and environment DNS all need rebuilding as Kubernetes objects. It is a much smaller migration than a rewrite, and calling it “no changes” is the overstatement interviewers probe.

When does serverless stop being the right answer? principal

Answer. At four boundaries. Sustained load: per-execution pricing beats reserved capacity for spiky traffic and loses badly for steady high throughput — there is a crossover, and you should compute it rather than assert it. Latency floors: if a cold start or a scale-out delay is unacceptable and the always-ready baseline you need is most of your peak, you are paying serverless prices for dedicated behaviour. Long-lived or stateful processes: connection-oriented workloads, in-memory caches that must stay warm, or anything holding a long-lived resource fight the model rather than using it. Operational fit: when the number of small deployable units exceeds what the team can observe and version coherently, the per-function granularity becomes the problem — a hundred functions with subtly different runtime versions is harder to operate than one service. The counter-argument to keep in mind is that these boundaries move: Flex Consumption's always-ready instances and per-function scaling removed two of the classic objections, so an assessment from three years ago is probably stale. The principal-level answer names the crossover with a number and says how you would measure it.

Example

Crossover sketch (illustrative arithmetic, run it with your own rates)
  Workload: 300 req/s sustained, 120 ms CPU each, 512 MB
    Flex Consumption: billed per GB-s of execution + per million executions
      300 x 0.12 s x 0.5 GB = 18 GB-s per second of wall clock, 24/7
    Dedicated equivalent: ~4 x P1v3 running continuously
  -> compute both at current list prices; at this duty cycle dedicated is usually cheaper.
  Now change the shape: 300 req/s for 3 hours a day, near zero otherwise
  -> consumption wins decisively, because you stop paying for 21 hours.
The question to ask is never "is serverless cheaper" but "what is the duty cycle".

Follow-ups

  • What non-cost factors matter most? — Time to first deploy, operational surface, and the team's ability to debug. Serverless usually wins on the first two for small teams and can lose on the third if tracing and local development are not solved.
  • How do you avoid a one-way door? — Keep business logic in plain classes that the function or controller merely invokes, and keep bindings thin. Then the hosting decision is a project structure change rather than a rewrite, and you can revisit it when the duty cycle changes.
  • What has changed recently that people miss? — Flex Consumption's always-ready instances and per-function scaling, and VNet integration at no extra cost, which removed the two standard reasons for choosing Premium. Reassessing a hosting decision made before that is a legitimate piece of work.
  • Is “serverless” even the right frame? — Increasingly the useful axis is duty cycle and operational ownership rather than a product category; Container Apps with scale-to-zero is serverless by behaviour and containerised by packaging. Say that, and the question stops being tribal.

Wrong answer: “Serverless is always cheaper because you only pay for what you use.” You pay a premium per unit of compute in exchange for not paying when idle; at high duty cycles that premium dominates. Anyone who has run a high-throughput function app at scale has seen this, and the interviewer is checking whether you have.

15Edge, APIM & WAF

The edge is where public traffic meets your estate, so questions here mix architecture with security. Screener depth is knowing what API Management and a WAF do; senior depth is policy design, authentication offload, rate limiting and origin protection; principal depth is the API platform question — versioning, governance and whether a gateway is worth its operational weight. This unit assumes unit 07 covered which load balancer goes where.

Docs: API Management · Web Application Firewall · Front Door

What does API Management give you that a reverse proxy does not? screener

Answer. Three things a plain proxy does not: a policy engine (an XML pipeline applied on inbound, backend, outbound and on-error, doing JWT validation, rate limiting by key, transformation, caching, mock responses, request routing and header manipulation without touching backend code), a product and subscription model (APIs grouped into products, consumers holding subscription keys with quotas, an approval workflow), and a developer portal with generated documentation and interactive testing. Around them sit versioning and revisions, an OpenAPI-driven definition, and a self-hosted gateway option for on-premises or other clouds. The tier choice matters: the v2 tiers (Basic v2, Standard v2, Premium v2) provision in minutes rather than the classic tiers' 30–45, and Premium is where you get multi-region, VNet injection, availability zones and multiple scale units. The judgment question interviewers are really asking is whether you know when it is worth it — APIM earns its cost when you have many APIs, external consumers, or a genuine need to enforce cross-cutting policy outside application code; for three internal APIs behind Front Door it is expensive ceremony.

Example

<policies>
  <inbound>
    <base />
    <validate-azure-ad-token tenant-id="{{tenant}}" failed-validation-httpcode="401">
      <audiences><audience>api://orders</audience></audiences>
      <required-claims><claim name="roles" match="any"><value>Orders.Read</value></claim></required-claims>
    </validate-azure-ad-token>
    <rate-limit-by-key calls="100" renewal-period="60"
                       counter-key="@(context.Request.Headers.GetValueOrDefault("X-Tenant-Id","anon"))" />
    <set-header name="X-Correlation-Id" exists-action="skip">
      <value>@(context.RequestId.ToString())</value>
    </set-header>
  </inbound>
  <backend><forward-request timeout="20" /></backend>
  <on-error><base /><set-header name="X-Error-Source"><value>apim</value></set-header></on-error>
</policies>

Follow-ups

  • Are subscription keys authentication? — No. A subscription key identifies the calling application for quota and analytics; it is a shared bearer string with no user context and no expiry. Real authentication is the JWT validation policy on top of it, and treating the key as security is a common design error.
  • Revisions or versions? — Revisions are non-breaking changes to the same API that you can test and then make current; versions are breaking changes exposed at a different path, query parameter or header, with consumers migrating explicitly. Using revisions for breaking changes is how you break clients silently.
  • What is the operational risk? — Policy becomes a second codebase, deployed differently from the services, often edited in the portal. Keep policies in source control and deploy them with the API definition, or you will have production behaviour nobody can review.
  • Self-hosted gateway — when? — Regulatory or latency reasons to terminate inside your own network or another cloud, while keeping one control plane and one developer portal. It adds a container to operate, so use it for a reason rather than by default.

Wrong answer: “APIM secures our APIs because clients need a subscription key to call them.” The key is in every client, is not rotated, and grants whatever the product grants. Interviewers ask this to see whether you distinguish identification from authentication and authorisation.

How does a WAF work on Azure, and how do you deploy one without breaking the application? mid

Answer. Azure's WAF runs either at the edge on Front Door Premium or regionally on Application Gateway v2, and both use a policy combining the Microsoft-managed Default Rule Set (derived from the OWASP Core Rule Set) with bot protection and your own custom rules for rate limiting, geo-filtering and allow/deny by IP or header. Deployment is the interesting half, because a managed rule set will always produce false positives against a real application: start in Detection mode, collect AzureDiagnostics/FrontDoorWebApplicationFirewallLog for a week or two, triage what matched — typically JSON bodies triggering SQL-injection heuristics, base64 blobs, and rich text fields — and write targeted exclusions scoped to the specific request attribute and rule ID rather than disabling whole rule groups. Then switch to Prevention, per-application, with monitoring and a fast rollback (mode is a single setting). Add custom rate-limit rules for credential-stuffing and scraping, because the managed rules do not do volumetric protection, and remember that a WAF is a compensating control: it buys time, it does not fix the vulnerability.

Example

// what would Prevention mode have blocked last week, and is any of it real traffic?
AzureDiagnostics
| where Category == "FrontdoorWebApplicationFirewallLog" and TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| summarize hits = count(), uris = dcount(requestUri_s), sample = any(requestUri_s)
          by ruleName_s, action_s, clientIP_s
| order by hits desc
| take 30
// a rule with many hits from many client IPs on one URI is usually a false positive;
// many hits from one IP across many URIs is usually a scanner.

Follow-ups

  • How do you write an exclusion safely? — Scope it as narrowly as the schema allows: this rule ID, on this request-body JSON path or this header, for this URI. Disabling a whole rule group because one field trips it is how a WAF becomes decorative.
  • Front Door WAF or Application Gateway WAF? — Front Door blocks at the edge before traffic reaches your region, which is better for volumetric and geographic controls; Application Gateway sees the request inside the VNet, which is better when the decision needs regional context or the app is private. Many estates run both, with different rule sets.
  • What about DDoS? — Azure's infrastructure DDoS protection is always on; DDoS Network Protection (a paid tier) adds application-tuned mitigation, telemetry, alerting and cost protection for the scale-out a large attack causes. WAF rate limiting handles layer-7 abuse, not volumetric layer-3/4 attacks.
  • Does a WAF let you skip fixing the code? — No, and saying so is the mature answer. It is a shield while you patch, and a control for classes you cannot patch quickly (a third-party component). Treating it as the fix leaves the vulnerability reachable from any path that bypasses the edge.

Wrong answer: “Turn the managed rule set on in Prevention mode — it's the secure default.” It will block legitimate traffic on day one, usually the API with JSON payloads, and the emergency response will be to disable the WAF entirely. Detection-first is not caution, it is the only rollout that survives contact with a real application.

How do you protect an origin so it can only be reached through the edge? senior

Answer. Three levels, and only the third is genuinely airtight. Level 1: restrict the origin's network access to the AzureFrontDoor.Backend service tag — necessary but insufficient, because that tag covers every Front Door tenant, including one an attacker creates in five minutes. Level 2: additionally validate the X-Azure-FDID header against your own profile's ID, either in the app, in an NSG-adjacent layer, or in Application Gateway/APIM — this closes the multi-tenant hole and is the minimum acceptable configuration for a public origin. Level 3: use Private Link origins (Front Door Premium) so the origin has no public endpoint at all — publicNetworkAccess: Disabled plus a private endpoint that Front Door connects through, approved on your side. That removes the entire class of bypass rather than filtering it. Whichever level you choose, verify it the same way an attacker would: resolve the origin hostname directly and try to reach it from outside, as a scheduled test, because this control silently regresses whenever someone adds a new hostname or a new environment.

Example

Level 1 (weak):    NSG / service firewall allows service tag AzureFrontDoor.Backend
                   -> any Front Door profile in the world can reach you
Level 2 (minimum): + validate header X-Azure-FDID == <your profile id>, reject otherwise
                   App Service: ipSecurityRestrictions with the tag AND the header check
Level 3 (best):    Front Door Premium Private Link origin
                   app: publicNetworkAccess = Disabled, no public path exists at all

Verification (monthly, automated):
  curl -sS -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' https://app-orders.azurewebsites.net/health
  expect: connection refused / 403 - NOT 200

Follow-ups

  • Why is the service tag alone dangerous? — Front Door is multi-tenant, so the tag's IP ranges are shared by all customers. An attacker points their own Front Door profile at your origin and inherits your allowlist. The FDID header is what makes the allowlist yours.
  • What about health probes? — They come from the same infrastructure, so a restriction that blocks them makes the origin look unhealthy and takes it out of rotation. Test the probe path explicitly after applying restrictions — this is the most common self-inflicted outage in this design.
  • Does this apply to APIM too? — Yes: APIM in a VNet with internal mode plus Front Door in front is the standard shape, and the same bypass question applies to whatever sits behind APIM. Every layer needs to know who it accepts traffic from.
  • How do you handle the developer who needs to hit the origin directly? — Give them a path: a private endpoint reachable over the VPN, or a separate hostname with an IP restriction. Otherwise someone will add 0.0.0.0/0 “temporarily” during an incident and it will stay.

Wrong answer: “The origin's URL is not published anywhere, so nobody will find it.” Certificate Transparency logs publish every hostname you issue a certificate for, and *.azurewebsites.net names are trivially enumerable. Obscurity is not a control, and interviewers ask this specific question because so many estates rely on it.

How do you rate-limit and protect an API used by many tenants? senior

Answer. Limit at more than one layer, on a key that means something, and make the response honest. In APIM, rate-limit-by-key and quota-by-key let you choose the counter key — tenant ID from a validated JWT claim is the right key, client IP is the fallback for anonymous traffic, and subscription key works for partner APIs. Set a short-window rate limit to protect against bursts and a long-window quota to enforce the commercial plan; return 429 with Retry-After so well-behaved clients back off. Add a WAF custom rate-limit rule at the edge for volumetric abuse that should never reach the gateway at all, and keep an application-level limit as the backstop, because the gateway can be bypassed internally and because only the application knows what an expensive request is. Two design points that separate senior answers: rate limits must be per tenant or a single noisy customer consumes everyone's capacity, and limits should be published, monitored and alertable — a tenant hitting its limit constantly is either a product conversation or a sign your limit is wrong.

Example

<!-- burst protection: per tenant, short window -->
<rate-limit-by-key calls="200" renewal-period="10"
  counter-key="@(context.Request.Headers.GetValueOrDefault("Authorization","")
                 .AsJwt()?.Claims.GetValueOrDefault("tid","anon"))"
  retry-after-header-name="Retry-After" />

<!-- commercial plan: per tenant, per day -->
<quota-by-key calls="1000000" renewal-period="86400"
  counter-key="@((string)context.Variables["tenantId"])" />

<!-- expensive endpoints get their own, tighter budget -->
<choose><when condition="@(context.Request.Url.Path.Contains("/reports"))">
  <rate-limit-by-key calls="5" renewal-period="60"
    counter-key="@((string)context.Variables["tenantId"])" />
</when></choose>

Follow-ups

  • Why not rate-limit by IP? — Many tenants share NATs and proxies, so IP limits punish large customers and miss distributed abuse. IP is the right key only for unauthenticated traffic, where you have nothing better.
  • What should a 429 contain? — Retry-After, a clear error code, and ideally the limit and remaining quota in headers so clients can self-regulate. A bare 429 teaches clients to retry immediately, which makes the problem worse.
  • How do you handle a legitimate spike from one tenant? — A per-tenant override (APIM named values or a policy fragment driven by a tenant tier), plus an alert so the account team knows. Hard-coding one limit for all tenants guarantees you will breach it for the customer who matters most.
  • Is rate limiting in APIM exact? — In the classic multi-unit tiers, counters were per-gateway-instance unless you used the distributed variants, so effective limits could be a multiple of what you configured. Know which behaviour your tier has before you promise a number contractually.

Wrong answer: “Set a global rate limit on the API so it can't be overwhelmed.” A global limit means the first tenant to misbehave denies service to all the others — you have converted an abuse problem into an outage. Per-tenant keying is the whole point.

Design an API platform for an organisation exposing 200 APIs to internal and external consumers. principal

Answer. Separate the planes and be explicit about who owns what. Runtime: Front Door with WAF at the edge for public traffic, APIM (Premium, multi-region, VNet-injected) as the gateway, backends private. Externally-facing and internal APIs get different products, different policy sets and, if the compliance boundary demands it, different APIM instances — one instance with strong product separation is usually enough and much cheaper. Governance: an API design standard (naming, versioning, error format, pagination, auth), enforced by linting the OpenAPI spec in CI rather than by a review board; a spec-first workflow where the definition is the artefact promoted through environments; and a catalogue so consumers can discover APIs without asking. Ownership: each API is owned by its team and deployed by its pipeline into APIM via IaC; the platform team owns the gateway, the shared policy fragments, the products and the portal. Lifecycle: a published versioning and deprecation policy with a minimum support window and usage telemetry per consumer, so deprecation is a conversation with named consumers rather than an announcement into the void. The principal-level content is that governance must be automated and the gateway must not become a place where teams put logic they could not get reviewed.

Example

Planes
  edge        Front Door Premium + WAF (public), Private Link origins
  gateway     APIM Premium, 2 regions, VNet-injected, zone-redundant
  policy      shared fragments: auth, correlation, rate-limit-by-tenant, error shape
              team policies may ONLY compose fragments + route; no business logic (linted)
  backends    private endpoints / internal ingress only

Pipeline per API (owned by the API's team)
  openapi.yaml -> spectral lint (org ruleset) -> contract tests -> apim import (Bicep/AVM)
  -> revision -> smoke -> make current. Breaking change? new version + deprecation entry.

Platform-owned
  products (internal, partner, public), subscription approval flow, developer portal,
  the shared fragments, the lint ruleset, and the usage-by-consumer dashboard.
Deprecation policy: 12 months for external, 3 months for internal, both with named consumers
identified from telemetry before the notice goes out.

Follow-ups

  • One APIM instance or many? — Start with one and separate with products and policy; split when you have a hard compliance boundary, a genuinely different availability requirement, or when the blast radius of a gateway change becomes unacceptable. Many instances multiply cost and fragment the portal.
  • How do you stop policy becoming a shadow codebase? — Lint it: allow only composition of approved fragments plus routing, reject inline C# expressions beyond a whitelist, and require policies in the API's repository. Business logic in a gateway policy is untested, unversioned with the service, and invisible to the team that owns the API.
  • How do you find who still uses an API? — Per-subscription and per-consumer telemetry in Application Insights or the workspace, aggregated into a usage dashboard. Deprecating without that data means you will find your consumers when they page you.
  • What is the failure mode of the platform itself? — The gateway is now a single point of failure for 200 APIs. Multi-region APIM, zone redundancy, a tested failover, and a documented bypass for the highest-criticality APIs. Also rehearse the “bad policy deployed globally” scenario, which is more likely than a regional outage.

Wrong answer: “Put everything behind APIM and have the platform team review each API before it goes live.” At 200 APIs the review board is the bottleneck and the standard becomes advisory. Governance at this scale has to be linting and templates, not meetings — and interviewers at principal level have all watched a review board fail.

16CI/CD & release strategy

Delivery questions test whether your Azure knowledge survives contact with a pipeline: how the pipeline authenticates, how environments are promoted, how a release is made safe, and how infrastructure and application changes coordinate. Screener depth is naming Azure DevOps and GitHub Actions; senior depth is federated identity, environment promotion and rollback; principal depth is delivery across many teams and the trade-off between central control and autonomy.

Docs: OIDC from GitHub Actions · Approvals and gates · Deployment Environments

How should a pipeline authenticate to Azure, and how do you scope it? screener

Answer. Workload identity federation, scoped per environment. Create a user-assigned managed identity (or app registration) per workload per environment, add a federated credential trusting the CI system's OIDC issuer with a subject that names the exact repository and environment, and grant it Azure RBAC only at the resource groups it deploys into — Contributor there, plus any specific data-plane or subnet-join roles it genuinely needs. Never one identity for all environments, and never Contributor at subscription scope “because deployments touch a lot of things”. On the CI side, gate the environment with required reviewers so the token can only be minted after approval, and make the workflow file itself protected (CODEOWNERS on the workflow directory), because whoever can edit the workflow can use the identity. The reason this is now table stakes rather than best practice: since mandatory MFA Phase 2 completed in 2026, the old fallback of running deployments under a human account with username and password is gone, so every estate has had to move to either federation or a service principal secret — and one of those is clearly better.

Example

jobs:
  deploy-prod:
    environment: production           # required reviewers gate the OIDC token
    permissions: { id-token: write, contents: read }
    steps:
      - uses: azure/login@v2
        with:
          client-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_CLIENT_ID_PROD }}
          tenant-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_TENANT_ID }}
          subscription-id: ${{ vars.AZURE_SUB_PROD }}
      - run: |
          az stack group create -n orders-prod -g rg-orders-prod \
            --template-file infra/main.bicep --parameters infra/main.prod.bicepparam \
            --action-on-unmanage deleteResources --deny-settings-mode denyDelete

Follow-ups

  • Why one identity per environment? — So a compromised or careless non-production pipeline cannot touch production, and so the audit log tells you which environment acted. Sharing one identity means the blast radius of the weakest environment is the whole estate.
  • What about Azure DevOps? — Service connections support workload identity federation, with a conversion path from secret-based connections. Same model, plus Azure DevOps' own environment approvals and checks.
  • How do you deploy to a private network from a hosted runner? — Self-hosted runners or Azure DevOps scale-set agents inside the VNet, or a private-endpoint-reachable deployment path. Poking a hole in the firewall for the hosted runner's IP ranges is the answer to avoid.
  • Who can approve production? — A named group, not the person who opened the PR, with the approval recorded. Combine with branch protection so the artefact being deployed came from a reviewed commit; approvals on an unreviewed artefact are theatre.

Wrong answer: “Create a service principal with Contributor on the subscription and store the secret in the pipeline.” It is the most common configuration in the wild and it is a long-lived credential with estate-wide power sitting in a system many people can edit. As an answer it says you have not revisited the pipeline since federation became available.

How do you promote a change from dev to production? mid

Answer. Build once, promote the same artefact, change only configuration. Concretely: the build produces an immutable artefact — a container image with a digest, or a zip plus the compiled Bicep — which is versioned and stored once; each environment stage deploys that artefact with its own parameter file and its own federated identity; and no stage rebuilds from source, because a rebuild is a different artefact and invalidates everything the earlier stage proved. Between stages sit automated gates (smoke tests, contract tests, a canary bake with metric checks) and, for production, a human approval. Infrastructure and application changes need an explicit ordering rule: infrastructure first when it is additive (a new queue, a new setting), application first when infrastructure removal is involved, and expand-migrate-contract for schema so the two can be deployed independently. The detail that reveals experience is what happens to the parameter files: they live with the code, are reviewed like code, and differ only in values — so promotion cannot silently change behaviour.

Example

build (once)      docker build -> push acr.azurecr.io/orders@sha256:9f2c...  + bicep build
deploy dev        same digest + main.dev.bicepparam        auto, on merge to main
  gate            smoke + contract tests
deploy test       same digest + main.test.bicepparam       auto
  gate            integration suite, load smoke (5 min at 2x expected peak)
deploy prod       same digest + main.prod.bicepparam       manual approval, business hours
  strategy        canary 10% for 30 min with automatic rollback on error-rate/latency SLO
  rollback        traffic weight back to previous revision - seconds, no rebuild

Never: rebuilding per environment, "hotfix straight to prod", or a parameter file edited
in the portal after deployment.

Follow-ups

  • Why tag images by digest rather than by tag? — Tags are mutable; a :latest or even :1.4.2 can be overwritten, so what you tested is not provably what you deployed. Digests are content-addressed and make the promotion claim true.
  • How do you handle a database migration? — As a separate, forward-only, idempotent step run by a pipeline identity with DDL rights, deployed before the code that needs it and written so the previous version still works. Migrations that require a specific deployment order are the ones that block rollback.
  • What about environment parity? — Non-production should be structurally identical and differ only in scale, or it stops being a test. The usual violations — no zone redundancy in test, a different networking mode, a shared database — are exactly where production surprises come from.
  • How do you handle emergency fixes? — Same pipeline, shortened gates, with the deviation logged and the full suite run afterwards. A separate emergency path with no gates is how the emergency path becomes the normal path.

Wrong answer: “Each environment has its own branch and we build from that branch.” You now have three different artefacts, merge conflicts as the currency of promotion, and no guarantee that what you tested is what runs. Branch-per-environment is a well-known anti-pattern and interviewers ask about promotion specifically to surface it.

How do you deploy infrastructure and application code together safely? senior

Answer. Keep them in one repository so a change is reviewable as a unit, but deploy them as separate, ordered steps with independent rollback. The ordering rule is that additive infrastructure goes first (new queue, new setting, new database column, new container) and removals go last, in a later release, after no running version depends on them — the same expand/contract discipline as schema. Deployment Stacks help by making the infrastructure step idempotent and by refusing to silently orphan resources; the application step is then a slot swap, a revision weight or a rolling update that can be reverted without touching infrastructure. Two practical guards: run the infrastructure preview (what-if or plan) as a required check on the pull request so a reviewer sees the resource-level diff, and separate the identities so the application pipeline cannot delete infrastructure. For long-lived stateful resources, split them into a separate stack with a different cadence and stricter approvals, because the tempo of application deployment and the tempo of database change should not be the same.

Example

repo/
  src/                 application
  infra/
    platform.bicep     stateful: SQL, storage, key vault   (stack: orders-platform, detachAll)
    app.bicep          stateless: plan, app, settings, PE  (stack: orders-app,      deleteAll)
Pipeline
  1. what-if on both stacks, posted to the PR as a comment (required check)
  2. deploy orders-platform  (rare; approval by platform + data owner)
  3. deploy orders-app       (every release)
  4. deploy application artefact to the staging slot, warm, canary, swap
Rollback: step 4 is a swap back. Steps 2-3 are reverted by redeploying the previous
commit's templates - which is why removals never ship in the same release as the code
that stopped using them.

Follow-ups

  • Why post what-if to the pull request? — Because reviewers approve code diffs and rarely imagine the resource diff. Seeing “this will delete the private endpoint” in the PR is the cheapest possible place to catch it.
  • Same repo or separate repos? — Same repo for a workload's own infrastructure: atomic review and a single history. Separate repo for shared platform infrastructure with a different lifecycle and different owners. Splitting a workload's app and infra across repos makes every change a two-PR dance.
  • What about configuration that both need? — Have infrastructure output what the application consumes (connection endpoints, identity client IDs) into app settings or App Configuration, rather than duplicating values in two places. Duplication here is a guaranteed future incident.
  • How do you test infrastructure changes? — Deploy to an ephemeral environment created by the same templates, run the smoke suite, tear it down. Azure Deployment Environments or a stack with deleteAll makes the teardown reliable; without a teardown, ephemeral environments become permanent cost.

Wrong answer: “Deploy the infrastructure and the app in one template so they're always consistent.” It couples two very different failure and rollback modes: a bad app build now requires an infrastructure redeploy, and a template error can take down a healthy application. Consistency comes from ordering and review, not from a single deployment.

Azure DevOps or GitHub Actions? And what actually matters in the choice? mid

Answer. Both authenticate to Azure with workload identity federation, both deploy anything, and Microsoft's investment is clearly in GitHub. GitHub Actions wins on ecosystem, developer familiarity, proximity to the code, reusable workflows, and integration with GitHub Advanced Security (secret scanning with push protection, code scanning, dependency review) — which increasingly matters more than the pipeline features themselves. Azure DevOps still wins on a few enterprise specifics: mature multi-stage release approvals and gates, fine-grained permissions, Azure Boards integration where that is in use, and organisations whose compliance processes are already built around it. The honest framing for an interview is that the choice rarely determines outcomes — what determines outcomes is whether the pipeline is fast, reproducible, secured with federation, and owned by the team that ships. If migrating, the sequence that works is to move new work to Actions first, keep Azure DevOps running for existing pipelines, and convert per service rather than as a big-bang programme.

Example

ConcernGitHub ActionsAzure DevOps
Azure authOIDC federationOIDC federation (service connections)
Approvals/gatesenvironments + rulesricher multi-stage gates
Security toolingGHAS: secret/code scanning, push protectionseparate tooling
Private network agentsself-hosted runnersscale-set agents
Direction of investmentwhere the platform is goingmaintained, stable

Follow-ups

  • What is the biggest security control either way? — Push protection and secret scanning on the repository, plus federation instead of stored credentials. Those two remove the most common real incidents; pipeline syntax removes none.
  • How do you keep pipelines maintainable across teams? — Reusable workflows or templates owned centrally, consumed by teams with parameters, versioned and pinned. Copy-pasted 400-line pipelines are the CI equivalent of copy-pasted templates.
  • What about self-hosted runner security? — A self-hosted runner executing untrusted PR code is a compromise waiting to happen: ephemeral runners, no secrets in the environment for fork PRs, and network isolation. This is a real attack path people forget when they move runners into the VNet.
  • Migration risk? — Losing release history and approvals evidence, and the long tail of pipelines nobody owns. Keep the old system readable for the audit window and convert by service, not by decree.

Wrong answer: “GitHub Actions, because Azure DevOps is deprecated.” It is not deprecated; it is maintained and widely used, and saying otherwise is an easily-checked factual error that costs credibility. The defensible version is “the investment is in GitHub, so new work goes there”.

Design delivery for 12 teams so it is consistent without being centrally controlled. principal

Answer. Provide golden paths and enforce a thin set of invariants. The golden path is a template repository plus reusable workflows that already do the right thing — federated auth, build once and promote, what-if on PR, canary with automatic rollback, SBOM and image signing, telemetry wired — so a new service is productive on day one and the compliant path is also the fastest. The invariants are the few things that must hold regardless of team preference, enforced mechanically rather than by review: no long-lived cloud credentials, deployments only through federated identities that humans do not hold, protected environments for production, artefacts built from reviewed commits, and provenance/attestation on what ships. Everything else — test frameworks, branching model, release cadence — belongs to the team. Then measure delivery rather than compliance: lead time, deployment frequency, change failure rate and time to restore, published per team, so improvement is visible and comparisons prompt curiosity rather than mandates. The principal-level judgment is knowing which rules are worth the friction: each invariant should map to an incident class it prevents, and if you cannot name that class, it is a preference, not a standard.

Example

Golden path (provided, opt-in, and by far the easiest route)
  template repo:  app skeleton + infra/ + reusable workflow calls + OTel wired + SLO stub
  reusable wf:    build-once, sign, SBOM, what-if-on-PR, deploy-with-canary, auto-rollback
  vending:        subscription + rg + federated identities + environments created for you

Invariants (enforced by policy/branch protection, not by review; each maps to an incident class)
  no client secrets for Azure auth        -> leaked-credential incidents
  prod deploys via protected environment  -> unreviewed change incidents
  artefact built from a reviewed commit   -> supply-chain incidents
  IaC preview visible on the PR           -> accidental-deletion incidents
  telemetry contract (trace propagation)  -> undiagnosable cross-team incidents

Team-owned: branching, test strategy, cadence, language, framework.
Published monthly: lead time, deploy frequency, change failure rate, MTTR - per team.

Follow-ups

  • How do you get adoption without a mandate? — Make the golden path faster than rolling your own, migrate one enthusiastic team first, and publish their lead-time improvement. Adoption follows evidence; mandates produce minimum-compliance implementations.
  • What do you do about a team that refuses? — Hold the invariants, let the rest go, and revisit after their next incident with data. Spending political capital on branching strategy leaves none for “no long-lived credentials”, which is the one that matters.
  • How do you evolve the shared workflows without breaking everyone? — Version them, pin consumers to a major version, announce deprecations with a window, and provide an automated upgrade PR. Treat internal tooling with the compatibility discipline of a public library.
  • What is the metric that tells you it is working? — Change failure rate and time to restore, together. High deployment frequency with a rising failure rate is not delivery improvement, and publishing only the flattering metric is how these programmes lose credibility.

Wrong answer: “Standardise on one pipeline that all teams must use, maintained by the platform team.” It becomes a bottleneck, then a queue, then a shadow pipeline in someone's repository. Consistency comes from making the good path attractive and enforcing a small number of invariants — not from owning everyone's build.

17Security operations & compliance

Asked when the role touches a regulated estate or a platform team with security responsibilities. Screener depth is knowing Defender for Cloud and Sentinel exist; senior depth is turning findings into action and understanding what the tools actually detect; principal depth is building a security programme that engineers cooperate with. Depth here is a differentiator rather than a gate for most application-focused roles.

Docs: Defender for Cloud · Microsoft Sentinel · Regulatory compliance initiatives

What does Microsoft Defender for Cloud actually give you, and what does it cost? screener

Answer. Two halves. CSPM (cloud security posture management) is free at the foundational level: it continuously assesses resources against the Microsoft Cloud Security Benchmark, produces recommendations and a secure score, and gives you an inventory and a regulatory-compliance view mapped to frameworks. Defender plans are the paid workload protections, enabled per resource type — servers (with endpoint detection), App Service, storage (including malware scanning and sensitive data discovery), SQL, containers/AKS, Key Vault, Resource Manager, DNS, APIs, and AI services — and they provide runtime threat detection and alerts rather than configuration findings. Defender CSPM, the paid posture tier, adds agentless vulnerability scanning, attack-path analysis and the security graph, which is what turns a list of a thousand findings into “this internet-facing VM has a critical CVE and a managed identity with Contributor on a subscription”. The costs are per resource per month and vary widely by plan, so the honest answer names a prioritisation: turn on CSPM everywhere, then Defender for Servers, Storage, Key Vault and Containers where the risk justifies it, rather than enabling everything and being surprised.

Example

Free everywhere
  foundational CSPM: recommendations, secure score, inventory, MCSB compliance view
Paid, prioritised by risk
  Defender CSPM        attack paths + agentless scanning        -> highest signal per euro
  Defender for Servers vulnerability + EDR integration          -> anything with an OS
  for Storage          malware scan + sensitive data discovery  -> customer data accounts
  for Containers       image scanning + runtime detection       -> AKS, ACR
  for Key Vault / ARM  anomalous access, suspicious ARM ops     -> cheap, high value
Governance rules in Defender assign findings to owners with due dates - use them, or the
recommendations list becomes 3,000 items nobody reads.

Follow-ups

  • Is secure score a good metric? — As a trend for a specific scope, yes; as an absolute target, no, because it weights controls generically and some findings are irrelevant to your architecture. Track the movement and the top-weighted items, and never make the number itself the goal.
  • How does it relate to Azure Policy? — Recommendations are implemented as policy definitions in the built-in initiatives, so “fix in Defender” and “enforce with policy” are the same underlying mechanism. Use Defender to discover and prioritise, and policy to prevent recurrence.
  • What about multicloud? — Defender for Cloud connects AWS and GCP accounts and assesses them with equivalent benchmarks, which is often the practical reason to standardise on it in a multicloud estate.
  • Who acts on the findings? — Governance rules assign an owner and a due date per recommendation, routed to the resource's tagged owner. Without ownership, the queue grows and the tool gets blamed.

Wrong answer: “Turn on all the Defender plans across every subscription — security is worth it.” The bill is substantial and the alert volume without tuning is unmanageable, so the usual outcome is that finance turns it off in three months. Prioritising by risk and naming the cost is what makes the answer credible.

What is Microsoft Sentinel, and when do you need one? mid

Answer. Sentinel is the cloud-native SIEM/SOAR built on a Log Analytics workspace: it ingests logs from Azure, Entra, Microsoft 365, third-party firewalls and SaaS via connectors, runs scheduled analytics rules in KQL to produce incidents, correlates related alerts, and automates response through playbooks (Logic Apps). Because it is workspace-based, the same KQL you use for operations works for detection, and cost is dominated by the same ingestion economics — which is why table plans, transformations and choosing what not to ingest matter as much here as in observability. You need one when you have a security function that will act on incidents around the clock (their own or a managed provider's), a compliance obligation to retain and monitor security logs, or a multi-source estate where correlation across identity, endpoint and cloud is the point. You do not need one to store logs — a workspace does that — and standing one up without a team to work the queue produces expensive, unread alerts. Note that Sentinel is increasingly operated through the unified Microsoft Defender portal rather than its own blade, which is a currency detail worth knowing.

Example

// analytics rule: impossible travel on a privileged Azure sign-in, 15-minute window
let priv = SigninLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where AppDisplayName == "Azure Portal" or ResourceDisplayName has "Windows Azure Service Management";
priv
| extend country = tostring(LocationDetails.countryOrRegion)
| summarize countries = make_set(country), ips = make_set(IPAddress),
            first = min(TimeGenerated), last = max(TimeGenerated)
          by UserPrincipalName, bin(TimeGenerated, 15m)
| where array_length(countries) > 1
| project UserPrincipalName, countries, ips, first, last

Follow-ups

  • What drives Sentinel's cost? — Ingestion volume, overwhelmingly. Firewall, DNS and network flow logs dominate; Auxiliary/Basic table plans and DCR transformations are the levers, plus deciding that some sources are stored cheaply for forensics rather than analysed live.
  • Sentinel or just alerts on a workspace? — Alerts are fine for a handful of known detections. Sentinel earns its cost with correlation into incidents, entity behaviour analytics, threat intelligence matching, and automated response — none of which matter without responders.
  • What are playbooks good for? — Enrichment and containment: look up an IP's reputation, post to a channel, disable a user, isolate a device. Fully automated containment needs care — an automated disable rule is a denial-of-service primitive if the detection is noisy.
  • Which sources are non-negotiable? — Entra sign-in and audit logs, Azure activity log, and Defender for Cloud alerts. Those three cover the majority of cloud-relevant detections at modest volume.

Wrong answer: “Enable Sentinel and connect every data source so we have full coverage.” You will ingest terabytes of low-value data, blow the budget, and produce alerts nobody triages. Detection engineering is choosing sources and writing rules for threats you have decided to detect, not collecting everything.

How do you demonstrate compliance with a framework like ISO 27001 or PCI DSS on Azure? senior

Answer. Split the evidence into three kinds. Inherited controls are Microsoft's — physical security, hypervisor, datacentre operations — and the evidence is Microsoft's audit reports and certifications, obtained from the Service Trust Portal; you cite them rather than testing them. Configured controls are yours and should be continuously evidenced by tooling: assign the matching built-in regulatory-compliance initiative in Azure Policy (Defender for Cloud renders it as a compliance dashboard), so “encryption at rest is enforced” is a live query rather than a screenshot, and export the compliance state on a schedule for the auditor's period. Process controls — access reviews, change management, incident response, training — are evidenced by the systems that run them: PIM activation history, pull-request approvals, incident records, access-review outcomes. The engineering contribution is to make evidence a by-product of doing the work: if change management is enforced by branch protection and deployment identities, then the pipeline's history is the evidence, and audits stop being a quarter-long screenshot exercise. Be honest about scope too — network segmentation and data-flow diagrams for PCI are architectural decisions, not policy assignments.

Example

Control: "cardholder data is encrypted at rest and keys are managed"
  Inherited   Microsoft PCI DSS AoC (Service Trust Portal) for the platform layer
  Configured  policy initiative assigned at MG scope, Deny on non-compliant creation
              evidence: monthly export of policy compliance state, per resource, retained
  Process     key rotation runbook + PIM history of who accessed the HSM + access reviews
  Architecture  CDE segmented into its own subscription + VNet, no peering to corp,
                data-flow diagram maintained in the repo and reviewed on change

What the auditor is handed: a live dashboard + a signed monthly export + the policy definitions
as code. What they are NOT handed: screenshots taken the week before the audit.

Follow-ups

  • What is the shared responsibility line? — It moves with the service model: for IaaS you own the OS, patching and configuration; for PaaS Microsoft owns more of the stack; for SaaS more still. Being precise about where the line falls for each service in scope is half of an audit conversation.
  • How do you handle exceptions? — Policy exemptions with a category, a justification, an expiry and a compensating control, reviewed on a schedule. Auditors accept documented, time-bound exceptions; they do not accept controls that are silently not applied.
  • What about data residency? — Region selection plus policy denying resource creation outside allowed regions, and then the hard part: the secondary systems (logs, backups, telemetry, support access) that quietly cross borders. That is where residency programmes actually fail.
  • How do you avoid compliance theatre? — Automate the evidence and make the control the same thing engineers already do. If compliance work is a separate activity from engineering work, it will be done once a year and be untrue for the other eleven months.

Wrong answer: “Azure is ISO 27001 certified, so our workloads inherit compliance.” The platform's certification covers the platform. Your configuration, your access management and your processes are in scope and are exactly what the auditor examines. Confusing the two is a well-known and disqualifying error in a regulated environment.

A subscription's activity log shows an unfamiliar principal creating VMs. Run the incident. senior

Answer. Treat it as a probable compromise with a crypto-mining or persistence motive, and work containment, investigation and eradication in parallel. Contain: identify the principal and revoke — disable the user or delete the service principal's credentials, revoke refresh tokens (which is a separate step from disabling: an existing token remains valid for its lifetime otherwise), and remove its role assignments. Stop and isolate the created VMs rather than deleting them, so you keep the forensic evidence. Investigate: from the activity log, establish the full set of operations by that principal and the time window; from Entra sign-in logs, how it authenticated (password, a leaked secret, a token replay, a consent grant) and from where; check for persistence — new role assignments, new federated credentials on an existing identity, a new app registration with a secret, a policy exemption, an added Conditional Access exclusion; and check the data plane, not just the control plane, because VM creation is often the noisy part of a quieter data access. Eradicate and recover: remove persistence, rotate anything the principal could reach, and only then restore normal access. Afterwards: the honest post-mortem question is not how they got in but why nothing alerted for however long it ran.

Example

// what did this principal do, everywhere, and when did it start?
AzureActivity
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Caller == "8f1c...b204" or CallerIpAddress == "203.0.113.99"
| summarize ops = count(), first = min(TimeGenerated), last = max(TimeGenerated)
          by OperationNameValue, ActivityStatusValue, SubscriptionId
| order by first asc
// persistence check: did it grant itself or anyone else a role?
AzureActivity
| where OperationNameValue has "roleAssignments/write" and TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| project TimeGenerated, Caller, CallerIpAddress, Properties

Follow-ups

  • Why revoke tokens separately? — Disabling an account stops new sign-ins; issued access tokens remain valid until they expire (commonly up to an hour), and refresh tokens longer. Revoke-MgUserSignInSession or the equivalent is the step people forget, and it is the difference between contained and not.
  • What if the principal is a legitimate service that was compromised? — Same containment, but plan for the outage: you are about to break a production workload. Decide the trade in advance by classifying identities, and have a way to stand up a replacement identity quickly.
  • What detection should have fired? — Defender for Resource Manager alerts on suspicious ARM operations, an alert on role-assignment writes at subscription scope, a cost anomaly alert (mining shows up on the bill fast), and quota-consumption alerts. Name which of these you would add.
  • How far back can you look? — Activity log retains 90 days by default and Entra logs 30 days on the base licence, which is usually shorter than an attacker's dwell time — the argument for exporting both to a workspace with longer retention, made before you need it.

Wrong answer: “Delete the VMs and the principal, then carry on — the immediate threat is gone.” You have destroyed the evidence, not established how they got in, and left any persistence in place. The compute was the symptom; the access path and whatever else it touched are the incident.

18AI workloads on Azure

Increasingly asked in 2026 even for non-AI roles, usually as “how would you productionise this” rather than as model theory. Screener depth is knowing what the Azure AI services are; senior depth is capacity, cost, grounding and evaluation; principal depth is the architecture and governance of AI features in a real product. Naming is in flux — Azure OpenAI and Azure AI Foundry are now presented as Microsoft Foundry — so describe capabilities rather than leaning on a brand.

Docs: Microsoft Foundry · Azure AI Search · Provisioned throughput

How do you provision model capacity, and what is the difference between the deployment types? mid

Answer. Capacity is the first real engineering decision. Standard (pay-as-you-go) bills per token against a shared pool with a tokens-per-minute quota per deployment: cheap, elastic, and subject to 429s under contention, so latency is not guaranteed. Provisioned throughput reserves dedicated capacity measured in PTUs, giving predictable latency and throughput at a fixed hourly cost — the right choice for a latency-sensitive production feature with steady load, usually purchased with a reservation to make it affordable. Batch processes asynchronously at a large discount with a long turnaround, ideal for bulk enrichment and offline evaluation. There is also a data-residency dimension — global, data-zone and regional deployment options trade routing flexibility for a guarantee about where inference happens, which is frequently the deciding constraint in Europe. The engineering consequences: quota is per region per model and is a hard ceiling you must request increases for; a spillover pattern (provisioned first, overflow to standard) is the standard way to get predictable latency without paying for peak; and every client needs retry-with-backoff on 429 plus a circuit breaker, because contention is normal, not exceptional.

Example

Sizing: 40 req/s peak, ~1,200 prompt tokens + ~400 completion tokens each
  ~ 40 x 1,600 = 64,000 tokens/s  -> convert to TPM: 3.84 M tokens/minute
  Standard quota per deployment is far below this -> multiple deployments or PTU

Chosen: PTU for the steady base sized to p50 load, standard deployment for spillover
  client: try PTU endpoint -> on 429, fall back to standard -> on 429, queue or degrade
  measure: % served by PTU, p95 latency per path, cost per 1k requests
Batch for the nightly re-embedding job: same model, a fraction of the price, hours not seconds.

Follow-ups

  • What does a 429 mean here? — Quota or capacity contention, with a Retry-After. Handle it as back-pressure: retry with jitter, spill to a second deployment or region, and degrade the feature rather than failing the whole request. A user-facing AI feature should have a defined degraded mode.
  • How do you control cost? — Cap max tokens per request, trim and cache prompts (prompt caching materially reduces cost for repeated prefixes), route easy requests to a smaller model, and set per-tenant budgets. Token cost per feature is the unit metric to track.
  • Do you need private networking? — Usually yes in an enterprise: private endpoints for the AI resource, disabled public access, and managed identity instead of API keys — the same posture as any other data service, and often the actual blocker in a regulated rollout.
  • How do you handle model versions? — Pin a model version per deployment, test upgrades against an evaluation set before switching, and expect deprecation notices with retirement dates. Auto-updating a model under a production prompt is how behaviour changes without a deployment.

Wrong answer: “Just call the API and scale as needed — it's a managed service.” Quotas are hard limits, contention is routine, and a feature with no capacity plan will fail at exactly the moment it becomes popular. Capacity planning is more important here than for most Azure services, not less.

Design a retrieval-augmented generation feature over internal documents. senior

Answer. Four stages, and the interesting engineering is not in the model. Ingest: extract text (Document Intelligence for PDFs and scans), chunk with overlap at semantic boundaries rather than fixed character counts, attach metadata — source, permissions, timestamp, version — to every chunk, and embed. Index: Azure AI Search with both a vector field and the original text, so you can run hybrid retrieval (BM25 keyword plus vector) and rerank with the semantic ranker, which consistently beats pure vector search on real corpora; alternatively Cosmos DB or PostgreSQL with pgvector if you want one store. Retrieve and generate: filter by the user's permissions at query time using security trimming — never rely on the model to withhold content it was given — then compose a prompt with the retrieved chunks and instruct the model to answer only from them and to cite sources. Evaluate and operate: build a labelled question set before launch, measure groundedness, relevance and retrieval recall on every prompt or model change, log prompts and responses (with a retention and privacy policy), and expose citations so users can verify. The two failure modes to design for explicitly are stale content and permission leakage, and both are ingestion-side problems.

Example

Ingest    blob/SharePoint -> Document Intelligence -> chunk (800 tok, 100 overlap, on headings)
          -> embed -> upsert to AI Search with fields:
             content, contentVector, sourceUri, aclGroupIds[], modifiedUtc, docVersion
          change feed / Event Grid on the source keeps the index fresh; deletes propagate

Query     user question -> (optional query rewrite) -> hybrid search
             filter: aclGroupIds/any(g: search.in(g, '<user's group oids>'))
          -> semantic reranker -> top 8 chunks -> prompt with citations required
          -> stream response -> log question, chunk ids, latency, tokens, and feedback

Evaluate  120 labelled Q/A pairs; on every prompt or model change measure
          groundedness, answer relevance, retrieval recall@8. Ship only if no regression.

Follow-ups

  • Why hybrid rather than pure vector? — Vector search misses exact identifiers, product codes and rare terms; keyword search misses paraphrase. Hybrid plus reranking is the empirically better default, and saying so shows you have measured rather than followed a tutorial.
  • How do you enforce permissions? — Store the ACL on each chunk at ingest and filter at query time with the caller's group memberships. Post-filtering results, or prompting the model to refuse, both leak — the second because the content is already in the context window.
  • How do you keep the index fresh? — Event-driven incremental indexing from the source's change feed, with deletions propagated and a periodic full reconciliation. A stale index answering confidently is worse than no feature.
  • What do you do about hallucination? — Require citations and show them, constrain the prompt to the retrieved context, measure groundedness on a fixed evaluation set, and add a content-safety layer. Also design the product so a wrong answer is recoverable — the mitigation is partly UX, not only model configuration.

Wrong answer: “Embed all the documents into a vector store and pass the top matches to the model — that's RAG.” It skips permissions, freshness, evaluation and hybrid retrieval, which are the four things that decide whether it works in production. The demo is a weekend; the product is these four.

How do you make an AI feature safe and observable in production? senior

Answer. Treat it as an unreliable dependency with a reputational blast radius. Safety: content filters on both input and output (Azure AI Content Safety, configurable severity per category), prompt-injection defences — treat retrieved documents and user content as untrusted input, never as instructions, and never give the model tools whose blast radius you would not give an anonymous user — plus per-tenant rate limits and abuse monitoring. Observability: log prompt, retrieved context identifiers, model and version, token counts, latency, filter verdicts and a request ID, with a retention policy agreed with privacy; emit OpenTelemetry spans so an AI call appears in the same trace as the rest of the request; and track quality metrics continuously — groundedness sampling, user thumbs, and a canary evaluation set that runs on a schedule. Operations: version prompts as code and roll them out progressively like any other change, pin model versions, and define the degraded mode for a 429 or a filter block. The point interviewers listen for is that an AI feature needs an evaluation suite in CI the way any other feature needs tests — without it, every prompt change is an unmeasured production experiment.

Example

Per request, logged (with PII handling agreed up front)
  requestId, tenantId, userOid(hashed), promptTemplateVersion, modelName+version,
  retrievedChunkIds[], promptTokens, completionTokens, latencyMs,
  contentFilterVerdicts{input,output}, degradedPath(bool), userFeedback

Alerts
  filter-block rate    > baseline x3         -> possible abuse or a bad prompt change
  groundedness sample  < threshold           -> retrieval or prompt regression
  429 rate             > 5% for 10 min       -> capacity, spill or degrade
  p95 latency          > budget              -> capacity or prompt bloat
  cost per 1k requests > budget              -> prompt growth or model routing regression

CI gate: the labelled evaluation set runs on every prompt/model/retrieval change.
Ship on no regression; a prompt is code and gets a review, a version and a rollback.

Follow-ups

  • How do you defend against prompt injection? — Separate instructions from data in the prompt structure, never let retrieved content grant capabilities, constrain any tool the model can call with its own authorisation checks, and validate outputs before acting on them. Assume injection will succeed sometimes and bound what it can do — that is the only durable control.
  • What do you log, given privacy constraints? — Agree it with legal before launch: often prompts and completions with a short retention and a redaction pass, plus permanent metadata. Deciding this after a support escalation is worse than deciding it early.
  • How do you evaluate quality without labels? — Model-graded evaluation on sampled traffic, user feedback signals, and task-completion proxies. Imperfect, and far better than nothing; the important part is that the measurement runs continuously, not once at launch.
  • What governance applies? — Responsible AI review for the use case, transparency to users that they are interacting with AI, human review for consequential decisions, and — in the EU — obligations under the AI Act depending on the risk classification. Naming that this is a compliance question as well as a technical one is a senior signal.

Wrong answer: “The content filters handle safety, so we just call the API.” Filters catch categories of harmful content; they do not catch a confidently wrong answer, a permission leak through retrieved context, or an injected instruction that makes the model misuse a tool. Safety here is an architecture, not a checkbox.

What are the architectural risks of adding AI features to an existing product? principal

Answer. Five, and they are mostly not about models. Non-determinism: the same input can produce different output, which breaks caching assumptions, contract tests, and the mental model of everyone who supports the product — you need evaluation-based testing and a support story for “it said something different yesterday”. Cost variability: token cost scales with prompt size, which grows silently as features are added, so cost per request must be a monitored unit metric with a budget, not a launch estimate. Latency: a generative call is seconds, not milliseconds, which changes the UX (streaming, optimistic states, async patterns) and can make a synchronous API design untenable. Data governance: prompts and retrieved context move data into new paths — logs, evaluation sets, third-party endpoints — and every residency and retention obligation follows it. Vendor and model churn: models are deprecated with retirement dates and behaviour changes between versions, so the abstraction seam and the evaluation suite are what protect you from a forced migration becoming a rewrite. The principal-level move is to name which of these is the binding constraint for this product and design for it, rather than listing all five.

Example

RiskSymptomMitigation
Non-determinismFlaky tests; unreproducible support casesEvaluation suite, logged prompt+context per request, pinned versions
Cost variabilityUnit cost creeping up release over releaseCost per 1k requests as an SLO; token caps; model routing
LatencyTimeouts; users abandoningStreaming, async with status, cached/degraded path
Data governancePrompts containing PII in logs and eval setsRedaction, retention policy, residency-scoped deployments
Model churnForced migration on a deprecation dateProvider seam, pinned versions, regression suite

Follow-ups

  • How do you decide whether the feature is worth it? — Define the success metric before building (task completion, deflection rate, time saved) and be willing to remove the feature if it does not move it. AI features are unusually prone to shipping and never being measured.
  • How much abstraction over the provider is right? — A thin seam that lets you swap model and provider, and nothing more. A heavy framework that abstracts prompts, retrieval and orchestration tends to become the thing you fight when behaviour needs tuning.
  • What is the organisational risk? — Expectation management: leadership sees a demo and assumes production is a week away. The gap is evaluation, permissions, capacity and support, and naming it early is part of the job.
  • When is the answer “don't”? — When a deterministic solution exists (a rules engine, a search box, a form), when the cost of a wrong answer is high and unrecoverable, or when the data needed cannot legally move into the path. Being willing to say no is what distinguishes the principal answer.

Wrong answer: “It's just another API call, so the architecture doesn't really change.” It changes latency budgets, testing strategy, cost modelling, data flows and support processes simultaneously. Interviewers ask this to see whether you have shipped an AI feature or only prototyped one.

19Hybrid, Arc & migration

Rarely probed unless the company has a datacentre, an edge estate or a live migration programme — but when it comes up it comes up hard, and a vague answer is disqualifying for a platform role. Screener depth is knowing Arc exists; senior depth is what Arc actually gives you and how a migration is sequenced; principal depth is deciding what stays on-premises and defending it.

Docs: Azure Arc · Azure Migrate · Azure Local

What is Azure Arc and what does it actually let you do? mid

Answer. Arc projects non-Azure resources into Azure Resource Manager so they become first-class ARM resources you can govern with the tools you already use. Three main flavours: Arc-enabled servers (a lightweight agent on a Windows or Linux machine anywhere — on-premises, AWS, GCP, a branch office — giving it an ARM resource ID, a managed identity, Azure Policy guest configuration, Defender for Servers, Update Manager, Machine Configuration and Run Command); Arc-enabled Kubernetes (any conformant cluster gets policy, GitOps configuration via Flux, Defender for Containers and Azure Monitor); and Arc-enabled data services and app services (running Azure-managed SQL Managed Instance or PostgreSQL, and App Service/Functions, on your own Kubernetes). The honest framing is that Arc is a management-plane extension, not a way to run Azure services for free on your hardware: you get consistent inventory, policy, RBAC, patching and security posture across a hybrid estate. Alongside it, Azure Local (the product formerly called Azure Stack HCI) is the validated on-premises infrastructure for running Azure-managed VMs and AKS where the workload genuinely cannot leave the building.

Example

# onboard an on-prem VM: it becomes /subscriptions/.../Microsoft.HybridCompute/machines/srv-42
azcmagent connect --resource-group rg-arc --tenant-id $TENANT \
  --location westeurope --subscription-id $SUB

# now the same governance applies as to any Azure resource
az policy assignment create --name require-defender \
  --scope /subscriptions/$SUB/resourceGroups/rg-arc \
  --policy <defender-for-servers-initiative>
# and the machine has a managed identity it can use to read Key Vault - no stored secret on-prem

Follow-ups

  • What is the underrated benefit? — The managed identity on an Arc-enabled server. An on-premises machine can authenticate to Azure services with no stored credential, which removes a whole class of secrets from datacentre estates.
  • What does it cost? — Arc-enabled servers' core control plane is free; the value-adding services on top (Defender for Servers, Update Manager at scale, Arc-enabled data services) are billed. Budget for the services, not for Arc itself.
  • Does Arc make the workload highly available? — No. It manages what you have; the resilience of an on-premises machine is still yours. Candidates sometimes present Arc as a hybrid availability story, which it is not.
  • When would you Arc-enable another cloud's VMs? — When you want one policy, patching and security-posture plane across clouds and your security team standardises on Defender. It is a genuinely common multicloud pattern and worth naming.

Wrong answer: “Arc lets you run Azure services on your own hardware.” Only in the narrow case of Arc-enabled data/app services on Kubernetes, and Azure Local for VMs and AKS. For the general case Arc manages your machines; it does not turn them into Azure.

How do you plan a datacentre exit? senior

Answer. Work backwards from the lease or contract end date and treat discovery as the long pole. Sequence: discover with Azure Migrate's appliance for inventory, dependency mapping, right-sizing and a cost model — the dependency map is what stops you moving an application and leaving its unknown database behind. Classify every workload into rehost, replatform, refactor, repurchase (move to SaaS), retain (it stays, for now) and retire — and expect retire to be 10–20% of the estate, which is the cheapest migration there is. Prepare the landing zone, connectivity (ExpressRoute early, because provisioning takes weeks) and identity. Move in waves grouped by dependency cluster rather than by team or by ease, with data moved first or together, each wave rehearsed and reversible. Optimise after landing, not during — right-sizing and re-platforming during the move multiplies the variables when something breaks. Throughout, the two schedule risks are workloads whose owner has left the company and licensing that turns out to be non-transferable; both surface in discovery and both need executive escalation paths agreed in advance.

Example

T-12m  Azure Migrate appliance deployed; 6 weeks of dependency data before any decision
T-11m  Classification workshop per application owner: 6R + a named owner + a wave
T-10m  ExpressRoute ordered (long lead time); landing zone + identity + policy baseline
T-8m   Wave 0: internal, low-risk, well-understood app -> proves the whole pipeline end to end
T-7m   Retire wave: shut down (not delete) 60 servers nobody claimed; 2-week complaint window
T-6m.. Waves 1-N by dependency cluster: data replicated first, app rehosted, cutover, soak
T-2m   Buffer for the two waves that will slip (they always do)
T-0    Lease ends. Only the "retain" list remains, with a documented reason and a review date.
Cost model updated every wave with ACTUAL Azure cost vs the estimate - the estimate is
always wrong in the first two waves, and correcting early keeps the business's trust.

Follow-ups

  • What is the biggest cost surprise? — Egress and inter-site chatter during the transition period, and running both estates in parallel for longer than planned. Budget explicitly for the overlap; it is the line most plans omit.
  • How do you handle the “retain” list? — Give each item a documented reason, an owner and a review date, and Arc-enable it so it is at least governed and monitored like everything else. A retain list with no review date becomes a permanent shadow datacentre.
  • Rehost or replatform first? — Rehost, unless the workload is trivially replatformable. Rehosting gets you off the hardware, which is the deadline; replatforming is optimisation and can happen at leisure afterwards, when you have more information.
  • How do you keep the business informed? — A wave burn-down, actual versus estimated cost, and a named risk register reviewed fortnightly. Migration programmes fail politically more often than technically.

Wrong answer: “We'll modernise as we migrate — it's the natural time to fix the architecture.” It sounds efficient and it converts a deadline-driven programme into an open-ended one, with the deadline still fixed. Move, then improve; and say so deliberately, because the interviewer is testing whether you can resist the temptation.
