What would you deliberately keep on-premises, and how do you defend it? principal

Answer. Four categories, each with a testable reason. Latency-bound to physical processes: factory-floor control, medical devices, trading systems co-located with an exchange — the speed of light is not negotiable and Azure Local or an edge deployment is the right shape. Regulatory or sovereignty where the specific control cannot be met by a region or a sovereign cloud offering, verified with legal rather than assumed. Economically irrational to move: a large, stable, fully-depreciated system with predictable load and no growth, where a lift-and-shift buys nothing but a bigger bill — though remember the counterweight of the hardware refresh cycle, which is where these decisions usually get revisited. Genuinely blocked: a vendor appliance with no cloud support, or an application whose licence forbids it. What makes this a principal answer rather than an excuse is the discipline attached: every retained workload gets a documented reason, a named owner, a review date tied to the next refresh or contract renewal, and the same governance as everything else — Arc-enabled, monitored, patched and in the same security posture. Without that, “strategic on-premises” is indistinguishable from “nobody got round to it”.

Example

WorkloadReason to retainReview trigger
Line-control PLC gatewaySub-5 ms to the shop floor; survives WAN lossIf the site gets redundant fibre; Azure Local evaluated at refresh
National-registry integrationRegulator requires processing in-country on approved infraRegulation review; sovereign cloud availability
Legacy actuarial batchDepreciated hardware, flat load, retiring in 20282027 decommission plan; earlier if hardware fails
Vendor imaging applianceVendor does not support virtualised or cloud deploymentAnnual vendor roadmap review; contract renewal 2027

Follow-ups

  • How do you keep retained systems from decaying? — Same governance: Arc-enabled, in the monitoring estate, patched by Update Manager, covered by Defender, backed up and DR-tested. The failure mode of a retain list is a security posture that quietly diverges.
  • What about the “we might need to move back” argument? — Ask what would trigger it and what it would cost. If nobody can answer, it is anxiety rather than strategy; if they can, write it down as an exit plan with a rehearsal, which is a legitimate and rare piece of engineering rigour.
  • Does a sovereign cloud change the answer? — Often yes: sovereign and government cloud offerings, and in-country regions with disconnected operation options, have removed several classic residency blockers. Check current availability rather than citing a policy written five years ago.
  • How do you handle the political version of this question? — Some retention arguments are really about jobs or control. Name the technical facts, offer the review trigger, and escalate the non-technical part rather than pretending it is an architecture debate.

Wrong answer: “Everything should move to the cloud eventually; on-premises is legacy by definition.” It ignores physics, regulation and economics, and it signals that you would push a migration that does not pay for itself. The strong answer is comfortable retaining things and rigorous about why.

20Control plane, limits & capacity

Rarely asked directly and disproportionately valuable when it comes up, because it is knowledge you only get from operating an estate at scale. Screener depth is knowing ARM exists; senior depth is throttling, quotas and deployment behaviour; principal depth is designing so limits are hit predictably rather than during a launch. Every question here maps to a real incident somebody has had.

Docs: Azure Resource Manager · Throttling · Service limits

What happens when you submit a deployment to ARM, and why does it matter? mid

Answer. The request goes to the ARM front door, which authenticates the caller, authorises against RBAC, evaluates Azure Policy (a deny stops it here), records the deployment, and then dispatches operations to the relevant resource providers — Microsoft.Compute, Microsoft.Network and so on — each of which owns its own API versions, its own regional endpoints and its own quotas. Two consequences matter operationally. First, resource creation is mostly asynchronous: ARM returns quickly with an operation you poll, so a “successful” deployment call does not mean the resource is usable, and a template's dependency graph is what serialises operations that must be ordered. Second, the resource provider — not ARM — enforces the resource's own rules, so an error can come from either layer and the distinction tells you where to look: RequestDisallowedByPolicy and AuthorizationFailed are ARM, SkuNotAvailable and QuotaExceeded are the provider. Providers must also be registered on a subscription before they can be used, which is a classic first-deployment failure in a freshly vended subscription.

Example

# the deployment record is the audit trail and the debugging tool
az deployment group show -g rg-orders -n main --query properties.provisioningState
az deployment operation group list -g rg-orders -n main \
  --query "[?properties.provisioningState=='Failed'].{res:properties.targetResource.resourceName,
             code:properties.statusMessage.error.code, msg:properties.statusMessage.error.message}"

# freshly vended subscription: providers must be registered
az provider register --namespace Microsoft.ContainerService --wait
az provider list --query "[?registrationState=='NotRegistered'].namespace" -o tsv

Follow-ups

  • Why pin API versions? — Each resource type's behaviour and schema is versioned; using the latest implicitly means a provider update can change your deployment's result. Pin, and upgrade deliberately with a what-if to see the diff.
  • Incremental or complete mode? — Incremental adds and updates, leaving everything else alone; complete deletes resources in the resource group that are not in the template, which is powerful and dangerous. Deployment Stacks are the modern, safer way to get deletion semantics.
  • Why do some deployments partially succeed? — Because operations are dispatched to independent providers and a later one can fail after earlier resources were created. There is no transaction; templates must therefore be idempotent so re-running converges.
  • What is the deployment history limit? — Each resource group keeps a bounded number of deployments (800), and ARM prunes automatically — but a pipeline that deploys with a unique name each run can still hit friction. Use consistent deployment names, or stacks.

Wrong answer: “ARM deployments are transactional — if anything fails, nothing is created.” They are not; a failed deployment routinely leaves half the resources in place. Idempotent templates and re-running are the intended model, and expecting a rollback is how people end up in a half-built environment they hand-repair.

Your pipeline starts getting HTTP 429 from ARM. What is happening and what do you do? senior

Answer. ARM throttles by subscription, by tenant, by principal and by resource provider, using token-bucket-style budgets that refill over time — historically expressed as read and write budgets per hour with a per-instance dimension. The response carries Retry-After and remaining-call headers, and the correct client behaviour is to honour them rather than to retry immediately. The causes are almost always one of: a polling loop querying resource state too aggressively, a deployment pipeline running many parallel operations in one subscription, a monitoring or CMDB tool enumerating every resource on a short interval, or a retry storm amplifying an unrelated failure. Fixes, in order: use Azure Resource Graph instead of enumerating resources through ARM (it exists precisely for this and has its own, far more generous limits), reduce polling frequency and use asynchronous operation URLs instead of polling the resource, batch and serialise deployment operations, spread work across subscriptions where the boundary allows, and cache what does not change. Then instrument it — the throttling headers should be logged so you see the budget being consumed before it runs out.

Example

# the headers tell you how close you are - log them
curl -si -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  "https://management.azure.com/subscriptions/$SUB/resourcegroups?api-version=2021-04-01" \
  | grep -i -E "x-ms-ratelimit|retry-after"
# x-ms-ratelimit-remaining-subscription-reads: 11832

# don't enumerate through ARM - this is what Resource Graph is for
az graph query -q "resources | where type =~ 'microsoft.web/sites' | project id, name, resourceGroup"

Follow-ups

  • Why is Resource Graph the answer? — It is a pre-built index with much higher limits and Kusto querying, so “list every VM in 60 subscriptions” is one fast call rather than thousands of throttled ones. Any tool looping over subscriptions with the management API is doing it wrong.
  • Does the SDK handle 429 for you? — The Azure SDKs retry with backoff, which is right for a transient throttle and wrong for sustained overload — it hides the problem until the pipeline times out. Log throttle events explicitly rather than letting the SDK swallow them.
  • How does this argue for subscription design? — Limits are per subscription, so a single mega-subscription means every team shares one throttling budget and one team's deployment loop stalls everyone's. It is a concrete, non-theoretical argument for subscription-per-workload.
  • What about data-plane throttling? — Entirely separate budgets per service (storage, Key Vault, Cosmos RU). A 429 from Key Vault is a Key Vault limit, not an ARM one — read which service returned it before you start optimising the wrong loop.

Wrong answer: “Open a support ticket to raise the ARM rate limit.” The control-plane budgets are generally not raised on request; the expectation is that you fix the calling pattern. A ticket buys days of delay and no change, while a switch to Resource Graph fixes it the same afternoon.

You cannot create the VMs you need in a region. Diagnose it. senior

Answer. Distinguish three different failures that look identical from the portal. Quota (QuotaExceeded, or an operation blocked by vCPU limits): your subscription's per-region, per-family core quota is exhausted — visible in Usage + quotas, self-service increases for most families, and a support request for large ones. Capacity (SkuNotAvailable, AllocationFailed, ZonalAllocationFailed): Azure does not have that SKU available in that region or zone right now — quota increases will not help. Restriction: the SKU is not offered in that region or to that subscription type at all. The diagnosis is a single command (az vm list-skus with the location filter shows restrictions and zones), and the responses differ: for quota, request more, in advance; for capacity, try another zone, another SKU family, or another region, and for anything business-critical use Capacity Reservations or an On-Demand Capacity Reservation group to hold capacity before you need it; for restriction, choose a different SKU. The lead-level point is that capacity is finite and popular SKUs — GPUs especially — can be unavailable for weeks, so a launch plan that assumes elastic capacity for a specialised SKU is a plan with an unpriced risk.

Example

# is it quota, capacity, or restriction?
az vm list-usage --location westeurope -o table | grep -i "Standard DASv5"   # quota
az vm list-skus --location westeurope --size Standard_D8as_v5 \
  --query "[].{name:name, zones:locationInfo[0].zones, restrictions:restrictions}" -o json
# restrictions: [] and zones: [1,2,3]  -> available; it's quota
# restrictions: [{reasonCode:"NotAvailableForSubscription", ...}] -> capacity/restriction

# hold capacity ahead of a launch
az capacity reservation group create -n crg-launch -g rg-compute --zones 1 2 3
az capacity reservation create -g rg-compute -c crg-launch -n res1 \
  --sku Standard_D8as_v5 --capacity 40 --zone 1

Follow-ups

  • How far ahead should you request quota? — Weeks for anything unusual, and always before a load test — a load test that cannot scale is a wasted week. Make quota a checklist item in launch planning alongside capacity reservations.
  • What does a capacity reservation cost? — You pay for the reserved capacity whether or not you use it, at the equivalent VM rate; it can be combined with reservations for the discount. It converts an availability risk into a known cost, which is exactly the trade a launch needs.
  • Does this affect PaaS? — Yes, indirectly: App Service plans, AKS node pools, Container Apps profiles and databases all sit on the same underlying capacity, and you will see allocation failures on scale-out in a constrained region. The symptom is a scale event that silently does not happen.
  • How does it interact with DR? — A regional failover assumes capacity in the secondary region at the moment everyone else is failing over too. If the RTO is contractual, reserve capacity in the secondary; otherwise state the assumption explicitly in the DR plan.

Wrong answer: “Raise a quota increase request and it will resolve.” It resolves exactly one of the three causes. If the error is AllocationFailed or SkuNotAvailable, more quota changes nothing and you have lost a day waiting for a ticket. Reading the error code first is the whole skill.

Which Azure limits have you designed around, and how do you plan for them? principal

Answer. The ones that actually bite are the per-subscription and per-resource ceilings that are invisible until you are near them. The list worth naming from experience: vCPU quota per family per region (per subscription); private endpoints and NICs per subscription, which a private-by-default estate consumes fast; role assignments per subscription (4,000, which is why you assign to groups); VNet peerings per VNet and address space exhaustion; storage account request rate and bandwidth per account; Cosmos DB's 20 GB logical partition and RU floors; Key Vault transactions per 10 seconds; ARM read/write budgets; Event Hubs partitions fixed at creation on standard; and App Service plan instance counts and the apps-per-plan practical limit. Planning for them means three habits: know which limits are hard (partition counts, logical partition size, address space) versus soft (quota, which is a ticket), because hard limits must be designed around and soft ones must be scheduled; instrument approach to a limit as a metric with an alert, not as an error; and make the subscription the scaling unit so most soft limits are solved by vending another subscription rather than by an architectural change.

Example

LimitHard?Symptom when hitDesign response
Cosmos logical partition 20 GBhardWrites fail for one keyHierarchical partition keys, chosen up front
Event Hubs partitions (standard)hardCannot parallelise consumers furtherSize at creation for 2–3 years of growth
VNet address spacehard-ishCannot peer; cannot grow a subnetCentral IPAM, CNI Overlay, generous spoke blocks
vCPU quota per family/regionsoftScale-out silently stopsRequest ahead of load tests; alert at 80%
Private endpoints per subscriptionsoftDeployment fails in a mature spokeSubscription per workload; monitor consumption
Role assignments per subscriptionsoftCannot grant accessAssign to Entra groups, never individuals
Storage account 20k req/shard-ish503 ServerBusy under loadShard accounts; high-entropy key prefixes

Follow-ups

  • How do you find the limits before they find you? — The service-limits documentation per service at design time, plus a monthly Resource Graph and metrics report of consumption against the limits that matter for your architecture. Alerting at 80% of a limit turns an outage into a ticket.
  • What is the most expensive limit to hit? — Anything set at creation and unchangeable: a Cosmos partition key, Event Hubs partition count on standard, a VNet address range that overlaps something. Those are migrations, not configuration changes, which is why they deserve disproportionate design attention.
  • How do you decide when to shard? — Before you reach 60–70% of a hard ceiling, because the migration takes longer than the remaining headroom lasts. Sharding under duress is how you get an outage and a rushed data migration in the same week.
  • How do you get this knowledge into a team? — Encode it in the shared modules and the design review checklist: the module that creates a Cosmos container asks for a hierarchical key, the one that creates an Event Hub asks for the partition count with a comment about why it cannot change. Documentation decays; defaults do not.

Wrong answer: “The cloud is elastic, so limits aren't really a design concern — you request more when you need it.” Half of the limits that matter cannot be raised at all, and the ones that can require lead time you will not have during a launch. This question exists to find out whether you have operated at a scale where the ceiling is visible.

21Judgment calls

These have no single right answer, and that is the point: the interviewer is listening for which trade-offs you name unprompted, whether you ask what the constraint is before answering, and whether you can hold a position without being dogmatic. Each block below says what a strong answer demonstrates at the highest level. Practise them out loud — the failure mode is being right and unconvincing.

Should a platform team own the network, or should workload teams? principal

Answer. A strong answer starts by splitting the question: the shared, dangerous parts of networking (address space, hub, egress inspection, DNS zones, peering, on-prem connectivity) are naturally central because a mistake there is estate-wide and because address allocation must be coordinated; the parts inside a workload's own boundary (subnets within its allocated block, NSG rules between its own tiers, private endpoints for its own resources) belong to the team, because central ownership of those turns the platform into a ticket queue and slows every delivery. The strongest version names the mechanism that makes the split work rather than the split itself: the platform vends a spoke with an allocated block, pre-linked DNS zones and a UDR to the firewall, and the team owns everything inside it, with policy preventing the specific dangerous acts (public IPs, open management ports, unrestricted egress). It also acknowledges the failure modes of both extremes — central everything produces shadow IT and a two-week lead time for a subnet; federated everything produces overlapping address space and inconsistent egress — and names the metric that would tell you the split is wrong: time from request to a working spoke, and the number of network-related production incidents by origin.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Separating “shared and irreversible” from “local and reversible” as the ownership criterion, rather than arguing from org structure.
  • Naming the automation (vending, policy, pre-linked DNS) that makes decentralisation safe, because without it the debate is unresolvable.
  • Willingness to state a metric that would change your mind — a real one, measurable this quarter.
  • Acknowledging the political dimension honestly: this is usually a question about trust and lead time, not about routing.

Weak answer: Picking a side on principle (“networking is specialist, so central” / “you build it you run it, so federated”) without naming the mechanism or the failure mode of your choice.

Multi-cloud: worth it, or a tax? principal

Answer. The strong answer separates multi-cloud as a fact from multi-cloud as a strategy. Most large organisations are already multi-cloud by acquisition, by SaaS, and by data-science teams choosing tools — so the question is not whether to be multi-cloud but which capabilities to make portable and which to run natively in each cloud. Deliberate active-active multi-cloud for a single workload is expensive and rarely justified: you pay in lowest-common-denominator architecture, duplicated tooling, duplicated expertise, egress between clouds, and a much harder security posture. The cases that do justify it are concrete — a regulator or a customer contract requiring it, a specific capability only one cloud has, negotiating leverage at genuine scale, or an acquisition you are not going to migrate. A strong candidate names what they would standardise across clouds regardless (identity, CI/CD, observability, policy and posture via a single pane such as Defender for Cloud, and container packaging) and what they would deliberately leave native (managed data services, networking, serverless), and then puts a number on the tax: typically a significant fraction of platform-team capacity, ongoing.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Distinguishing portability of skills and process from portability of architecture, and knowing the first is worth far more.
  • Naming a real trigger for the decision — a contract, a regulator, a capability — rather than the abstract fear of lock-in.
  • Quantifying the tax in people and time, since that is the currency the decision is actually paid in.
  • Comfort saying “we are multi-cloud in fact and single-cloud in strategy, and here is how we govern the difference”.

Weak answer: “We should be cloud-agnostic to avoid lock-in” with no scenario, no cost estimate, and no example of an actual migration between clouds.

When is a managed service's SLA good enough, and when do you build more? principal

Answer. A strong answer first points out that the SLA is a credit, not a promise about your workload, and that your real availability is dominated by your own changes. From there it works from the user-facing SLO backwards: compute the composite of the dependency chain, compare it against what you have promised, and see whether the gap is real. If it is, the cheapest closures are usually not more redundancy — they are removing dependencies from the hot path, caching, graceful degradation, and better deployment safety. You build more only when the composite genuinely cannot meet a committed target, and then you build the specific thing that closes the gap (zone redundancy, a second region, a fallback path) rather than a general-purpose redundancy programme. The most senior part of the answer is the willingness to go the other way: to say that a tier-3 system's 99.5% is fine, that adding a second region would cost more than the downtime it prevents, and to have the numbers to defend it.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Arithmetic: a composite SLA computed out loud, and downtime expressed in minutes per month rather than nines.
  • Knowing that self-inflicted outages dominate, so deployment safety usually buys more availability per euro than redundancy.
  • Naming graceful degradation as a first-class option — the feature that fails softly rather than the dependency that never fails.
  • Being able to argue for less availability with evidence, which is rarer and more valuable than arguing for more.

Weak answer: Treating the SLA as a design guarantee, or reflexively adding a second region without computing what it buys or what it costs to keep tested.

Kubernetes or PaaS for a company standardising now? principal

Answer. A strong answer reframes it as a question about the operating model rather than the technology. Kubernetes is a platform-building toolkit: it pays off when you have enough services and enough platform capacity to amortise the upgrade treadmill, the add-on estate and the on-call, and when you need extensibility (operators, mesh, GPU scheduling, portability). PaaS — App Service, Functions, Container Apps — trades extensibility for a much smaller operational surface, and for most organisations under a certain size it is simply cheaper in the currency that matters, which is engineer-hours. The candidate should name the crossover honestly (roughly: do you have a platform team that can own a cluster's lifecycle indefinitely, and do you have a requirement PaaS cannot meet?), note that AKS Automatic and Container Apps have narrowed the gap from both directions, and — critically — point out that the container image makes the decision reversible: standardising on containers and OCI packaging keeps both doors open, which is a much better hedge than choosing Kubernetes for optionality. Then they should commit to a recommendation, because “it depends” without a default is not an answer.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Pricing the operational tail — upgrades, CVEs, add-ons, on-call — rather than comparing feature lists.
  • Knowing the current state: AKS Automatic and Karpenter-based node auto-provisioning reduce the ops burden, and Container Apps covers most microservice needs without a cluster.
  • Identifying the reversible part (the image) and the irreversible part (the platform investment and the skills).
  • Making an actual recommendation with the condition that would change it.

Weak answer: “Kubernetes, because it's the industry standard and gives us the most flexibility” — flexibility nobody has costed, standard nobody needs to conform to.

How much governance is too much? principal

Answer. The strong answer proposes a test rather than a level: every control should map to an incident class it prevents, and its friction should be smaller than the expected cost of that incident. Controls that fail the test — a mandatory tag nobody reads, an approval nobody has ever rejected, a review board that rubber-stamps — are pure tax and should be removed, and being willing to remove a control is the rarest signal in this conversation. The second half is that the friction of a control is largely a function of how it is implemented: a deny policy with a fast, documented, time-boxed exemption path is far cheaper than the same rule enforced by a human queue, and a guardrail baked into a shared module costs nothing at all. So the honest answer to “too much” is usually “the wrong implementation”, and the diagnostic is measurable: lead time from idea to production, the number of standing exemptions, and the amount of shadow IT. If teams are routing around governance, the governance has already failed regardless of its compliance score.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • A stated test for whether a control earns its keep, applied to a specific control the candidate has removed or would remove.
  • Understanding that automation converts a control from friction into a default — the main lever available.
  • Naming shadow IT and standing exemptions as the failure signals, not just the compliance percentage.
  • Treating the exception process as a designed product with an SLA, not as an escalation.

Weak answer: Answering only in one direction — either “you can never have too much security” or “governance slows teams down” — without a criterion for deciding case by case.

Do you let teams choose their own Azure services, or standardise? principal

Answer. The strong answer distinguishes a supported set from a permitted set. The supported set is small: services the platform team has modules, monitoring, policy, runbooks and expertise for — using them is fast and comes with support. Anything else is permitted but self-supported: the team owns the operations, the security review and the on-call, and must document why the supported option did not fit. That structure preserves autonomy while making the cost of divergence visible and borne by the diverging team, which is the only sustainable arrangement. The candidate should also describe how the supported set evolves — a team's justified divergence today becomes a supported module next quarter if others need it — so the standard is a living catalogue rather than a frozen list. The sophistication signal is recognising that the real cost of sprawl is not licence fees but the long tail: fourteen ways to do messaging means fourteen sets of failure modes for the on-call engineer at 03:00, and nobody who chose the fourteenth is on that rota.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Supported versus permitted as the mechanism, with the cost of divergence landing on the diverging team.
  • A path for the catalogue to grow, so standardisation does not ossify and teams do not have to fight to innovate.
  • Naming the operational tail — on-call cognitive load, incident response, hiring — as the true cost of sprawl.
  • Comfort with a small number of deliberate exceptions rather than an absolute rule in either direction.

Weak answer: A blanket “teams choose, we're not the police” (which leaves the platform team supporting everything) or a rigid approved-list with no path to change it (which produces workarounds within a quarter).

Is a second region worth it for this workload? principal

Answer. A strong answer refuses to answer without three numbers: the cost of an hour of downtime, the probability and duration of the failure modes a second region actually addresses, and the all-in cost of the second region — which is not just infrastructure but pipeline changes, data replication, drills, and the permanent tax on every subsequent design decision. It then points out that a second region addresses only regional failures, which are rare, while doing nothing about the far more common causes: bad deployments, expired certificates, a bad configuration change, a dependency failure, and a data corruption that replicates faithfully to both regions. So the ranking is usually zone redundancy first (cheap, addresses a real and more frequent failure), then deployment safety and backups, then a second region — and if the second region is justified, it must be a tested second region with regular failover drills, because an untested standby often makes an incident worse. The senior move is offering the intermediate options: a cold pilot-light region with IaC and data replication, or a documented rebuild-from-code plan with a measured RTO, both of which cost a fraction of warm standby.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Asking for the cost of downtime before proposing anything, and being willing to conclude “no”.
  • Knowing that replication propagates logical corruption, so DR and backup are different problems that need different controls.
  • Offering a spectrum — zone redundancy, backup/restore, pilot light, warm standby, active-active — rather than a binary.
  • Insisting that whatever is built is drilled, with a measured RTO rather than a documented one.

Weak answer: Jumping to an architecture (“active-active with Front Door and Cosmos multi-region writes”) before establishing what failure is being defended against and what it is worth.

A team wants to run a database on VMs instead of using a managed service. What do you say? principal

Answer. A strong answer asks for the blocker before arguing. If there is a real one — an unsupported extension, a version requirement, a licensing constraint, a performance characteristic they have measured — then the conversation is about how to do it well: HA design, backup and restore testing, patching, monitoring, capacity, and who is on call at 03:00 when it fails over. If the blocker is preference or unfamiliarity, the counter-argument should be specific rather than dogmatic: name what the managed service does that they are about to build (automated backups with point-in-time restore, patching, failover, encryption, threat detection, monitoring) and price the ongoing engineering cost of replicating it, which for a stateful system is at least a fraction of an engineer indefinitely. Then propose a decision with a trigger: run managed now, revisit if the specific limitation appears, with the criterion written down. The mature version also concedes that self-hosting is occasionally right and says under what conditions — which is what makes the recommendation credible rather than a policy.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Curiosity before advocacy: eliciting the actual blocker rather than assuming it is ignorance.
  • Making the hidden work explicit — backup restore testing, patching, failover drills, on-call — as costs, not as criticism.
  • Proposing a reversible decision with a written trigger, rather than winning an argument.
  • Genuine willingness to accept self-hosting when the reason holds, which is what makes the default recommendation trusted.

Weak answer: “Managed services are always better, use Azure SQL” — an assertion that ends the conversation, teaches nothing, and will be ignored the moment you leave the room.

How do you handle a service that is over budget but business-critical? principal

Answer. A strong answer separates “expensive” from “wasteful”. First establish the unit economics: cost per transaction, per tenant, per user — because a service whose absolute cost is rising while its unit cost is falling is succeeding, and the budget is the thing that is wrong. If the unit cost is bad, work the ranked list from the cost unit: waste first (idle, over-provisioning, log volume, orphans, wrong tiers), then commitments, then architecture. If the unit cost is defensible and the total is still unaffordable, the conversation moves to the business: which features or capabilities would we stop paying for, what service level would we reduce, or what would we charge. The essential move at principal level is refusing to let the choice be made silently — reliability, retention, environments and roadmap are all purchasable with the money, and someone with the authority to make that trade should make it explicitly, with the consequences written down. It is also the moment to check the incentive structure: if no team sees its own cost, the overspend is systemic and the fix is attribution, not a one-off cut.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • Reaching for unit economics rather than absolute spend, and knowing which one the budget conversation should be about.
  • A ranked plan with risk attached to each step, so the safe savings are separated from the architectural ones.
  • Escalating the trade-off explicitly rather than quietly degrading reliability to hit a number.
  • Diagnosing the incentive problem — invisible cost — as the systemic cause rather than blaming a team.

Weak answer: Cutting the most visible line (usually non-production, or DR) to hit the number, without naming what risk was just accepted or who accepted it.

When do you say no to a “quick” production change? principal

Answer. The strong answer avoids both extremes — the process absolutist and the pushover — by making the criterion the reversibility and blast radius of the change rather than its size. A change that is reversible in seconds, observable immediately, and limited to one service can go fast with a lightweight path. A change that is irreversible (a delete, a schema contract, a key rotation, a policy at management-group scope), or whose blast radius crosses teams, does not go fast regardless of how small it looks or how urgent it feels — and the way to say no without being obstructive is to offer the fastest safe alternative and a realistic time for it, immediately. The candidate should also name the exception: during a live incident, the calculus changes and the on-call has authority to act, with the review happening afterwards. The deepest version notes that repeated pressure for quick changes is a symptom — the normal path is too slow — and that the durable fix is making the safe path fast, not defending the slow one.

What a strong answer demonstrates

  • A concrete criterion (reversibility × blast radius) instead of “it depends on the situation”.
  • Always pairing the no with an alternative and a time, so it reads as engineering rather than gatekeeping.
  • Distinguishing incident-time authority from normal-time process, and requiring the retrospective either way.
  • Treating recurring pressure as feedback on the delivery pipeline rather than as a discipline problem.

Weak answer: “All changes go through the process, no exceptions” — which is how the process gets bypassed entirely during the first real incident, permanently.

22Design exercises

Three full exercises where Azure knowledge meets system design. Read the prompt, ask the clarifying questions out loud, sketch for fifteen minutes, and only then open the solution. The graded part is rarely the diagram — it is which questions you asked first and which trade-offs you named before being asked.

Exercise 1 — Multi-tenant SaaS on Azure

Prompt. Design the Azure architecture for a B2B SaaS product: 800 tenants today growing to 5,000, ranging from ten-user startups to a 40,000-user enterprise that is 30% of revenue. Web app plus REST API, moderate write volume, heavy read, some tenants require data residency in the EU and one requires customer-managed keys. Target 99.95% availability, and the enterprise tenant has a contractual 15-minute RTO.

Clarifying questions to ask. What is the isolation requirement per tenant tier — logical, or contractual physical separation? What is the noisy-neighbour tolerance, and is the enterprise tenant allowed to degrade the others? Which regions must data stay in, and does that include backups, logs and telemetry? What is the read/write ratio and the size of the largest tenant's data? Is per-tenant restore a requirement (“restore tenant X to yesterday”)? What is the deployment cadence, and can tenants be on different versions? Who owns onboarding — self-service or sales-led?

Solution outline and the trade-offs you are expected to name principal

Shape. A pooled tier for the long tail and a dedicated tier for the few tenants whose contracts require it — the standard, defensible answer, because pure pooled fails the CMK and residency requirements and pure siloed fails the economics at 5,000 tenants.

Edge        Front Door Premium + WAF, custom domains per tenant (or subdomain), Private Link origins
App         App Service Premium v4 (or Container Apps), zone-redundant, min 3 instances
            stateless; tenant resolved from the JWT tid/tenant claim at the edge of the request
Data        POOLED:    Azure SQL elastic pool, database-per-tenant  (best isolation/cost balance:
                       per-tenant restore, per-tenant move, noisy-neighbour bounded by the pool)
            DEDICATED: own SQL database (own pool or single), own storage account, own CMK
            Router:    a small "tenant catalogue" DB mapping tenantId -> region, tier, connection
Blobs       pooled: shared account, container per tenant; dedicated: account per tenant + CMK
Cache       Azure Managed Redis, keys namespaced by tenantId (never a shared key space)
Async       Service Bus with sessionId = tenantId for per-tenant ordering
Residency   deployment per region (weu, and a second EU region); the catalogue routes; NO
            cross-region fallback for residency tenants, and logs/backups stay in-region too
DR          pooled: zone-redundant + geo-replica, RTO ~1h. Enterprise tenant: failover group
            + warm secondary + quarterly drill, to meet the contractual 15 minutes.
Onboarding  vending pipeline: catalogue row -> database (or dedicated stack) -> DNS -> done

Trade-offs to name unprompted.

  • Database-per-tenant in an elastic pool is the crux: it buys per-tenant restore, per-tenant migration between pools or regions, and bounded noisy-neighbour, at the cost of schema-migration fan-out across thousands of databases (which needs an orchestrated, resumable migration runner) and a per-database floor cost. Say why you rejected the alternatives: a single shared schema with a tenant column is cheapest and makes per-tenant restore and residency nearly impossible; a full stack per tenant is clean and does not survive 5,000 tenants operationally.
  • The 30%-of-revenue tenant should be on the dedicated tier for both the RTO and the noisy-neighbour reason — and you should say that the pooled tier's availability target is not the enterprise tenant's, because a single number for both is what forces the whole estate to the highest cost.
  • CMK means the tenant can take their own service down; design the disabled-key state explicitly, alert both sides, and put it in the contract.
  • Residency is not just the database: backups, logs, telemetry, search indexes and support access all need to respect it, and that is where these designs actually fail an audit.
  • Tenant isolation must be tested, not asserted: a CI suite that replays tenant A's requests with tenant B's token and asserts 403 on every route.
  • Version skew: decide early whether all tenants are on one version (simpler, forces coordinated migrations) or whether dedicated tenants can lag (flexible, doubles the support matrix). Most successful products choose one version and hold the line.

Exercise 2 — Real-time event pipeline

Prompt. A logistics company has 200,000 vehicles reporting position and telemetry every 10 seconds. Build the Azure pipeline: live map for dispatchers (sub-2-second freshness), geofence alerts within 5 seconds, 90 days of queryable history for operations, and multi-year retention for compliance and analytics. It must not lose data during a consumer outage.

Clarifying questions to ask. How large is a message, and is it batched by the device? Is the alerting exactly-once critical, or is a duplicate alert acceptable? How many concurrent dispatchers, and does the map need every vehicle or only those in view? What is the acceptable data loss during a regional outage? Are devices trusted, and how are they provisioned and credentialed? Do queries over the 90 days need to be interactive, or are minutes acceptable? What is the budget envelope?

Solution outline and the trade-offs you are expected to name principal

Volume first, out loud. 200,000 vehicles ÷ 10 s = 20,000 events/s; at ~400 bytes that is ~8 MB/s ingress — comfortably within Event Hubs Premium, and the sizing conversation is about partitions and consumers rather than about raw throughput.

Ingress     IoT Hub (per-device identity, provisioning, C2D commands) -> routes to Event Hubs
            OR Event Hubs directly if devices are a trusted fleet with a shared credential.
            Event Hubs Premium, 32 partitions, partitionKey = vehicleId (ordering per vehicle),
            retention 7 days = the replay window if a consumer breaks.

Hot path    Stream Analytics (or Flink) job:
              - geofence evaluation with a reference-data join on zones -> Service Bus -> alerting
              - 5-second tumbling window -> latest position per vehicle -> Azure Managed Redis
            Dispatcher UI: Web PubSub / SignalR subscribing to viewport-filtered updates from
            Redis, NOT all 200k vehicles per client.

Warm path   Functions (batched Event Hubs trigger) -> Cosmos DB, pk = vehicleId,
            TTL 90 days, so expiry is automatic and free. Interactive per-vehicle history.

Cold path   Event Hubs Capture -> ADLS Gen2, Avro/Parquet, partitioned by date/hour.
            Fabric or Databricks for analytics; lifecycle policy to cool/archive.

Reliability Consumers checkpoint per partition; lag is the primary SLI with an alert well
            before it approaches retention. Zone-redundant everything; second region only if
            the business prices the outage - state that as an open question, not an assumption.

Trade-offs to name unprompted.

  • Three paths, one ingest. The single most important structural decision is that the hot, warm and cold consumers read the same stream independently via consumer groups, so a slow analytics job cannot delay the dispatcher map. Also name the cost: each consumer group re-reads the full stream, so egress multiplies.
  • Retention is the safety net. “Must not lose data during a consumer outage” is answered by Event Hubs retention plus checkpointing, not by a queue — say the number (7 days is the window in which you must fix a broken consumer) and alert on lag long before it.
  • The UI is the scaling trap. Pushing 20,000 updates/s to every dispatcher browser is the naive design; viewport filtering and server-side aggregation are what make it work, and this is often what the interviewer is really probing.
  • Cosmos TTL versus a time-series store. TTL gives free 90-day expiry and cheap per-vehicle reads; it is poor at “all vehicles in this area last Tuesday”, which belongs in the cold path or a purpose-built store. Name the query you are choosing not to serve.
  • Duplicate alerts. At-least-once delivery means a geofence alert can fire twice; deduplicate on (vehicleId, zoneId, entry timestamp bucket) rather than promising exactly-once.
  • Partition count is fixed at creation on the standard tier — size it for years of growth, and say that you did.
  • Device identity. If devices are physically accessible, a shared key is a fleet-wide compromise; IoT Hub with per-device credentials and revocation is the reason to pay for it.

Exercise 3 — Landing zone for a regulated enterprise

Prompt. A financial-services company with 3,000 engineers is moving to Azure. Design the landing zone: tenant and subscription topology, network, identity, governance, and how a team gets a production environment. Regulators require data residency in two EU countries, full audit of privileged access, and demonstrable segregation between the payments estate and everything else.

Clarifying questions to ask. One tenant or several, and is there an existing Entra tenant with M365? What does “segregation” mean contractually — network, identity, subscription, or separate tenant? Is there an existing on-premises estate and ExpressRoute? What is the target for time-from-request to a working environment? Who holds the exception authority — architecture, security, or the business? Is there an existing IaC skill base (Bicep or Terraform)? What is the audit evidence cadence, and who is the auditor?

Solution outline and the trade-offs you are expected to name principal 
Tenant      ONE Entra tenant (identity boundary; multiple tenants multiply CA policy sets,
            B2B and licensing). Segregation achieved below the tenant, unless the regulator
            explicitly requires a separate directory - ask, do not assume.

MG tree     root
             +- platform        (identity | management | connectivity)
             +- landingzones
             |    +- corp       private only, no public ingress
             |    +- online     public ingress behind WAF
             |    +- payments   SEGREGATED: own policy set, own connectivity, own
             |                  approvers, no peering to corp, own log workspace,
             |                  own PIM approvers and access reviews
             +- sandbox         budget-capped, auto-expiring, no data
             +- decommissioned  deny all writes

Network     Virtual WAN (3,000 engineers, many spokes, two countries) OR classic hub-spoke
            per region - decide on routing complexity, not fashion. Central Azure Firewall
            Premium, egress deny-by-default with FQDN allowlists, private endpoints for all
            PaaS, private DNS zones centralised and auto-linked at vending.
            Payments: its own hub, its own firewall, no transit to corp. Segregation is
            structural, not a rule.

Identity    Entra ID with phishing-resistant credentials for privileged roles; PIM eligible
            for all Owner/UAA; no standing subscription-scope assignments; break-glass x2
            excluded from CA and alerted on. Workload identity federation for every pipeline.
            Payments has separate privileged groups and separate approvers.

Governance  ALZ policy baseline via Bicep AVM (GA Jan 2026) deployed through Deployment
            Stacks with denySettings on platform resources. Audit-first rollout, Deny in
            tranches. Regulatory compliance initiatives assigned per MG. Residency enforced
            by an allowed-locations policy per MG, including for backups and logs.

Vending     PR to the platform repo -> pipeline creates subscription, places it, applies
            budget + policy + tags, peers network, links DNS, creates rg + RBAC group,
            opens a starter PR on the team repo. SLO: 4 business hours. Payments vending
            requires a second approver from the payments security function.

Trade-offs to name unprompted.

  • One tenant versus several. Argue for one and say what would change your mind — an explicit regulatory directive, or an entity that must survive divestment. Multiple tenants are frequently proposed for “segregation” and are the most expensive way to achieve it.
  • Segregation is structural. Payments gets its own management group, its own connectivity, its own approvers and its own log workspace, because a policy exception is easier to grant than a missing peering is to create. That is the property an auditor can verify in five minutes.
  • Virtual WAN versus classic hub-spoke. Name the criterion — number of hubs, branches and routing complexity versus the control you give up — rather than picking one. At 3,000 engineers and two countries it is genuinely arguable.
  • Audit-first policy rollout. Deny at the root on day one breaks every existing deployment and ends the programme politically. Say this before the interviewer asks how you avoid it.
  • Vending is the deliverable. Without a four-hour path to a compliant environment, 3,000 engineers will build shadow estates, and the landing zone becomes a document. The SLO on the platform is as important as any control in it.
  • Evidence as a by-product. Policy compliance exports, PIM history and pipeline approvals are the audit evidence; if compliance work is separate from engineering work it will be true once a year.
  • Bicep AVM versus Terraform. Decide on the existing skill base and say so; both are supported by AVM, and the classic ALZ-Bicep repo was archived in February 2026, so “we'll use ALZ-Bicep” is a migration statement rather than a plan.

23Night-before cheat sheet

The ten questions most likely to be asked, with a one-sentence answer each. If you can say these cleanly and then go one level deeper when probed, you will clear the screening layer of almost any Azure loop.

  1. What is a managed identity? — A service principal whose credential the platform creates, stores and rotates, retrieved by your code from a local token endpoint, so no secret exists anywhere for anyone to leak.
  2. How does Azure RBAC work? — Principal + role definition + scope, inherited downward and strictly additive: there is no deny in role assignments, only deny assignments (from stacks and managed apps) and Azure Policy's deny effect.
  3. App Service, Functions, Container Apps, AKS or VMs? — Choose by the hardest constraint and by how much Kubernetes your organisation can afford to operate; Container Apps is the default for containers without a cluster, AKS only with a platform team.
  4. What is the difference between a private endpoint and VNet integration? — VNet integration is outbound (your app reaching private things); a private endpoint is inbound (a private IP for a service so it can refuse public access) — a fully private app needs both.
  5. Availability set or availability zone? — Sets spread across racks inside one zone and do not survive a zone failure; zones are the modern answer, with VMSS Flex spread across three of them.
  6. How do you choose a Cosmos DB partition key? — High cardinality, even access distribution, alignment with the most frequent query, and every logical partition under 20 GB — use hierarchical keys when one field cannot satisfy all four.
  7. Service Bus, Event Hubs or Event Grid? — Messages that matter individually and need retry, ordering and dead-lettering go to Service Bus; high-volume ordered streams read by offset go to Event Hubs; discrete notifications pushed to HTTP subscribers go to Event Grid.
  8. How should a pipeline authenticate to Azure? — Workload identity federation with a federated credential scoped to the exact repository and environment, granting RBAC only on the resource groups it deploys into — never a stored client secret.
  9. Bicep or Terraform? — Bicep for Azure-only estates (no state file, day-one feature support, Deployment Stacks now give deletion semantics); Terraform for multi-provider work or an existing skill base — both are covered by Azure Verified Modules.
  10. How do you compute a composite SLA? — Multiply the SLAs of everything in series (Front Door × App Service × SQL × Key Vault ≈ 99.92%, about 35 minutes a month) and remember the SLA is a service credit, not a prediction of your uptime.

One last currency check before you walk in: mandatory Entra MFA Phase 2 for ARM writes completed in 2026, Basic Load Balancer and Basic public IPs (and default outbound access for new VNets) retired 30 September 2025, Application Gateway v1 retired 28 April 2026, Front Door classic retires 31 March 2027, Bicep AVM landing zones went GA in January 2026 with Deployment Stacks, and Karpenter-based AKS node auto-provisioning is GA.
